The Committee, in gathering facts for this report, has consulted prominent Judges, Lawyers and Doctors in the Pittsburgh area and elsewhere. Insofar as it has been possible to do so, we have devoted equal time to the problems, interests and views of plaintiffs and defendants, Judges and Physicians. We are grateful for the helpful advice and suggestions which have been given to us by all. 1 Valuable information was given us by Justices Bernard Botein and Aron Steuer, of the Appellate Division, First Department, Supreme Court of New York - who traveled to Pittsburgh on separate occasions to talk regarding the operation of the New York Plan - perhaps the most thoroughly tested plan in existence. 

Judge Francis L. Van Dusen of the U.S. District Court, Eastern 

District of Pennsylvania, also visited Pittsburgh to speak regarding operation of the Plan in that court, and has given us the benefit of reference to his extensive bibliography on Impartial Medical Testimony. 

We wish to thank specially Federal Judges Michael L. Igoe, Joseph Sam Perry, Julius H. Miner and Edwin A. Robson, who interrupted their busy schedules in order to give us their personal views based upon eighteen months' experience in use of Impartial Medical Testimony in the U. S. District Court for the Northern District of Illinois. 

The first edition of this report, published in March 1961, was distributed to members of the bench in the United States District Court for the Western District of Pennsylvania and the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County. These Judges have indicated that the report was of material assistance to them in reaching decisions to enact rules adopting Impartial Medical Testimony Plans in their respective courts, The Committee sincerely hopes that the report may be of future benefit in other jurisdictions, where use of Impartial Medical Testimony is under consideration. 

Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania

October 1, 1961

NEW YORK PLAN (Special Rule of Appellate Division, Supreme Court, 

First Department, effective December 1, 1952) 1 1. There is established in the Supreme Court for the County of New York an office to be known as the Medical Report Office, which shall be in charge of a deputy clerk of the Supreme Court. 1 1 2. In any personal injury case in which, prior to the trial thereof, a justice shall be of the opinion that an examination of the injured person and a report thereon by an impartial medical expert would be of material aid to the just determination of the case he may, after consultation with counsel for the respective parties, order such examination and report, without cost to the parties, through the Medical Report Office of the Supreme Court, New York County. The examination will be made by a member of a panel of examining physicians designated for their particular qualifications by the New York 

Academy of Medicine and New York County Medical Society. Copies of the report of the examining physician will be made available by the clerk of the Medical Report Office to all parties. 1 3. If the case proceeds to trial after such examination and report, either party may call the examining physician as a witness or the trial justice may, if he deems it desirable to do so, call the examining physician as a witness for the court, subject to questioning by any party, but without cost to any party. - O O- Funds for the operation of this plan first were provided by a grant by the Sloan Foundation and the Ford Fund, but later permanent financing was arranged by inclusion of the plan in the regular court budget. 

The plan was extended in 1953 to the Bronx County Supreme Court, with cooperation of the Bronx Medical Society.

See the following page for discussion of the organizing and operation of this plan. - 2 -

-- !

V2.:.:.:.....: 

THE ORGANIZATION AND OPERATION OF THE NEW YORK PLAN 

The Justices of the Supreme Court of New York County indicated the areas of medical specialty in which expert testimony was needed, and the number of doctors they felt were required in each field. The New York 

Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society cooperated to compile a list of highly qualified doctors in each specialty, composed panels, and persuaded the ones selected to serve on them. 

Those selected were top authorities in their branches of medicine, and men who had not been identified particularly with the plaintiff's or defendant's side of personal injury litigation. The panels are subject to cor.stant supervision and review by the societies. The names of panel members are kept confidential, and the Medical Report Office assigns the experts to the cases in a rotating order.

The Justice handling each injury case during pre-trial decides if examination by an impartial expert is needed. Counsel for either party may request it, but this is not required. In his order for the examination, the Justice indicates the medical specialty involved, and arranges for medical and X-ray reports relating to the injured person to be delivered to the expert. All of the details in the operation of the plan, such as names of parties to be examined and the doctors who will see them, dates, places, next available expert in each specialty, et cetera, are kept strictly within the custody of the Clerk in the Medical Report Office. 

The report of the Impartial Expert's examination in each case is sent in triplicate, with his bill, to the Court, and the Judge and each party receive it prior to the next pre-trial discussion. 

If settlement is not agreed upon, the case is listed for trial. The Impartial Expert may be called to testify by either party or by the Judge. His status as an impartial, court-appointed expert is made known to the jury. 

He may be cross-examined, even by the party who called him. 

The Impartial Expert is requested to make the same charge for his services as he would to a private patient in moderate circumstances. He is paid from the funds of the Court, and an annual appropriation of $15,000 is made for this purpose. - 3 -

THE PHILADELPHIA PLAN 

(Rule 22 of the U. S.

District Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania) Effective June 2, 1958 The Judges of the U.S. District Court asked the Medical Society of the 

State of Pennsylvania to contact the most highly qualified experts in each of several medical specialties and persuade them to serve on panels, from which doctors would be drawn at the request of the court, to make impartial examinations of individuals involved in personal injury lawsuits. 

The Medical Society asked 170 experts to participate in this plan, and 

169 of them agreed to do so.

Prior to the trial of a case, when the Judge feels that examination by an Impartial Expert would aid in a just determination, he may, after consultation with counsel for the parties and after giving counsel a hearing, order such examination and report.

A Deputy Clerk of the District Court is in charge of the lists of panel members, which are kept confidential and advanced in rotation for assignment to the cases. If there is objection to the assignment of the expert next on the panel list, the doctor following him may be offered as an alternate. If counsel will not agree on the second expert, the Judge then will designate one of the two 1 

The Impartial Expert is given all of the X-rays, reports, et cetera, which have been made by doctors for the plaintiff and/or defendant, and he is authorized to have such special tests, examinations or reports as he considers necessary made to aid in his study of the case. His report is sent to the Court, with copies for counsel for each party. The Impartial Expert may be asked to discuss his findings with the doctors employed by each side, in an effort to have all the doctors agree as far as possible. 

If the case reaches trial, the Impartial Expert will be called as the Court's witness, and he may be cross-examined by either side. 

The Impartial Expert is expected to charge his regular reasonable fee, which is approved by the Court and charged to the parties equally. In special circumstances, the Judge may direct other division of this expense. - 4-

THE CHICAGO PLAN 

(Rule 20 of the U. S. District Court for the Northern District of Illinois) 

Effective November 3, 1959

The Illinois State Medical Society and the Chicago Medical Society, having studied the problem of conflicting medical testimony occurring in the trial of personal injury cases, offered to the courts a plan to aid in the just determination of such cases through presentation of Impartial, Expert Medical 

Opinion. 

The Societies made a careful investigation into the qualifications of the specialists proposed to be used in this plan and arrived at a list of 146 doctors conceded to be the best in 21 medical specialties. 

The Judges of the U. S. District Court adopted a plan to use these experts effective November 2, 1959. There is a proposed amendment to the rules which would enable the Superior and Circuit Courts of Illinois also to use them. This is pending now before the Supreme Court of Illinois. 

In the U. S. District Court, a Judge may on his own motion, or on motion of either party, after affording opportunity for a hearing on the question, order that the injured party be examined by an Impartial Expert. 

The names of the specialists on each panel are kept confidential, and in the custody of the Executive Administrator of the Illinois State Medical Society, who is to be appointed as a Deputy Clerk of the District Court. The experts are assigned to serve in rotation. If counsel for either party objects to assignment of the first expert on the list, the next doctor in regular order is offered as an alternate. If agreement still does not result, the Judge will designate one of these two doctors to make the examination and report. 1 The Judge's order provides that medical reports in possession of the parties be given to the Clerk of Court, who transmits them to the Impartial Expert for study in advance of his examination. Either side may have counsel and/or doctors present at the impartial examination, if advance notice of this intention is given to the other side. The Impartial Expert may have such special examinations and te made as he considers necessary to a thorough diagnosis and if he requires the aid of a Radiologist or Pathologist, that need is filled by the next available specialist on the appropriate panel. 1 

The report of the Impartial Expert is returned in triplicate, via the Clerk of Court to the Judge who distributes copies to counsel for each party. 

If the case is not settled during pre-trial, the Impartial Expert may be called to testify at trial, and both sides as well as the Judge may examine and cross-examine him. The jury is informed that the doctor is a court-appointed, impartial expert.

The Impartial Expert is expected to charge his usual fee to a patient in moderate circumstances for this service. The Judge decides the manner in which payment is to be made by either or both parties - and the expert does not know who is paying him until after the case has been concluded. - 5-

fr fr

THE BALTIMORE PLAN 

(Discovery Rule 5-1 of the General Rules of Practice and Procedure of the Superior Court of Baltimore City)

This plan was devised in 1954 by a joint committee of the Maryland and Baltimore City Bar Associations, and the State Medical Society (known as the 

Medical and Chirurgical Faculty of Maryland).

The Medical Society appointed eighteen panels of doctors whom their own profession regarded to be eminent practitioners in the various specialties. The lawyers merely asked that the physicians selected be those whom the doctors themselves would consult; and whose judgment and standing were such as to place them above suspicion of favoritism or incompetence. Ultimately panels in twenty-one specialties were appointed, each consisting of three doctors whose names, while not secret, were not published, Appointment of a neu'ral expert is intended to be confined to extraordinary cases, in which there is wide divergence between medical reports or other special circumstances. The request must originate with one side or the other and the lawyer making it must give advance notice of his intention to do so to his opponent. 

A simple petition is then prepared by the requesting lawyer, and if it is approved, a simple order is made by the Judge. 

If in the opinion of the Pre-Trial Judge appointment of an impartial witness is desirable, he telephones to the Secretary of the Medical Society and asks the names of the doctors on the panel for the appropriate specialty. These are then made known to counsel for the parties. If the lawyers object to any doctor named, the Judge may, in his discretion, eliminate said doctor and appoint one of the others, The Judge then communicates with the selected doctor, whose willingness to serve has been determined at the time of his appointment to the panel; and arrangements are made for examination of the injured person at the doctor's office. 

The doctor sends his report in triplicate to the Judge, who in turn gives a copy to each side.

Ordinarily, the party requesting the impartial witness guarantees payment for his services; but this, too, is left to the discretion of the Court. The selected doctor is not intended to know who is paying the bill. 

If the case cannot be settled on the basis the report of the impartial examination, the neutral expert may be called as a witness at trial. His status as an impartial witness appointed by the Court is made known to the jury. The parties may cross-examine him, and may also produce their own medical witnesses. 

The plan is weakened by the fact that appointment of an impartial witness is dependent upon petition by one of the parties; and further by the fact that even if such petition is made, use of the plan is still left to the discretion of the Judge. Some of the judges have never used it. Many of the attorneys are opposed to it. 

The Honorable Emory Niles, Chief Judge, Supreme Bench of 

Baltimore, speaking before the Judicial Administration 

Section, American Bar Association, at Dallas, Texas, in 

August, 1956, said, "I cannot, in addressing you here, pretend that in Baltimore we have an established and comprehensive Plan for Impartial Medical Testimony in full operation." - 6 - 1

-- li 



THE UTAH PLAN (Sec. 35-2-57 of the Occupational Disease Law and Sec. 35-1-77 of the Workmen's Compensation Law of the State of Utah) 

The Utah Legislature in 1949 amended the Occupational Disease Law to provide a panel of qualified physicians to resolve medical issues in all occupational disease cases, especially silicosis. In 1955 the 

Legislature amended the Workmen's Compensation Act to provide that a panel would resolve all disputed medical issues, . .

The Utah State Medical Association submits to the Industrial Commission of Utah a list of doctors qualified in the various specialties. 

The Commission selects one of these to serve indefinitely as Chairman of each specialty panel, with two remaining positions on that panel to be rotated among the doctors of that specialty who were listed by the Medical Association. The Industrial Commission pays for the services of the panel members, and the cost of any laboratory, autopsy, or other special services. The panel chairman prepares a digest of the findings in each case, which is studied and discussed with the other two members, after which their conclusions are formally written and mailed to the interested parties. 1 

Money for this plan is obtained by the Commission from deposits by employers of persons who are killed in their work but have no dependents. 

The fund created by such deposits prior to adoption of the plan was so large that disbursements to date have not materially reduced it - although the Commission has in unusual cases engaged experts outside the state and even outside the United States.

The Commission makes clear to the parties that it is inclined to accept the findings of the panel. However, hearings may be held in cases where the parties do not wish to accept a determination based upon these findings. The usual rights of appeal are preserved to the parties. 

This plan is confined to occupational disease and workmen's compensation cases. - 7 -

THE CLEVELAND PLAN 

(Rule 21A of the Court of Common Pleas, Cuyahoga County, Ohio) 

Pre-Trial Medical Panel: 

The Pre-Trial Judge in any personal injury case, after conferring with counsel for the parties, may conclude that examination of the injured person by a panel of medical experts would be of material aid to a just determination of the case and he may order an examination and report, without cost to the parties.

The parties shall stipulate in writing (1) that if the case is tried, neither side will make reference to the fact that a medical panel plan was used during pre-trial, or to the fact that any medical witness at the trial had previously served as such a panel member; and (2) if stipulation (1) is breached, the Judge shall declare a mistrial.

The Academy of Medicine shall then designate three experts as a panel to conduct the examination and make a written report. If counsel for the parties advise the Judge that examination by all three panel members is not practicable, the Judge may direct one member to perform the examination and make written report, for review by the remaining two, who shall indicate their approval or disapproval "in whole or in part. If either of the reviewers disagrees with the examiner, he shall submit a written report giving his reasons. 

All the reports shall be referred via the Academy of Medicine to the 

Pre-Trial Judge, with copies for counsel.

If the case goes to trial after the panel examination and report, either party may, at his own expense, call any panel member as a witness, on the usual fee basis for employment of medical experts. 

No physician shall be designated by the Academy of Medicine of Cleveland to serve on the panels unless (1) he is an acknowledged expert in the field which he is to represent and (2) he is willing to accept compensation for his services under a fee schedule established by the Academy of Medicine with the approval of the court. 

Criteria of the Academy of Medicine for selection of persons to serve on the panel were that (1) They should not be persons habitually associated in court trials with either the plaintiff or defense. (2) They should be eminently qualified and locally and nationally recognized and competent in the specialty they represent. The feeling generally among the 

Medical Witness Committee was that panel members should come from the older age group that is, 45 years of age and over whenever feasible.

This plan was adopted for a two-year trial period beginning November 9, 1959. As of November 30, 1960 (more than one year later) only four orders of court had been issued to utilize it. The weaknesses are the refusal to identify panel members as such to the trial jury; the cumbersome provisions for use of three experts, more or less independently, in each case, and the requirement that counsel for both parties agree to the use of the plan. See article from the 

Cleveland Press, November 30, 1960. LA - 8 -

I 

THE LOS ANGELES PLAN 

1 1 Section 1871 of the California Code of Civil Procedure authorizes courtappointed expert witnesses. The Superior Court of Los Angeles County sponsored an amendment to this law, so that such experts now may be paid by the County.

With cooperation by the Los Angeles County Medical Association, highly qualified experts in each area of medical specialty were selected and persuaded to serve on panels as Impartial witnesses upon request of the Court. Their names are kept confidential, and the lists for each panel are maintained so that the members will be called upon to serve in rotation. The administration of the plan is entirely in the hands of two Superior Court Judges. 

During the pre-trial stages of any case, the Judge or either party may request that the injured or disabled person be examined by an Impartial Expert and the final decision in the matter is made by the Judge. 

One Impartial Expert may be assigned to the case for each medical specialty which is involved. If counsel for either party objects to the assignment of the expert who is next in regular order on the panel, he is passed over, and subsequent experts are considered in order until agreement is reached. 

The Impartial Expert is given copies of the medical reports of both sides relating to the subject; and when his examination is complete his report in turn is given to the Judge and to counsel for both parties. 

If the case is not settled during pre-trial, the Impartial Expert may be called as a witness during the trial, by the party who requested his participation in the first place; but the Judge determines finally if he is to appear. 

If he appears, the Jury is informed that he is a court-appointed, Impartial Expert. He may be cross-examined by both sides, or by the Judge. 

The fee charged by the Impartial Expert must conform to the scale established by the California Industrial Accident Commission. It is paid by the party who moved for appointment of the expert, or if in the opinion of the Judge this party is unable to pay the fee, it may be paid by the County. 

This plan as it now stands contains a unique and rather limiting feature: 

Every case in which an impartial medical examination is desired must be transferred to the Chief Judge for handling, including trial. 1 1 - 9 -

Trial Judges requested impartial examinations in 76 additional cases, 33 of them before trial actually began, and 20 during trial. Of these, 44 were thereafter settled, before verdict or judgment. Trial Judges in non-jury cases requested the impartial examinations after trial in the remaining cases, to aid them in determination of damages.

Group A, Group B and the 76 cases explained above total 554 cases. 

An additional 107 cases were referred for impartial examinations for various special reasons, such as preference applications, motions to transfer cases to or from City Court, mental health issues, etc.

Of the 554 cases comprising the general run, more than half (290) were settled before trial assignment, after the impartial examinations. Another 101 cases were settled after they had been assigned for trial. Thus 391 cases, or 

72% of the total had been settled short of final judgment or verdict. Only 62 cases, or 11% went all the way through trial. The rest were withdrawn, dismissed, transferred to a lower court; or were awaiting results of impartial examinations, or awaiting trial as of the closing date in the study. 

Justice Aron Steuer points out that all cases reported here are ones in which irreconcilable differences existed on the question of damages before the impartial medical examinations; and when this fact is considered, the percentage which went to trial is remarkably low. CHICAGO

Impartial medical examinations were ordered in a total of 67 cases through September 15, 1961 by the United States District Court for the Northern District of Illinois. The court has not compiled statistics showing the results. However, the Illinois State Medical Society is now collecting this data, and indications are that their report will prove the Plan has been quite successful. Judge Julius Miner spoke before the State Medical Society at the end of the first six months of operation of the Plan. He and all of the other Judges of this court who have been interviewed regarding Impartial Medical Testimony are unanimous in their enthusiasm for it. 

It is significant to note that the Illinois Supreme Court adopted a rule effective September 5, 1961 providing for Impartial Medical Testimony in the State Courts. The Illinois State Medical Society is cooperating by establishing panels of experts to serve those courts. They will review the membership of the panels yearly, and will inquire diligently into the quality of the examinations, reports and testimony of panel experts. The State Medical Society, which terms Impartial Medical Testimony "an important public service", has put up $5,000 of its own funds toward the payment of panel members during the next two years, and intends to appropriate another $5,000. - 11 -

Judge Francis L. Van Dusen has spoken to several interested groups in various cities regarding the successful and gratifying experience of this District 

Court with the Plan.

ULLT BALTIMORE 

The Plan is receiving very little use in Baltimore, and there are no statistics available at this writing relating to its operation. UTAH The Plan is applied only to Occupational Disease and Workmen's Compensation cases in Utah. For this reason, statistical information concerning it would have little value for comparative purposes. However, it is understood that impartial medical examinations have aided in the settlement of every case in which they have been used. . 12 -

SI 

CLEVELAND 

The Court of Common Pleas of Cuyahoga County, Ohio, is the only court now using the Plan which does not permit the panel member to be so identified when he is called to testify. This undesirable feature, plus other cumbersome features of the Cleveland Plan, are believed to have seriously weakened it. Operation of Impartial Medical Testimony has become a controversial issue among the lawyers and doctors in Cleveland, and the Plan has been used in only 3 cases over a 12-month period. No other statistics are available. 

LOS ANGELES

ITTIIN The Superior Court of Los Angeles County has compiled no statistics for the comparatively short period during which its Plan has been in use. 

However, it is understood that the plan is credited there with having brought about settlement of numerous cases, as a result of Judges merely indicating to the parties that impartial examinations appeared to be needed. Judge 

Reginald I. Bouder, speaking on March 11, 1961 at the American Medical Association's Medicolegal Symposium in San Francisco, stated that not a single case in which an Impartial Expert had been appointed had gone to trial. IFTTTT 1 Impartial Medical Testimony is used in a relatively small number of the total cases processed by a court. However, it is applied to those "hard core" cases which otherwise offer the least hope of settlement; and these cases are the ones whose trials consume much of the time and resources of the court.

Ideally, all cases in which medical issues are widely divergent should be identified in the pre-trial stage, and impartial examination ordered promptly. 

Remarkable proof of the effectiveness of the plan is seen, however, in the substantial numbers of cases which were settled after impartial examinations had been requested as late as the Calendar or Trial phase, in the New York figures.

The amount of use a Plan will get in any jurisdiction will depend not only upon the number of personal injury cases which are on the court calendar; but also upon how well it is organized and operated. 

The Plan affects many more cases than the ones in which it actually is used. In fact, the greatest benefits may well be obtained in those cases where it is not used, because the medical contentions of the parties are stated with less divergence, due to the influence of the Plan. - 13 -

SECTION III ARGUMENT 

!

Following are brief statements of each of the basic arguments for and against Impartial Medical Testimony, each one followed by the comment of this committee, based upon its research and investigation: PRO

Supporters of the Plan assert that: 1. 

Medical testimony under the Adversary System in personal injury cases has become so divergent as to reflect unfavorably upon the practice of medicine.

Comment: 

We regret to state that we believe this general criticism has been justly made, and that our own area is not immune to it. 2. 

Jurors are without technical knowledge to enable them to choose truly and correctly between the widely separated views of medical witnesses testifying for the plaintiff and defendant. 

Comment: 

This may in some instances be true not only of jurors, but of Judges and Counsel as well. It is aggravated by the tendency of certain medical witnesses to phrase their testimony in unnecessarily complex scientific and technical terms. This tends to defeat the purpose of the jury's deliberations, rather than to aid it. 3.

The testifying doctor who has the greater experience in the courtroom, and who is the more eloquent, may win the minds of the jurors over the doctor who may give more appropriately qualified but less eloquent testimony on the matters in question. 

Comment: 

We believe this assertion has merit, based upon our conferences with four Federal Judges in the

Northern District of Illinois and in the exhibitive literature which we have examined pertaining to several Impartial Medical Testimony plans which are in use, and particularly the material relating to the New York Plan. 1 - 14 -

1 A properly administered plan can focus upon personal injury cases testimony which is not only impartial, but is based upon medical knowledge of a caliber which might otherwise not be available. Comment:

Any successful plan must have as members on its medical specialty panels doctors of the highest professional reputations and attainments, such as Professors of Medicine and 

Department Heads of great hospitals. Due to the nature of their work, such doctors do not usually make themselves available to litigants; but they will respond to a request from the Court, in the interest of serving the ends of justice in a case of perplexing contradictory medical claims. The rotating feature in the assignment of panel members to the cases insures these busy doctors that they will not be called upon in this way with burdensome frequency. 5.

The Plan can reduce court calendar congestion and save money for the taxpayer. Comment:

Facts and figures compiled on the results of several years of operation of the New York

Plan have proved this beyond any doubt. 

Although the Impartial Medical Testimony Plan actually functions in a comparatively small number of cases, these are the "hard core" of the total case load, the ones which otherwise could be expected to consume substantial amounts of the court's time and resources. 

The Committee on the Medical Expert Testi mony Project in New York reported (Impartial 

Medical Testimony, p. 35), "Savings will be effectuated of more than 10 times the total amount spent for the fees of impartial experts.' 

A Judge of the U. S. District Court for the 

Northern District of Illinois said the Plan has materially aided in reducing the backlog of cases in that Court. 15 

---

6.

The Plan results in improved examinations, reports and testimony on the part of doctors engaged by the plaintiffs and defendants. 

Comment: 

1 We believe this is to be true. When it is known in advance that any widely divergent medical claims will be examined by the Court with the aid of an impartial analysis by a recognized authority, both the lawyers and the doctors for the parties will wish their findings to bear up well under such scrutiny. "It has had a wholesome prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of medical testimony in court." (Impartial Medical 

Testimony, p. 5) . 3 11 1 - 16 -

? 기 ​C Ο Ν 

Opponents of Impartial Medical Testimony allege that: 

1 1. 

No Doctor can be completely impartial. His individual background, and all of his education, training and experience have contributed to his total concept of Medicine, and have made him "partial" toward personal conclusions and theories which differ in varying degrees from those of other Doctors.

Comment: 

This is true, although it is not of such consequence as to affect the recognized basic procedures in the practice of Medicine.

Moreover, it is irrelevant to the issues in this discussion. Impartiality, within the meaning of the Plan, is complete lack of identification with the parties in a lawsuit. 

If the Impartial Medical Expert meets this test, and is of the caliber that he must be to become a member of a panel, his examination, report and testimony will serve the purpose intended in the Plan, 2.

There are two schools of medical thought regarding certain types of physical conditions. An expert, no matter how well qualified or ethical, will not be completely impartial when he has a predilection toward a certain theory or school of thought; and this in itself can constitute a bias hurtful to the plaintiff or to the defendant. 

Comment: 

Experience in the New York and Chicago

Plans has proved that cases fitting this description are very infrequent. However, if they are encountered, the impartial expert, if he is used, can and should testify that there are two schools of thought; state the one to which he subscribes; and explain the other.

It should be noted that there is no reason why the impartial expert cannot be asked by either plaintiff's or defendant's attorneys whether there are two schools of thought in an effort to bring this out before the jury. HELL - 17 -

Justice Aron Steuer has remarked, however, that if he realized in advance there were two schools of thought regarding the alleged injuries in a case, he would not call an Impartial Expert, as he is not interested in a discussion of the philosophy of basic medical problems, but is seeking (1) facts, and (2) justice -- and he feels that the existence of conflicting medical views can be brought out by the examining physicians for the plaintiff and the defendant, providing they are well-qualified experts. 3.

If the impartial witness is able to so identify himself when testifying, it is tantamount to vesting him in the cloak of infallibility. 

Comment: 

The Plan contemplates nothing of the kind. It merely gives notice to the jury that the panel member is a completely neutral witness, beholden to neither plaintiff nor defendant, and his testimony is to be considered as "impartial." It does not carry with it thereby any greater weight with respect to his qualifications or ability to testify in the field in which he has established competence.

Four Judges of the United States District Court for the Northern District of Illinois were asked this question individually. Each replied independently, to the effect that in 18 months' experience up to that time, they had received not one objection, based upon an alleged cloak of "infallibility. 18 4.

Doctors will rely upon this Plan as an opportunity to escape from their duty to testify for the parties, when requested. Comment:

The basic purpose of an Impartial Medical Testimony Plan is to make available to the Court the knowledge of those physicians and surgeons who would not otherwise testify in personal injury cases. The plan is operable only when there is a major division of opinion between doctors already acting on each side of the case. Based upon the experience in New York, Philadelphia,and Chicago, it is evident that the Plan brings more physicians into this field than would otherwise be available. ..

WE - 18 - 1

5. 

The jurors have the ability to sift the truth out of conflicting testimony by doctors and it is not necessary to have impartial experts to resolve such differences.

Basically, this objection relates to what is termed the preservation of the jury system and the right of trial on the merits of the case before a jury.

Comment: It is difficult for the members of our profession to understand how laymen can possibly resolve medical testimony presenting widely divergent views on questions which might trouble many doctors, if they were not experienced in the particular specialty involved. Therefore it would seem that the judge and jury should have the opportunity to hear testimony from a source having all the necessary medical qualifications in addition to the qualification of total lack of identification with either of the parties. Such testimony will be given only such weight in the minds of the jurors as their judgment dictates. 

There have been instances in which the testimony of the panel member apparently was completely disregarded. 6. . (Similar to 5.) The Plan deprives the plaintiff of his right of trial by jury.

Comment: The jury will be instructed by the judge that the impartial expert's testimony is to be given no greater weight than that of any other qualified expert who may testify. It is clear that the jury has the right to disregard the testimony of the panel member; and this objection therefore is considered to be extreme. The panel expert's only function is to give medical testimony in keeping with scientific truths. He can be cross-examined just as vigorously as any other witness in the proceeding. The function of the court and the jury is to search out the truth and this is just another device to help them arrive at a more perfect expression of the truth than would otherwise be obtainable. - 19 -

7. 

The Judge, having selected a witness, becomes a party in the case and therefore is no longer qualified to conduct the proceeding impartially.

Comment: 

This is pure fiction in that the Judge does not select the panel member. He merely calls upon the Medical Society (or other controlling agency) to furnish an expert in the medical specialty involved in the dispute, to testify and assist in bringing out the truth. The Judge does not know the identity of the panel member in advance of making the request, nor does he know the details of the expert's qualifications until he receives the report or accepts the testimony. (This comment is based upon an assumption that the panel members' names are being kept confidential, and that a proper rotating system is in use for assignment of the members to the cases as requests are received from the court.) 8. .

The panel member is severely handicapped by reason of the lapse of time between the incident causing the injuries and the time of his examination.

Comment: 

A careful analysis of this criticism discloses the fact that both plaintiffs' and defendants' lawyers frequently call expert medical witnesses to testify in court proceedings on the basis of examinations which they have made just a few weeks before trial. Medical witnesses in that category are in no better position than the panel member; nevertheless, they testify. 

If we were to assume that the only medical testimony available or produced in the trial of a given case came from the treating physicians and from those doctors who had the opportunity to examine the patient at an early date and who had access to all of the clinical records available to the treating physician, we still would recognize that the panel expert who has the opportunity to review all the reports, records, et cetera, and further has the opportunity to examine the plaintiff, can resolve major areas of disagreement to a substantial degree. - 20 -

In fact, it would seem that the judge and jury are entitled to weigh the testimony of the doctors for the parties with that of the impartial expert on the basis of examinations which all have made within a relatively short space of time, preferably close to the date of trial. 9.

The Impartial medical panel should be used primarily for pre-trial conferences; but in the event the case must be tried, the panel member may be permitted to testify but not to reveal his identity as court-appointed, impartial expert.

Comment: 

We have examined this suggestion critically and make the following observations: (a) In the only jurisdiction where this limitation applies (The Court of Common Pleas of Cuyahoga County, Ohio), the Plan has been used only three times within a year. (b) We believe that the parties would not be as anxious to settle their differences if they were assured that the panel member would not be permitted to so identify himself because where the expert is allowed to do so, , that fact alone is one of the most impelling forces to bring about settlement before trial. (c) The fact that the impartial expert is permitted to so identify himself in several jurisdictions where the Plan has been in operation long enough to permit evaluation of the results is the basic cause for the improvement which has been noted in the quality of plaintiffs' and defendants' medical examinations, reports and testimony.

One Judge points out that the limitation is selfdefeating: The panel member would be asked by one of the opposing counsel, "Who referred this client to you for examination, Doctor?" -- and if the doctor refused to answer, he would not be permitted to testify; whereas if he did answer that question, he would thus identify himself as a panel member.

UK - 21 -

SECTION IV OBSERVATIONS 

.- tri-

OBSERVATIONS 

AND

CONCLUSION 

After studying the Plans which are now in use, reading the numerous articles about them, and analyzing the arguments regarding Impartial Medical Testimony, we have reached the conclusion that a Plan, if it is to function successfully, must embody the principles described below. 

There are several ways to implement these basic principles; and this Committee does not intend to set forth the mechanics of a Plan, feeling that to do this is the proper function of the officers of the Court where the Plan will be used:

A. 

Selecting the specialists who will serve on the panels must be done with the greatest of care, and should be left to Medical Societies. B.

The names of the members of each panel should be kept strictly confidential.

C. Assignment of the experts to the cases should be made on a rotating basis, both to aid in keeping their identity confidential and to avoid undue hardship or burden upon any individual expert.

D. Only one expert should be assigned to a case for each special field of Medicine which is involved in the alleged injuries.

E. 

In the interest of fairness, the rules should permit either party or the Judge to request the services of an impartial expert. F.

If the parties do not agree upon the assignment of the expert who is next in line on the panel list, only one alternate (the doctor whose name appears second) should be offered; and if the parties will not agree on one of the two, the Judge should assign one of them. This will prevent counsel from "shopping" the entire panel, in search of an expert whom they may prefer, with the result that identity of all the members will become known, as well as the order of their rotation. ! - 22 -

G. The impartial expert should have the benefit of all reports, X-rays, laboratory studies, etc., which have been used by the experts for the plaintiff and defendant in arriving at their conclusions. H.

Contact by counsel for either party with the impartial expert should be discouraged. ! I.

The litigants must be permitted also to call their own medical experts to testify. 1 J.

The panel expert who is called to testify should be introduced to the jury as a court-appointed, impartial witness. 1 This is the heart of a successful Plan. 

The impartiality of the expert is an indispensible ingredient of his testimony, of which the jury should be aware. 

Either party should have the right to demonstrate to the jury all of the qualifications of a witness, including his identification with the parties, or lack of it. K.

Both parties and the Judge should have unlimited rights to cross-examine the panel expert. L.

The panel experts should be expected to set fees for their services which they would normally charge a patient in moderate circumstances. They should not be obliged to accept fees fixed according to any sort of arbitrary rate structure.

M. Panel experts should not know the source of their compensation at least until after their service in the case has been concluded. Under ideal circumstances, where public funds have been provided for payment of the experts, such secrecy is of course unnecessary.

It is our conclusion that a Plan embodying these principles can be of substantial benefit to the Bench, the Bar, the Medical Profession and the general public in our area. - 23-

An Appendix to this report is composed of articles, speeches, newspaper accounts, and progress reports from the various jurisdictions where Impartial Medical Testimony Plans have been in use. Expressions of the views of supporters and opponents, including Jurists, Attorneys and Physicians and resolutions of the American Medical Association and American Bar Association are included. 

The Appendix was compiled to afford a fair and accurate cross-section of all the informed thought, favorable and otherwise, regarding the principles of the several Plans and mechanics of their operation. 

We recommend also to those who wish to be fully informed the reading of the book Impartial Medical Testimony, a report by a Special Committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, on the Medical Expert Testimony Project (published 1956 by MacMillan). 

The final item in the Appendix is a bibliography, listing many additional sources of information on this subject. - 24 -

Adopted by American Bar Association July, 1957

REPORT OF COMMITTEE ON IMPARTIAL MEDICAL TESTIMONY

Section of Judicial Administration

July, 1957 - #71

AMERICAN BAR ASSOCIATION REPORT OF THE SECTION OF JUDICIAL ADMINISTRATION RECOMMENDATION The Section of Judicial Administration submits the following resolution and recommendation for adoption thereof :

I. 

RESOLVED, That the American Bar Association adopt a national program, to be implemented at the local level, of fostering the creation and employment of panels of impartial medical experts, under court aegis, in the pre-trial consideration and trial of personal injury cases especially in those communities where there is a volume of personal injury litigation in the courts and where there is a sufficient number of qualified doctors available to constitute a panel; 1 1 That the panel be selected by professional bodies on the basis of professional qualifications; 

That the panel be employed at the pre-trial and trial stage of such cases; and 

That copies of this resolution, together with the attached report of the Section's Committee on Impartial Medical Testimony dated August, 1956, be forwarded to all State and local bar associations represented in the House of Delegates with a recommendation that this program be adopted. 

REPORT 

1. 

Reasons for Recommendation

As personal Injury cases constitute the bulk of litigation in the courts of this country, and as a large part of the time consumed in the trial of personal Injury cases is devoted to ascertaining the nature and extent of the injuries, any procedure which would improve or expedite the taking of this evidence and the ascertainment of the medical facts would be a major contribution to the administration of justice. *As amended and adopted by the House of Delegates at the 1957 Annual Meeting.

The medical phase of personal Injury litigation particularly calls for court attention and remedial action, not only because of the large part of court time absorbed in the medical inquiry, but also because under prevailing methods of taking medical proof courts often are not receiving sufficiently reliable reports of medical facts. 1 The effectiveness of such panels of medical experts and the ease with which they may be established are clearly demonstrated by the results which have been obtained in the Supreme Court of the State of New York, First Department, and the Supreme Bench of Baltimore City, Maryland. 

The details of the setting up and of the functioning of these plans are fully set forth in the attached report. 

2. Expenditures Contemplated by Recommended Action 

It is believed that the adoption of this resolution will not require the expenditure of Association funds beyond the cost of reproducing and distributing copies of this recommendation and report. Conclusion

The Section of Judicial Administration believes that the establishment of impartial medical panels in the courts in centers of population would be a fruitful undertaking by the bench, bar and medical profession. Indeed, we can think of no single step that would be as pointed and effective a contribution to the administration of justice as enlleting this aid in the consideration and disposition of personal Injury cases under the leadership of the American Bar Association. 

Respectfully submitted,

SECTION OF JUDICIAL ADMINISTRATION

AMERICAN BAR ASSOCIATION SECTION OF JUDICIAL ADMINISTRATION REPORT OF COMMITTEE ON IMPARTIAL MEDICAL TEST IMONY 

August, 1956

The Committee on Impartial Medical Testimony, appointed by the Chair man of the Section of Judicial Administration of the American Bar Association, recommends to the Section the adoption of a national program, to be implemented at the local level, of employing Lopartial medical experts in the pre-trial consideration and trial of personal Injury cases.

As personal injury cases constitute the bulk of litigation in the courts of this country, and as a large part of the time consumed in the trial of personal lajury cases 18 devoted to accertoInlag the nature and extent of the injuries, any procedure which would Lup xove or expedite the taking of this evidence and the ascertainment of the medical facts would be a major contribution to the administration of justice. 

The medical phase of personal Injury litigation particularly calls for court attention and remedial action, not only because of the large part of court tima absorbed in the medical inquiry, but also because under prevailing methods of taking medical proof courts are not receiving sufficiently reliable reports of the medical facts. While the weighing and determination of the facto bearing on liability can satisfactorily be made by lay judgments on the basis of the common experience of a jury, the determination of medical facts calls for special kaowledge which a jury' does not possess.

The jury 1o dependent on the word of experts in this scientific realm. 

That & Jury does not receive altogether trustworthy information

---

ind reliable guidance under the purely adversary method of presenting medical evidence through partisan experts is abundantly clear. 

A sample study of cases in the Supreme Court of New York revealed that in a quarter of the cases there were gross errors in the reading of Xorays and that in over a quarter of the cases essential testo to warrant a diagnosis were not made, WI !

We may accept with a certain equanimity the controversies presented at to the facts of how an accident happened and who was to blame. Such contro- versies cannot be avoided or eliminated, and can as well be resolved by a representative body of the community as by any other means. 

It 18 disturbing, however, indeed discouraging, to find such variations as frequently exist on the professional plane of inedical testimony. 

Unfortunately, we have developed a fashion of employing doctors as well as lawyers as advocates, and the doctors re- tained as experts for the respective sides may, albeit unconsciously, readily become partisans. 

Because of the scientific nature of medical evidence the jury 18 more often confused than enlightened by such a presentation of that evidence, and is left without adequate insight to determine the facts or pass upon the credibility of the contesttag experts. Experts are frequently sought and retained more for a cultivated art of testifying than for basic competence and essential ! Integrity.

The best corrective for this condition is rather apparent. It 18 to bring into the medical Inquiry neutral experts of uniquestioned competence and honesty, doctors of the highest professional qualifications who have no interest other alan the elicitation and presentation of objective truth. Establishing a panel of such exparts who will be available at the call of the court in those cages which present a substantial controversy as to the nature or extent of the T- 2

Injuries 18 a proper function of a court. 

It can easily and effectively be done as has been derronstrated by court undertakings in two cities, New York and Baltimore.

In the Supreme Court of the State of New York, First Department, an Independent medical panel of authorities in the several branches of medicine was established by the court in collaboration with the city bar association and city medical societies.

Appointments to the panel are made by a joint committee of the New York Academy of Medicine and New York County Medical Society. Members of the panel in their required specialtles are called upon by the court in those cases where substantial differences exist between the parties as to the injury. 

In those cases a reference 18 made to the panel at the pre-trial stage of the case, and the panel doctor makes an examination of the plaintiff and reports his findinge to the court and to the parties. 

Another pre-trial conference follows the submission of the report, giving the parties an opportunity to come to ... PL agreement as to the medical issue in the light of the independent expert's report.

Experience has demonstrated that in most cases a settlement follows as a result of the reference to the panel. 

In the cases which have to go to trial • the impartial expert is subject to call by either party or by the court. All costs of the reference, of the report of testimony of the independent expert, are paid by the court.

For an initial test period funds for this purpose were supplied by the Alfred P. Sloan Foundation and Ford Motor Company Fund, Inc. 

For the past year all costs of panel services have been paid directly by the court and included in the regular court budget. 

The Baltimore plan 18 in essence similar to the New York plan, except that designation of an independent expert is made only upon application of one of

the parties, and payment for the expert's services (s made by the parties, either by inclusion in costs, or by division in a stipulated manner, or by the direction of the court.

Payment of the expert's fec is made by the clerk of the court in order that the physician may not know the source of payment. The panel of ex® perts in the required specialties was selected by the Medical and Chirurgical Faculty of Maryland. While the appointment of a neutral expert can be made at any stage of the proceedings prior to the submission of a case to the jury, the plan contemplates the appointment at an early stage so that an ample opportunity for settlement negotations can be had in the light of the neutral expert's findings.

Your committee has no doubt that a court has inherent power to establish and employ an impartial medical penel along the lines developed in New 

York and Baltimore.

If existing rules of court are not sufficient authority for the creation and use of such a panel, rules can be adopted to carry out the program.

We are satisfied from our observations of the New York. and Baltimore experience that the introduction of impartial medical experts of the highest standing into those cases where the parties are in dispute as to the medical facts has the following wholesome and beneficial results: 1.

It improves the process of finding the medical facts, vastly increasing the likelihood of reaching the right result. 2.

It serves to relieve court congestion by bringing about the settlement of many cases which would otherwise have to be tried and which by their nature would entail lengthy trials. 3.

It has a prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of medical testimony in court. 4.

The modest cost involved in the payment of Independent experts is a positive economy in effecting a large saving in court operations,

The plan and proceclure for establishing and cmploying an independent medical panel may readily be acopted in any community where there 18 a volume of personal injury 11t1.gation in the courts, and where there is a pufficient number of qualified doctors available to constitute a panel. 

The first and principal essential of a successful panel program is the availability and selection of experts of the highest qualifications. Such doctors would eschew the role of a partisan and would ordinarily refuse to appear in litigation. They are almost invariably willing, however, to respond to a call from the court as a public and professional duty. 

It is not difficult, therefore, to recruit a panel, but it is essential that the selection be made by professional bodies on the basis of professional qualifications. 

The panel muy readily be employed at either the pre-trial stage or trial stage of a case, but its most cffective use is at a pre-trial stage. It 18 therefore recommended that the selection of cases for a panel reference be nade at a pre-trial conference at which the medical claims and differences con be detected, and that the findings of the independent medical expert also be 1 taken up at a pre-trial conference with a view to effecting some agreement upon the medical facts and a disposition of the case. 

The manner of payment for the services of the impartial expert is optional.

It can be made, as in New York, from court funda, or, as in Baltimore, by some arrangement or division between the parties. 

Your committee believes that the establishment of impartial medical panels in the courts in centers of population would be a fruitful undertaking by the bench, bar, and medical profession. Indeed, we can think of no single step that would be as pointed and effective a contribution to the adıninistration of justice as enlisting this aid in the consideration and disposition of personal injury cases.

We recommend that the Section of Judicial Administration of the

American Bar Association undertake a national program of fostering the creation of such panels under court aegis in the communities in which a panel might be effectively employed. 

Respectfully submitted,

DELMAR KARLEN EMORY H. NILES DAVID W. PECK LELAND L. TOLMAN

AMERICAN MEDICAL ASSOCIATION HOUSE OF DELEGATES 

ET 

Dictated by:

JPP-eb-6/21/61 

Supp. Rep.: J 

Submitted by: 

Board of Trustees 

Julian P. Price, M.D., Chairman

Subject: 

Non-Partisan Medical Testimony 

Referred to:

Reference Committee on Reports of Board of Trustees (Frank A. MacDonald, M.D., Chairman)

Inasmuch as approximately 70 per cent of all trial litigation involves medical testimony, it is readily apparent that the physician occupies a major role in the administration of justice, Frequently, the outcome of personal injury litigation depends upon a "battle of experts" between opposing medical witnesses.

Whether judge and jury believe one doctor more than another too often is predicated upon nothing more than courtroom manner, personality, or forensic ability. Physicians are well aware of this and as a consequence many highly qualified doctors refuse to be treated as partisans and will not voluntarily participate as expert witnesses in personal injury litigation. This has given rise to the so-called "professional" medical witness who is willing to devote a substantial amount of his time to court appearances on a fee basis. 

The medical witness may be faced in court with contrary opinions of medical treatises. Unfamiliar with legal debate and subjected to a determined advocate, a physician can frequently be harried into confusion and discomfort.

The chronic court congestion which exists in many metropolitan areas in the handling of personal injury litigation means a delay of anywhere from three to five years before a case comes to trial. In many instances, the parties are unable to agree upon a figure for settlement because of a dispute concerning the injuries sustained by the plaintiff, If they could anticipate with some reasonableness the medical findings which would be made in the event of trial, this would substantially facilitate the settlement of claims, However, where partisan medical testimony is irreconcilable, there can be no termination of the case except by trial. This, we are informed, has caused court congestion to increase to the point where it would be impossible to put court calendars on a current basis except by multiplying the judicial manpower by many times,

It is therefore requested that the House of Delegates endorse the principle of non-partisan medical testimony in the trial of personal Injury litigation. We urge the House to approve this supplementary report and adopt the following resolution:

Bo of T. Supp. Rep. J - page 2 ,

Whereas, The partisan selection of medical expert witnesses in personal injury litigation tends to promote biased medical testimony and is therefore degrading to the medical profession and hampers the equitable administration of justice; now therefore be it 

Resolved, that the American Medical Association endorses the principle of non-partisan medical testimony; and be it further 

Resolved, That the American Bar Association and other national bar and judicial organizations having a bona-fide interest in the subject, be invited to participate in a joint venture for the purpose of formulating a model plan for non-partisan medical testimony. 

The above report was amended in accordance with the following recommendation by the Reference

Committee on Reports of the Board of Trustees: "9.

Non-Partisan Medical Testimony (Supplementary Report I) 

1 This report deals with the problems of medical expert testimony. It states that the American Medical Association endorses the principle of non-partisan medical testimony and that the American Bar Association and other national bar and judicial organizations who have a bona fide interest in this project be invited to participate in a joint venture for the purpose of formulating a model plan for non-partisan medical testimony.
