Caribbean Suite Impartial Medical Testimony 

"Court Appointed Expert Medical Witness"

COURT-APPOINTED EXPERT MEDICAL WITNESS 

The advent of the automobile, the enactment of Workmen's Compensation Acts, and similar legislation have flooded the Courts with personal injury cases. The value of these personal injury cases depends upon medical testimony. 

The plaintiff's lawyer seeks ! that medical testimony which will produce a maximum verdict; the defendant's lawyer seeks that medical testimony which will produce a minimum verdict.

The plaintiff's lawyer seeks a maximum doctor, and the defendant's lawyer a minimum doctor. 

Under these circumstances, it is inevitable that the doctor becomes a partisan along with the lawyer. 

The scope and 1 importance of this testimony is self evident when it is estimated that "Seven out of ten personal injury cases are decided on medical 2 considerations rather than legal".

This demand for medical testimony has brought in its wake the so-called medical expert, This medical expert is not the attending physician.

He is not employed to treat the patient, but to testify for the patient. "The reason for his employment is his value as a witness not his skill as a physician. 

He is a paid partisan witness.

As stated by David W. Peck, then Presiding 

Justice of the New York Supreme Court, Appellate Division, First 

18 Department:3 1. 2. 

See Reports Court Congestion Project of American Bar Foundation 

Ben F. Small "Law Schools need to be given a Shot of Medicine" 

American Bar Journal, August, 1955, Vol. 41, p. 693; David W.

Peck Court Organization and Procedures To Meet the Need of 

Modern Society, p. 15 (Reprint from the Indiana Law Journal 

Winter 1958) 

American Bar Journal, October 1956, Vol, 42, page 931 3.

"The system of presenting such testimony only through doctors retained by the parties and paid as medical mouthpieces has given rise to serious abuses and created grave doubts as to whether courts and juries are receiving reliable guidance in a HK field where they are dependent on experts.

The doctors retained by parties are obviously partisan. They are selected not for their medical qualifications or objectivity but principally for their personality and manner of testifying, their jury appeal. When, as often happens, the medical views expressed at the trial are as one-sided and far apart, as the parties' differences as to how the accident happened, the jury is left hopelessly confused."

These medical experts are divided into two classes plaintiffs' experts and defendants' experts. 

Plaintiffs' experts testify for plaintiffs; defendants' experts testify for defendants. "The testifying expert, hired by the lawyer and not the patient, is usually so much more plausible and ingratiating than the treating physician and so cooperative. 

Hence the trial degenerates into a battle of opposing experts. The jurors, with no better norms for finding the truth than personality appraisal of the experts, look in despair to the judge; and he looks back at them just as despairingly..4 4. . 

Bernard Botein, Associate Justice, Appellate Division, Supreme 

Court of New York - University of Detroit Law Journal, May 

1956, vol. 33, page 388 -2-

The expert witness is personable and engaging. 

The jury is duly impressed by his manner and his background, and well confused when two such eminent experts, representing opposing parties, seem to be talking about two different injuries, incurred by the same claimant in the same accident. The jury's dilemma is summed up in a recent magazine article as follows: "A large self-confident man enthrones himself in the witness chair.

Yes, he is a doctor specializing in diseases of the heart; he has written books; he has lectured to a generation of medical students. 

His expert professional opinion echoes utmost conviction:

The fall from the ladder damaged the man's heart. 2 Later a brisk, incisive witness for the defense, boasting equally impressive medical credentials, contradicts this conclusion. 'Impossible', he says, 'that a sound heart should be damaged by a blow that did not even raise a bruise.' He is positive that a man with a diseased heart climbed a ladder, suffered an attack and consequently fell. . 

Neither doctor ever saw the deceased. 

Each was hired not for his skill as a physician but for his value as a witness. If either had reached a different conclusion, he would not have been called.

You could decide either way with equal justice 

5. 

Readers Digest, November 1956, page 134 -3-

or lack of it."

The extreme differences in expert opinion is a common incident of today's trials. "Expert opinions do not always diverge to the extent of 100%. 

But opinions expressing differences as great as between 30% and 70%; 15% and 85%; or substantial' and 'minimal' are heard every day.,6

Nor can this consistent discrepancy be dismissed as mere errors in diagnosis.? "It seems to be a fact that the two opposing medical experts quite often disagree sharply in their testimony concerning cause and consequence of the injury. 

Such disagreement could, theoretically, derive from two sources; some bias on the part of the examining physician or the inherent difficulties of accurate medical diagnosis. 

This latter possibility, though, must be less important, since the divergence occurs invariably in the same direction the defendant's physician never sees as serious a liability as his counterpart Whatever the reason for this divergence, the present situation, in which the divergence tends always in the direction of the party who pays the witness, is deplorable and patently not intended by the law."

The problem of the medical expert was early recognized by both the legal profession and the medical profession. 

6. Judge Emory H. Niles, Chief Judge of Supreme Bench of Baltimore

City - Delaware State Medical Journal, October 1957, Vol. 29, page 247 7.

Prof. Zeizel, University of Chicago Law School - Stanford Law 

Review, July 1956, Volume 8, page 735 4-

"The situation has been obvious on all hands, and an extensive literature about it, mostly developed in the medical field, ranges over the past half century.

Most of the literature is critical and destructive. 

Flew constructive proposals, that are at all feasible, have been made. 

The principal feature of the breakdown seems to be the distrust of the expert witness, as one whose testimony is shaped by his bias for the party calling him. 

That bias itself is due, partly to the special fee which has been paid or promised him, and partly to his prior consultation with the party and his self-commital to a particular view.1,8 

In 1926, The Standing Committee on Jurisprudence and Law 

Reform of the American Bar Association (Judge Taft later Mr. Chief 

Justice Taft - Chairman) made certain suggestions with reference to law reform.

One of the suggestions was the appointment of a Court-appointed Expert, "which suggestion might conveniently be ,9 embodied in an Act of Congress. 1 An effort to meet the problem was made by the Model Expert Testimony Act approved by the National Conference of Commissioners 10 on Uniform Laws in 1937, and re-designated a Model Act in 1943. 

The reason for the Act is summed up in the Commissioner's Prefatory Note wherein, among other things, it is stated: 

8. Wigmore on Evidence, 3rd Ed., vol. II, par. 563, page 645. 

See also McCormick on Evidence, Par. 1718, pages 34-39; Law and contemporary Problems, 1935, Vol. 2, pages 403-524 

9. Reports of American Bar Association, Vol. 51, pages 435-441, at 437 10. U.L.A., Volume 9, Miscellaneous Act, pages 429-439 (See discussion pages 429-430) 5-

"Parties consistently employ experts upon the strength of their bias, which may range from a professional prejudice to naked perjury. 

There can be no doubt of the need for expert testimony. The problem is how to eliminate the evils of bias and partisanship which shape it.

The National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform State Laws has prepared an act which is aimed to remedy the evils."

Section (1) of this Act empowered the Court to appoint expert witnesses whenever "the Court deems expert evidence is desirable". A similar provision is to be found in Rule 403 of the 11 

Model Code of Evidence of the American Law Institute, The reasons for the Act are even more valid today than WA when the Act was passed, but the Act itself seems antiquated. It belongs to the age before the Modern Rules, with their flexibility, discovery procedures, pre-trial conferences, and other procedures designed to remove a trial from a sporting event to an accurate 1 appraisal of the facts involved in a legal controversay. 

In furtherance of the intent and spirit of these Rules, and as a result of the dissatisfaction with, and inaccurate nature of, the medical testimony in personal injury cases, two well integrated plans have been developed to cope with this problem. 11. 

American Law Institute - Model Code of Evidence, page 202; See also Rule 35(a) Federal Rules of Practice and Procedure, and

Compilation of Cases and Statutes, 63 Yale Law Journal 1023 (1954) -6-

Plans concerning Expert Medical Testimony 

To meet the problem of the so-called medical expert, two general plans have been created. 

One plan is usually designated as the New York Plan, the other, the Minnesota Plan. 

Both plans seek the same results but approach the problem by different methods. 

The Minnesota Plan is predicated on disciplinary action after the fact.

The New York Plan seeks to insure impartial testimony before the fact.

The New York Plan is often associated with the so-called Baltimore Plan.

The Baltimore Plan has received some publicity, but, as stated by Judge Emory H. Niles, Chief Judge of 12 the Supreme Bench of Baltimore City: "I cannot, in addressing you here, pretend that in Baltimore we have an established and comprehensive Plan for Impartial 

Medical Testimony in full operation.

The subject has been under discussion for three or four years, and our Plan has been tried out in a number of cases. 

There has been, however, some opposition to it, and while I believe that the Plan will grow and be fully accepted within a reasonable time, I must confess that as of the present the situation could be best described as an emergence from the experimental stage and an entry into 11 the stage of education and acceptance by the Bar. 12. 

Delaware State Medical Journal, October 1957, Vol. 29, page 247 -7-

Minnesota Plan MUUT 

The Minnesota Plan was outlined by Dr. Ernest M.

Hammes, Chairman, Medical Testimony Committee, Minnesota State Bar Associa tion, at a Regional meeting of the American Bar Association held in 

Baltimore in 1956.13

It is primarily a Plan whereby charges may be brought against a physician for alleged biased or improper testimony after the testimony has been given and the case tried. 

These charges are heard by a panel of Doctors.

If the Panel finds that biased or improper medical testimony has been given, the testimony is discussed with the offending doctor by a member of the Board. 

The Panel does not have the power of revocation but in extreme cases can send the matter to the State Board of Medical Examiners. 

As of the date of Dr. Hammes' paper only one case had been forwarded to the State 

Board of Medical Examiners, but in a number of cases, the medical testimony of physicians has been discussed with members of the Panel, The Minnesota Plan has received widespread publicity, but has not been too successful. "The Minnesota Plan was initiated in 1940 Thereafter, the Illinois State Medical Society, the Kansas Medical Society, the Chicago Medical Society, and the Harris 

County Medical Society (Texas) established committees 13. 

The Daily Record*, May 18, 1957 *The Daily Record herein referred to is The Daily Record, a legal newspaper published in Baltimore City. -8-

patterned after the Medical Testimony Committee in Minnesota; however, these committees, in general, have not been successful. 

In addition to the above, the Louisiana State Medical Society and the Wisconsin Medical Society have worked with their state bar associations in connection with the review of medicolegal testimony

The Medical Disciplinary Board Act of Washington provides for the review of medical testimony by the disciplinary board. 14 Interest in the last few years has shifted from the 

Minnesota Plan to the New York Plan. 

New York Plan 

A program is now in operation originally sponsored by the Justices of the Supreme Court for the County of New York, the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, New York County 

Lawyers Association, New York Academy of Medicine and New York 

County Medical Society.

This program obtained a grant of $40,000.from the

Alfred P. Sloan Foundation and the Ford Motor Car Company Fund. 

It was initiated by the Justices of the Supreme Court in New York County.

The Court was dissatisfied with the conflicting medical testimony given by experts retained by the parties, and recognized the need for medical examinations and reports which were authoritative and impartial.

It recognized that the system of employing 

14. Journal American Medical Association, April 14, 1956, page 1334 -9-

partisan experts frequently resulted in the exaggeration of injuries by the plaintiff's experts and the minimizing of injuries by the defendant's experts. 

The New York Law Journal, November 25, 1952, in discussing the plan stated: "The project is prompted by the dissatisfaction of the Supreme Court Justices with the conflicting medical testimony which is given by the experts retained by the parties and the need for having medical examinations and reports which are both authoritative and impartial. 

The system of employing partisan experts by the parties frequently results in the exaggerating of injuries by the plaintiff's experts and minimizing of injuries by the plaintiff's experts and minimizing of injuries by the defendant's experts.

Because of their distaste for hiring themselves out as partisan experts, and because of the time which a court appearance consumes, many of the best qualified doctors have tended to stay clear of involvement in litigation. 

But leading medical men have indicated their willingness to respond to a call from the court and to aid in the administration of justice as 15 11 impartial experts.

Under this program sponsored by the Courts, the Medical Profession and the Bar, there was established: 

15. New York Law Journal, November 25, 1952. -10-

"a panel of independent experts, of the highest standing, who would be available to respond to a call from the Court for truly expert and objective medical findings on conflicting medical claims.

The stated requirements for panel membership was that the doctors should be top authorities in their branches of medicine and men who were not previously identified particularly with the plaintiff's or defendant's side of personal injury litigation,

The responsibility of selecting the members of the panel was delegated to a joint committee of the New York 16 

Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society. 

A panel of doctors was selected, and the Appellate Division promulgated the following special rule effective December 1st, 1952: "In any personal injury case in which, prior to the trial thereof, a justice shall be of the opinion that an examination of the injured person and a report thereon by an impartial 1 medical expert would be of material aid to the just determination of the case, he may, after consultation with counsel 1 for the respective parties, order such examination and report, without cost to the parties, through the Medical Report Office of the Supreme Court, New York County. 

The examination will be made by a member of a panel of examining physicians 1 designated for their particular qualifications by the New York 

16. Impartial Medical Testimony 21 F.R.D., pages 21-27 Article by David W.

Peck, former presiding Justice, Supreme Court of 

New York, Appellate Division, First Department. 

-11-

Academy of Medicine and New York County Medical Society. 

Copies of the report of the examining physician will be made available by the clerk of the Medical Report office to all parties.

If the case proceeds to trial after such examination and report, either party may call the examining physician as a witness, or the trial justice may, if he deems it desirable to do so, call the examining physician as a witness for the court, subject to questioning by any party, but without cost ,17 to any party. 

The Rule 28 is only evoked when there is a sharp dispute as to the nature and extent of the plaintiff's injuries, and it appears to the Judge that a report by an impartial medical expert would be helpful.

This dispute invariably appears in pre-trial conferences.

Arrangements are made to have the plaintiff examined, and copies of the Doctor's report are forwarded to the attorneys for all parties and to the Court, The matter is then discussed at a subsequent pre-trial conference. 

As might be anticipated, the use of the Rule has increased settlements since the disagreement over the nature and extent of the plaintiff's injury has been dissipated or minimized. 19 17. 18. 

Judge Bernard Botein in University of Detroit Law Journal, May 1956, Vol. 33, page 391

New York County Supreme Court Rules - Special Rules - Special Rule for Medical Examinations in Personal Injury Actions 

For discussion, see - Impartial Medical Testimony by the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, pages 28-35 19. -12-

Former Presiding Justice of the Appellate Division, David 

W. Peck in evaluating the Rule, states: "The most tangible result of the use of impartial medical testimony, which can be statistically expressed, is the settlement experience,

Approximately 575 cases have gone through the 

New York referral procedure.

Settlements at a pre-trial stage, attributable to the panel procedure, were made in 330 cases. 

Settlement during trial were made in another 62 cases, while 68 cases were tried to completion. 

There remain 115 cases awaiting trial or other disposition, It may fairly be assumed that at least half of those cases will still be settled, bringing the total number settled to 75% of the number of cases referred

This settlement ratio is not impressive in itself, as it is below the settlement average in the run of cases, but it is very impressive when one remembers that none of these cases could be settled at a regular pre-trial conference, and that they constituted a hard core of the most difficult cases, which by their nature would have taken longer than ordinary cases to try.

Thus the settlements effected contributed markedly to the relief of congestion and to the promptness of dispositions.

Equally or more important, the judges and lawyers concerned with those cases are satisfied that the dispositions made were more in accord with right and justice than would have been -13-

likely if the cases had gone to trial with only partisan HH medical testimony produced. 

And in the cases which went to trial, the judges certainly, and it is believed the lawyers generally, found that the impartial experts contributed significantly and soundly to the adjudication, It is interesting to note that in the cases in which the impartial expert was called as a witness at the trial he was called almost equally by the plaintiff and defendant. In thirty-five cases he was called by the plaintiff and in thirtynine cases by the defendant.

In only one case was he called 20 by the court.

The Plan has proven so successful that it is no longer supported by a grant, but is supported by public funds. 

Baltimore Plan

The creation of the Baltimore Plan, unlike the New York 

Plan, was accidental. 

The President of the Baltimore City Bar

Association and the President of the Maryland State Bar Association appointed three identical committees, with identical members on each Committee, to consider the mutual problems of doctors and lawyers.

The Medical and Chirurgical Faculty of the State of Maryland was then contacted.

The Medical and Chirurgical Faculty of the State of Maryland represents practically all the doctors in Maryland. It 20. 

21 F.R.D., pages 21-27 -24-

IN was incorporated January 20th, 1799, and is the third oldest Association of its kind in the United States. 

It approved the idea, and appointed members to the Committees of the Bar Associations so that each committee consisted of an equal number of doctors and lawyers. 

One of the primary purposes of the Bar Associations in appointing its committees was to explore with the Medical and Chirurgical Faculty the advisability of public symposiums at which lawyers and doctors might discuss matters of mutual interest to each profession, as well as matters of interest to the public. 

The second symposium had as its topic "The Doctor in court 

Expert Medical Testimony."

One of the speakers at this symposium was Dr. Conrad Wolfe, Professor of Medicine at the University of 

Maryland, and a member of the Occupational Disease Board of the State of Maryland.

In discussing his experience on the Board, which Board is made up entirely of Doctors, Dr. Wolfe, among other things, said: "These doctors (members of the Board) cross question the 'Expert Witness' and learn almost at once that his knowledge is fundamentally lacking. 

He reasons falsely from ignorance of basic science. 

His conclusions are untenable. However, he is glib and has picked up enough pseudo-professional jargon so that in the hands of a sharp lawyer he could probably impress a jury.

However, he is heard by physicians, not by jurymen, and the value of his testimony is zero. 

Wlay has the attorney brought such a man to the witness stand as his Expert Witness?

Why has such a doctor connived at being rated as an -15-

Expert Witness in a matter in which his knowledge was so palpably deficient?"

At the conclusion of his paper, Dr. Wolfe suggested that the Medical and Chirurgical Faculty prepare a panel of impartial expert witnesses; that from this panel medical experts be selected for use in judicial proceedings.

He further stated that it was the obligation of physicians, as a matter of civic responsibility to 22 serve on this panel.

Immediately following this suggestion, one of the joint committees of Doctors and Lawyers began working on such a plan. 

It was first believed that special legislation would be necessary to put such a plan into effect. 

After further study, the Committee decided that no statute was necessary, and that Discovery Rule 420 

of Practice and Procedure of the Court of Appeals of Maryland contained the necessary power to put the plan into operation.

This 

Rule provides: "Whenever the mental or physical condition of a party is material to any matter involved in any proceeding, the Court may, upon motion by any party and notice to all other parties, for good cause shown, order such party to submit to a mental or physical examination by a physician or physicians. The order (1) shall specify the time, place, manner, conditions 21. 22. 

Maryland Law Review, Vol. XIII, No. 4, page 294 

For complete Symposium see: Maryland Law Review, Vol. XIII, No. 4, pages 285-323 -16-

and scope of the examination and the person or persons by whom it is to be made and (2) may regulate the filing of a report of the findings and conclusions and the testimony at the trial by the examining physician or physicians, the payment of the expenses of the examination and any other 23 relevant matters.

There is nothing so new and revolutionary about this Rule and the application of this Rule to the appointment of a 

Court-appointed expert.

In 1909, a special committee of the Maryland State Bar Association recommended a bill under the title "Expert Witness".

The first section provided : 

"Any Judge of any Court of Record in this State shall in any case, civil or criminal, pending before said Court, on a motion of any party therein, or on its own motion, at any time before or during the trial thereof, when the cause of justice seems to require it, and after notice to the parties, and a hearing, appoint one or more disinterested, skilled persons to serve as an expert witness ,,24 

You will note Rule 420 is similar to Rule 35 of the

Federal Rules of Practice and Procedure. 

Insofar as Maryland is concerned, the Rule had its foundation in Maryland Case Law. As early as the case of United Railways and Electric Co. vs. Cloman , 23. Maryland Rules of Procedure, Rule 420, Annotated Code of 

Maryland, Vol. 9, 1957 Ed. 

24. Proceedings Maryland State Bar Association, 1909 -17-

Md. 681 (1908), the right of the Court to order a physical examination of a claimant was recognized. 

The rule itself was 25 specifically upheld in Purdum vs. Lilly, 182 Md. 612 (1944).

Next came the creation of a panel of experts. 

This was done by the Medical and Chirurgical Faculty, The panel is divided into twenty-one specialized sections, such as anesthetics, blood, cancer, cardiology, etc., with three Doctors in each Section. Every effort was made to obtain the most outstanding specialists for each 

Section. 

The Judge who desires an expert calls the Executive Secretary of the Faculty, explains the medical problem involved, and is given the names of the three specialists in the Section which Section corresponds to the injury in the case pending before the Court.

Originally it was anticipated that the name of only one 2 physician would be given the Court, and that in this way the work would be fairly rotated among the members of the Section. 

This is not the case.

All three names are given to the Court and the lawyers in the case are asked to agree upon one name. 

If they cannot agree upon one name, the Court chooses the particular specialist who will be used from the three names given him. 

The question of costs for services rendered by panel members was a matter of some difficulty. 

It was determined that WIN 25. 

For other Maryland cases see: Scheffler vs. Lee, 126 Md. 373 (1915) Brown vs. Hutzler, 152 Md. 39 (1927) -18-

the question of costs should rest in the sound discretion of the Court.

The Court may demand costs in advance, or tax the same after the case is over.

All reports of the Court-appointed experts must be delivered to the Court, and counsel for both sides before trial. 

Reports must be forwarded at such time that each side can study the same and take any action deemed advisable. 

Each side shall have complete access to all material available to the Court-appointed expert.

Three objections were raised to this plan. 

These objections may be summarized as follows: (a) The Plan would be used in small damage suits, causing unnecessary delays and complications in small cases: (b)

The Court would restrict the cross examination of the Court-appointed expert; and (c)

The Court-appointed expert would determine the medical issues thereby withdrawing these issues from the realm of adversary proceedings.

With respect to these objections, it might be said: (1) The Plan will not be used in small cases, , The Plan 1s only for use in the big case in which there is a great difference of opinion between the doctors representing the plaintiff and the doctors representing the defendant. 

It could not be used in small cases since there are not enough doctors to testify in small cases. -19- 17.

(2) The Court-appointed expert is subject to cross examination to the same extent as any other medical witness; (3) It does not destroy the adversary's system. 

It is an improvement in the adversary's system, since it enables the triers of fact to weigh more intelligently the evidence before them. "One of the distinguishing features of Anglo-American law has been its reliance upon the adversary system as the most feasible and reliable method of getting at the truth. 

It is most effective when the triers of the facts possess sufficient knowledge and experience to come to grips with the subject matter of the litigation and make a sensible resolution of the issues,

However, when conflicting testimony is offered as to facts beyond the layman's areas of experience and comprehension, fact finding has all too often degenerated to pure guesswork. 

This distressing frustration of the fact-finding process in litigation occurs most frequently in the presentation of completely incompatible medical testimony in personal injury cases. 526 The Plan has been used sparingly in Baltimore City. 

It is believed the existence of the Plan, and the use of the Plan, has facilitated the settlement of cases. 

The Plan was neither immedjately known or understood. 

However it was available. 

Over the last 

26. Judge Bernard Botein - "The New York Medical Expert Testimony 

Project" University of Detroit Law Journal, May 1956, Vol. 33, page 388. -20-

***...…

few years there has been a gradual increase in its use with a rather definite increase within the last year. 

The Plan has not received the approval of the Maryland State Bar Association or the Baltimore 

City Bar Association.

The Plan was neither immediately known or understood, nor was it readily accepted, but there has been a steady growth in its use over the last few years, and in the last year a definite increase in the acceptance and use of the Plan. 

Further Development in the 

Use of the Plan

The Holbrook Survey, financed by an $80,000 grant from the Haynes Foundation, was a three-year study of the Superior, Municipal and Justices Courts of Los Angeles County. 

This Survey, among other things, recommended: "For a period of at least one year, the Superior Court should experiment with the use of impartial expert medical testimony as a regular program in personal injury cases, and the results 27 thereof should be carefully studied and evaluated. 

Section 1871 of the Code of Civil Procedure of the State of California provides for the appointment of an expert whenever it shall appear that "expert evidence is, or will be, required by the Court".

The Medical Association has supplied the Courts of Los Angeles County with a panel of physicians and of medical specialties to be available to accept appointments by the Court in accordance with the provisions of this Section, but to date this section has not been used to any great extent. 27.

The Holbrook Report 

- Paul Fussel 

August 1951, page 55 -21-

The U. S. District Court for the Eastern District of 

Pennsylvania, on June 2nd, 1958, adopted a rule providing that in personal injury cases where the report or an impartial doctor or doctors would aid in the just determination of the case, such doctor or doctors would be appointed by the Court from a panel to be designated by the Medical Society of the State of Pennsylvania. The constitutionality of this rule was upheld by the Circuit Court and 28 certiorari denied by the Supreme Court.

The matter has been under advisement by the American Bar 

Association for several years.

The Section on Judicial Administra tion of the American Bar Association submitted the following resolution and recommendations for adoption by the Association at the 1957 29 Annual Meeting held in New York and London: 

RESOLVED, That the American Bar Association adopt a national program, to be implemented at the local level, of fostering the creation and employment of panels of impartial medical experts, under court aegis, in the pre-trial consideration and trial of personal injury cases especially in those communities where there is a sufficient nunber of qualified doctors available to constitute a panel;

That the panel be selected by professional bodies on the basis of professional qualifications; 

28. Hankenson v. Van Dusen et al, Vol. 3, L. Ed. (2a), page 628 (Adv. Sheets) 

29. Report of Section of Judicial Administration, July 1957 No. 71 

-22-

That the panel be employed at the pre-trial and trial 

TOT 

1 stage of such cases;

The Committee's reasons for making these recommendations, among others, were as follows: "The medical phase of personal injury litigation particularly calls for court attention and remedial action, not only because of the large part of court time absorbed in the medical inquiry, but also because under prevailing methods of taking medical proof courts are not receiving sufficiently reliable reports of the medical facts.

While the weighing and determination of the facts bearing on liability can satisfactorily be made by lay judgments on the basis of the common experience of a jury, the determination of medical facts calls for special knowledge which a jury does not possess.

The jury is dependent on the word of experts in this scientific realm. 

That a jury does not receive altogether trustworthy information and reliable guidance under the purely adversary method of presenting medical evidence through partisan experts is abundantly clear. . Unfortunately, we have developed a fashion of employing doctors as well as lawyers as advocates, and the doctors retained as experts for the respective sides may, albeit unconsciously, readily become partisans.

Because of the scientific nature of medical evidence the jury is more often confused than enlightened by such a presentation of that evidence, and is left without adequate insight to determine the facts or pass upon the credibility of the contesting experts, Experts are frequently sought and retained -23-

more for a cultivated art of testifying than for basic competence and essential integrity.'

The Committee found that the use of the Court-appointed medical experts has the following wholesome and beneficial results: "1.

It improves the process of finding the medical facts, vastly increasing the likelihood of reaching the right result. 2.

It serves to relieve court congestion by bringing about the settlement of many cases which would otherwise have to be tried and which by their nature would entail lengthy trials. 3.

It has a prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of medical testimony in court. 4. .

The modest cost involved in the payment of independent experts is a positive economy in effecting a large saving in court operations."

The recommendations of the Committee were duly approved by the House of Delegates at the Annual Meeting of the Association 30 in London in July of 1957. 

Opposition to the Plan

Opposition to the Plan seems to stem primarily from Plaintiffs! 31 attorneys. This opposition is clearly set forth by Mr. Emile Z.

30. 31.

American Bar Association Summary of Action taken by House of 

Delegates, page 13. 

20 NAAC Law Journal, pages 25-31; Paper by Elwood S. Levy before 

Regional Medicolegal meeting sponsored by A.M.A, at Washington, D.C., March 21st, 1959

TT -24-

33 Berman and Mr. Harry Gair in a panel discussion conducted by the Insurance Section of the American Bar Association, and by 34 Mr.

Paul Berman in a discussion on this subject at the 27th 

Judicial Conference of the Fourth Circuit at Asheville on July 8th, 1957.

This opposition is primarily based on two premises (a) That a trial is an adversary proceeding; that a courtappointed expert removes the trial from adversary proceedings into the realm of an administrative proceeding determined by the testimony HIN of Court-appointed specialists; "that it is the abolition of jury 35 trials in personal injury litigation"; and (b) That medicine is not an exact science; that a courtappointed doctor can be as wrong as any other doctor; that this is particularly true in certain fields of medicine wherein there is a strong diversity of opinion among the doctors themselves. 

It should be noted that Mr. Emile Berman, and, to some limited extent, Mr. Gair, in the Panel discussion before the 

Insurance Section, concede the fact that the use of a courtappointed expert is helpful in pre-trial proceedings and may be helpful in the final disposition and settlement of cases. 

Mr. Gair states 32. 33.

Proceedings Insurance Section - American Bar Association 

1956, page 293

Proceedings Insurance Section - American Bar Association 

1956, page 299 

Paper delivered by Mr. Paul Berman Daily Record June 22, 1956 

Paper delivered by Paul Berman The Daily Record, June 22, 1957 34. 35. -25-

"I agree with Mr. Berman that it has its place as part of a pre-trial procedure in mediating between antagonistic claimants. 

The opinion of a third impartial specialist would be a powerful ,,36 adjunct toward compromising the conflict, Mr. Berman would seem to indicate that the use and value of a Court-appointed expert varies with the medical problem involved. 

Some medical problems may be subject to greater certainty in medical findings and diagnosis than others. 

The greater the certainty in medical findings, the greater the value of Court-appointed experts the less the certainty in medical findings, the less the value of the 

Court-appointed expert. "I believe it justifiable to state that the problem of x-ray interpretation may readily yield in the quest for certainty by reason of the development of roentgenology as a specialty. 

Through proven techniques and the fairly concise field that it occupies, it is probable that orthopedic problems, being in a large measure mechanic, may readily lend themselves to a fair and accurate diagnosis and evaluation from a highly competent and disinterested expert. 

But then here the legitimate area of disagreement may well be foreseen. !! 37

Both feel strongly that medical experts should not be appointed by the Court in a case in which there is a recognized 

36. Proceedings American Bar Association, 1957, page 299 

37. Proceedings Insurance Section, American Bar Association, 

1952, page 295 -26-

division of medical opinion. 

Mr. Berman: "I think medicine itself recognizes that there are fields of medical thinking where there are recognized divisions of opinion.

It is my view that minimally the medical societies should delineate and define the area of conflicting schools of medical thought.

In these controversial fields of medical thinking no case should be sent to the panel for any purpose, 38 whether pre-trial or trial.

In discussing this phase of the matter in Impartial 

Medical Testimony, a Report by a Special Committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, at page 33 it is stated: "This point of view overlooks several important facts. First, while it is true that there are many areas of medicine where the knowledge of doctors is sketchy and uncertain, there are also many areas of certainty where competent doctors do not disagree.

If the diagnosis of an injury is certain, that fact should be made known to the jury. 

If it is uncertain and doubtful in view of the limitations of medical knowledge, that fact is more likely to be admitted on the witness stand by a doctor who is truly expert and truly impartial than by a partisan doctor." 

38. Proceedings Section of Insurance Law, 1956, page 298 

IT :-27-

In considering this opposition to the plan, it should be noted that Discovery Proceedings and right to take Depositions with practically no limit on the scope of the inquiry also remove a case to some extent from an adversary proceeding. 

The Plan is essentially a further extension of Discovery Proceedings. 

The use of a Court-appointed expert does not remove the proceedings from adversary proceedings but adds an additional skill or aid to the Court in its fact-finding function in adversary proceedings.

It enables the Court and the jury to more accurately evaluate the facts in these proceedings. It is believed the Plan should at least be tried. If it proves of no value, it can be dropped. 

If it needs amendment, amendment can be made.

If it proves of real value, than an 1 addition has been made to the trial of the hard personal injury 1 cases.

IMPARTIAL MEDICIAL TESTIMONY A NEW HORIZON 

"Justice is the great interest of man on earth.” Daniel Webster

INTRODUCTION. THE PROBLEM 

In the past decade a serious problein of our present court system has grown to such extent as to alarm inany judges and cause much apprehension to attorneys on both sides of the adversary process. This problem is the spiralling number of civil cases over seventy-five per cent of which are comprised of personal injury suits in both federal and state courts.' 

The federal district courts today have the highest backlog of cases in history. Only seven districts in the country were able to keep the period between filing and trial as low as six months during the last year. In the 

Fastern District of New York the delay in getting a civil case to trial is almost four years. Eighteen districts, all cast of the Mississippi, except for the District of Colorado, report a median of over fifteen months from filing to trial. Filings of new actions during fiscal 1958 were highest in the 

Southern District of New York and the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, loth of which showed an increase over 1957.8 

The backlog has produced much debate and niany suggestions, but little has heen done in the way of both practicable and concrete solutions. 

Since the majority of the civil cases are personal injury cases a major component of the trial is medical testimony as to the extent of the injuries. 

Plaintiff and defendant each have a doctor of their own choice to present the plaintiff's injuries to the jury. These paid partisans manage to find the extent of plaintiff's injuries in dispute. Otherwise, there might be no trial, since this determines the issije of fact that goes to the jury. Are they to blame even pinly for congestion or are niost areas of medicine open to disagreement among reliable physicians ? The problem encountered in the first 

Ivecause of the present system of use of expxert witnesses will now be considered.

THE PASTA CONFLICT 

The use by both plaintill and defendant in a personal injury case of each one's expert witness it irial has produced a type of trial abhorred by both the legal and medical professions. Lawyers despair of obtaining coinpetent doctors to testify and neclical nen who have reputations to maintain

are adverse to having their ability belittled by the scathing cross-examination of the opposition's attorney. For this very reason many very competent doctors refuse to testify unless subpoenaed, and consequently their place is taken by the school of "professionally polished testifiers"-available for a price with testimony more distorted than truthful. They arc looked upon with contempt by the lawyers who use them and reccive nothing but disdain from their medical colleagues. This is not meant to cast aspersion at the well-known and recognized expert who is called upon to testify in many trials simply because of his expertise. But even here some of these experts get the reputation for being either plaintiffs' or defendants' doctors. 

Since it is never certain as to the exact time a doctor's testinrony will be needed at trial and since he may have to spend one or more days of valuable tiine sitting in the court waiting to be called (though many courts put a doctor on the stand when he arrives), hardship often results. The fact that he has already planned the next day's activities and he may have desperate cases under his care which demand his presence causes many doctors to eschew the whole trial system. As mentioned previously many will only come when subpoenaed. As to the treatment of the doctor on the stand the succinct statement of Dr. J. A. Gannon is well-worth quoting. 

The opposing counsel now takes over and performs his duty to his client, which is to minimize the effect of the doctor's direct testimony. 

Too often the voice and manner of opposing counsel indicate contempt for the witness as a person. Opposing counsel frequently infers incompetence, carelessness, untruthfulness and bias, and leaves the doctor frustrated and outraged, with the condition made worse by court decorum, which prevents expression of his feelings. Is it any cause for wonder that the average doctor is unwilling to appear in court and be subjected to such an inquisition? 

Within recent years the courts have attempted a partial solution to the volume and time consumption aspects of cases. The federal courts and many state courts have instituted the procedure of pre-trial conferences. 

Although the main purpose of the conference is to simplify the issues and liniit the number of expert witnesses, it often led to settlement when facts were brought out concerning the weakness or strength of the plaintiff's case. However, when there was a dispute (which is the usual case) as to the extent of the plaintiff's injuries, the pre-trial conference did not obviate the demand for jury trial of the fact issues, and thus no real diminution of civil cases resulted. 

A solution 10 the problem that was suggested by some writers with amazing furcsight, as far back as the carly thirties when automobiles were still in their infancy, was compensation for automobile accident victims

ing in the presentation of meclical proof in judicial proceedings, the judges of the Supreme Court of New York devised a plan as a partial solution of the problem. 

Working in conjunction with Professor Jelmar Karlen 10 and Dr.

Irving S. Wright 11 the New York and Bronx County Bar and Medical 

Associations cooperated with the judges to implement the Impartial Medical Testimony Project. With grants from the Ford and Sloan foundations to finance the two-year trial run the Project went into effect on December 1, 1952 and the cut-off date for statistical analysis was December 1, 1954. 

The basic idea of the Project is that a group of doctors recommended by the Medical Association for their ability in sprecific fields constituite the “Medical Panel". The liaison between the panel and the courts is the 

Medical Report Office which is in the Supreme Court building. Whenever at the pre-irial conference there is a dispute all the nature of the injuries suffered by the plaintiff, the judge can order an impartial examination. 

Consent of the lawyers is not necessary, and no lawyer registered any strong objections--some have even requested the use of an impartial. 

The judge does not know the name of the doctor available only the specialties. The judge makes a memorandum for the plaintiff's lawyer who goes to the Medical Report Office. There he receives instructions as to procedure and the examination is set up. The next doctor in line is called up. The attorneys are not allowed to request any certain doctor and are discouraged from dealing directly with him. All inquiries are to go through the Medical Report Office. In the New York Project the doctor's lces were paid by the court out of the funds granted for the Project."? 

After the examination another conference is held. If the case is not settled either the judge or in.y party may call the doctor to testify and his manner of being employer is disclosed to the jury. If he is called by one side the other side may cross-examine him. 

The special committee of the Bar Association of New York City which wrote the report on the l’rvject concluded that the Project has proved successful. It listed as accomplishments: 

1. The Project has improved the process of finding medical facts in litigated cases. 

2. It has helped relieve court congestion. 

3. It has had a wllolesome prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of inedical testimony in court. 

4. It has provexi that the modest expenditure involved effects a larger saving and economy in court operations.

197 5. It hins pointed the way to better diagnosis in the field of traumatic niedicine. Unlike the others listed above, this accomplishment is an unexpected dividend, which was not in contemplation wlien the Project was initiated. 13 ! ! The most tangible result of the use of impartial medical testimony which can be statistically expressed is the settlement experience. Approximately 575 cases have gone through the New York referral procedure. 

Settlements at a pre-trial stage, attributable to the panel procedure, were made in 330 cases. Settlements during trial were made in another 62 cases while 68 cases were tried to completion. There remained, at the time of printing the report, 115 cases awaiting trial or other disposition. The writers of the report assumed that at least half of those cases would still be settled, thus bringing the total number settled to 75% of the number of 

Cases referred.

Another beneficial result was the saving in court time and expense. 

The cost of the incdical experts' fees for the two years was $20,383.35. 

Since cach day of trial in New York costs the taxpayers $750.00 and the average trial lasts three days thus costing $2250.00, the Project saved substantially more than its cost. In fact it saved more than ten times the fees of the experts."

There has of course been criticism of the project by the so-called traditionalists of the law. One of the most vehement opponents of the idea is Thonias F. Lambert, Jr.--the editor of the NACCA Law Journal.16 In editorials eulogizing the jury arid condemning the impartial medical expert he puts forth the doctrine that incertainty is inevitable in many areas of traumatic medicine. 16 No one disputes this, but is it any reason to sit back and criticize atiempts at improvement when the critic himself suggests no better solution. Mr. Lamlert states that an expert has been defined as "a inan who avoids small errors as he sweeps on to the grand fallacy" or "a snan seldom in doubt, frequently in error." Again, one can point out that these Wildian epigrans can be overdone when no better solution has been proposed. Mr. Lambert concludes that was long as there remain substantial differences of medical opinion, there is no alternative to entrusting them to a jury." 17

However, the fact that those who worked with the project, studied its results and found them better than anticipated lave continued its use and expanded it is commendation enough. Up until 1958 the New York Supreme Court and the Supreme Bench of Baltimore City were the only two instances of the use of impartial experts. 18 The fouleral courts never had the impartial expert but used the procedure under Rule 35 of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure lu which provided that for good cause shown the court may order a physical exanination where the pliysical condition of a party is in controversy. Of course, if upun defendant's request the plaintiff was examined, it was by the defendant's selected physician. Then on June 2, 1958 the judges of the 

Federal District Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania (located in Philadelphia), pursuant to Rule 8320 and supplementing Rule 35, promulgated a rule providing for medical examinations in personal injury cases on the court's own motion before triint. 

Adoption of the rule was recommended by a committee of the Pemusylvania State Bar Association after consultation with representatives of the Pennsylvania Medical Society. This action was in litle with a progrant of the American Bar Association, approved in the summer of 1957 on the basis of a report by the Section on Judicial Administration, 21 

The rule is as follows: 

A. In any personal injury case in which prior to the trial thereof, a judge shall be of the opinion that an examination of the injured personi and a report thereon by an impartial incdical expert or experts would be of material aid to the just determination of the case, he nay. after consultation with counsel for the respective parties and after giv ing counsel a hearing, if such licaring be requested by either counsel, order such examination and report. The examination will be made by a inember or members of a panel of examining physicians designated for their particuar qualifications by the Medical Society of the State of Pennsylvania after consultation with a committee appointed by the 18. B. The compensation of the expert or experts shall be fixed by the judge ordering the examination and shall be paid equally by each party to the litigation unless such judge, in his discretion, orders pay. ment to be made otherwise in order to meet special factual situations.

The rule was invoked in October, 1958 lvy District Court Judge Van Dusen in a case involving injuries sustained by a workmans working as a mail handler at the 30th Street Station of the Pennsylvania Railroad in Philadelphia.26 Plaintiff's doctor diagnosed his injuries as a fracture of the first sacral segment of the spine plus a ruptured invertebral disc in addition lo a heart attack sustained some time after the accident. The last two injuries are in dispute because of defendant's doctor's diagnosis. The plaintiff's doctor claims the heart attack is trauniatic and due to the injury. 

The defendant's doctor disputes this saying there was a latent heart condition prior to the accident. 

The plaintiff was ordered by the court to submit to an examination by a doctor selected by the court. The attorney for the plaintiff filed a petition for a writ of mandamus and prohibition in the Third Circuit Court of Appeals.24 The petition was denied by the Third Circuit without a hearing. 25

The attorney for the plaintiff announced his intention of petitioning to the Supreme Court of the United States for a writ of certiorari. 

In his pxetition to the Third Circuit the plaintiff averted that the case in its medical aspects involved no more than simple, disputed factual issues and that reference of these issues to "so-called" impartial medical exaininers, the introduction into evidence of their findings at trial and the undue weight that a jury will "undontedly give to such findings, will result in the denial of the right to trial by jury as guaranteed by the Seventh Amendment. He argued thal the local rule is illegal and unconstitutional in that the judge compels a litigant to pay compensation to doctors without due process of law in violation of lhe Filih Amendment. 28

Of course, these are the standard arguments given by those who claim the impartial will usurp the jury's role. They point to this rule as an innovation in trial procedure whereas the basis for the rule was pronounced nearly fifteen years ago by the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania in the case of Galante z. H'est Penn Pouver Company.27 Pursuant to Rule 212 of the 

Pennsylvania Rules of Civil Procedure 2' the Conimon Pleas Court of 

Allegheny County adopted pre-trial procedure rules providing that the parties shall "make preparations to agree, if possible, on the number of expert witnesses to be called and for the appointment by the court of an impartial expert nominated by the court with counsels' approval." 29 The expenses of this impartial were to be borne by both sides, or taxed as part of the costs by agrer'nent of counsel. In the Galante case a doctor who had been agreed upon testified upon defendant's request to the judge. 

However, the defendant first rested his case, and then the court called the doctor to the stand. Thereupon, the plaintiff objected that this was prejudicial to him as he felt the doctor should be interrogated by the defendant as his owni witness, The court overruled the objection since both counsel had agreed to the doctor's examination. The doctor testified and both counsel cross-examined him. The Supreine Court said it was not prejudicial thereby upholding the Allegheny County rule. In effect the doctor had been an impartial as far as the jury was concerned because he was not called as a witness for either side. 

The rule promulgated by the Federal District Court in Philadelphia is different from the New York practice in that the New York court pays the impartial's fee. This fact may be the reason why the New York rule was never attacked as being unconstitutional whereals a main argunioni against the Philadelphia rule is that the costs are to be paid equally by the parties or in some other manner in the judge's discretion. 

It might be argued that if the costs were orrlered to be paid by one of the parties, for example by the plaintiff if he got a verdict, then the witness was not an impartial witness but a party-pxid witness and therefore partisan. 

The rule has been criticized for usurping the jury's function in a field where honest medical men can diller and therefore it is argued that the jury should be the final arbiter. However, asking an untrained, lay jury to determine which of inn puiscel, polished and highly qurid expert testifiers is correct is like asking an accused criminal 10 swallow a corsued in order to determine his guilt or innocence 30 

27. 3.19 Pa. 616, 37 A.Zi 548 (1944). 

28. Rule 212. -- Pre-Trial Confcrcne... (1) such other miltiers as may aid in the disposition of the acrion ) Cirl.wite v West Per l'uwer Comp.014, slepota nole 27, at 619; 37 A.2: at 546). 

30. Jun Soroll den - od inosol or peruricion. It was a yuries of ordeal performed by eating a priezieni borvitit lille fallseed inte i iscrdu, bie Wits pruncounced innocent; birl, it it seek ju lui turnil it litis considered as pont of hij wilt. See Blick's

1959) 

Advocates of a rule for impartial medical testimony have proposed three alternative rules. One is for the judge to appoint a doctor upon agreement of both counsel who would also split the fee, with counsel only agreeing to the use of his report at a pre-trial conference in an attempt at settlement. 

The second alternative is for the testimony of this doctor to be available for use by either side with no disclosure to the jury as to how he was selected. 

The implication to the jury would be that he is an extra witness for the side which his testimony favors. The third alternative method is for the jury to be given full disclosure as to the method of selection when the impartial testifies at trial. This is the rule adopted by the Eastern District 

Court of Pennsylvania. Sonie scholars in the field believe that the second alternative would be better in order to avoid garbing the witness with a "cloak of infallibility."

However, the Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania has made its choice and the rule is in operation. Only the United States Supreme 

Court can strike it down now. Until it acts, other district courts will be anxiously awaiting the decision.

CONCLUSION-A Hore. 

If the Supreme Court of the United States upholds the rule as promulgated in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, there is no doubt that other district courts will follow suit and prescribe similar rules. If the Supreme 

Court strikes down the rule, it is possible that some alternative rule may be proposed such as in New York where the court pays the impartials' fees. 

Nevertheless, regardless of the Supreme Court's decision, it must be remembered that this is only a partial solution and a temporary one at best. 

The problem of personal injury due to the automobile will looni larger as our economy grows and highways reach the point of saturation. 

Perhaps the time has come to think in terms of automobile accident compensation. Even with this in mind as a future possibility it would seem that the use of impartial medical testimony offers a partial but substantial solution. The results of the test Project are heartening. Lethargy and a tenacious clinging to the past will only make the problem worse. The time is ripe for a bold step forward and never were Justice Holmes' words more appropriate-"our idcal may be repose, but our destiny is effort." 

Wilina I. Baran
