Nov. 1957

EDITORIAL Impartial Medical Testimony: A New Audit 

"Justice is the great interest of man on earth" - so said the Titan, Webster, in words which will command ready assent. Over a century ago, Lord Brougham eloquently summarized the aims to be achieved by the administration of justice: "It was the proud boast of Augustus ... that he found Rome of brick, and left it of marble; . . But how much nobler would be the Sovereign's boast when he shall have it to say that he found law dear, and left it cheap, found it a sealed book — left it a living letter; found it the patrimony of the rich — left it the inheritance of the poor; found it the two-edged sword of craft and oppression - left it the staff of honesty and the shield of innocence .” As every schoolboy knows, justice delayed is justice denied. And the notorious glut of personal injury cases in several metropolitan areas, the attendant bottleneck of automobile accident cases, and general calendar congestion must be reduced unless the quest for justice, saluted and glorified in the above quotation, is to become a cynical mockery, sagging insincerity, and a "catching of the wind in nets."

In recent years, there has been a suitcaseful of suggestions for short-circuiting calendar congestion, expediting the judicial process, and reducing delay in personal injury cases. Some of these suggestions are sensible and beneficial, such as increasing the number of judges to try personal injury cases, liberalizing discovery practice, expanding resort to pretrial of such cases, substituting the superior doctrine of comparative negligence for the unjust and feral contributory negligence rule. Others have been misconceived, illstarred and misbegotten, such as scuttling the jury system in civil cases and substituting automobile compensation boards, with frozen, inadequate, inflation-gutted schedules of awards. Sce 17 NACCA (251

VOL. 20 NACCA LAW JOURNAL 

Law Journal 23-24. Certain of these suggestions have been translated into the structure of the trial process; others have not got off the drawing boards.

WT7 

The Impartial Medical Panel 

Recently, a pilot project, involving the use of a so-called impartial medical panel, was instituted in certain courts of New York 

City for a 2-year period from December 1, 1952, to December 1, 1954. The basic idea of the New York experiment, since extended to the principal trial courts in New York and Bronx Counties, was to make available to the courts, in cases involving issuable facts in the field of traumatic medicine, panels of “neutral, outstanding physicians in the various specialized branches of medicine." These court-sponsored, "impartial” medical experts make medical examinations of plaintiffs in personal injury cases, report their findings and, where necessary, testify in cases in which the medical aspects were significant and controversial. 

In 1956 Macmillan Company published Impartial Medical Testimony, a report by a special committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, giving an account of the Medical Expert Testimony Project as put into effect in New York City. 

The Report, containing about 40 pages of text, details the procedure used in establishing the project, the basis of its financing, the manner in which the plan operates, and the results claimed for the project in its 2 years of operation. Specifically, the Report aims to: answer such questions as: How the "impartial" experts are chosen and made available; how the “neutral” specialist for a particular. case is selected; what use is made of the reports of the court's experts; and the asserted accomplishments of the project in its 2 years of experimental use.

Let it be said at once that the objectives of the project are irreproachable. No one can gainsay the desirability of expediting the judicial process in the personal injury field and securing for courts and juries a surer means of access to "truthful medical testimony" where required.

The Report is concise, readable and, within limits, complete and objective. It deserves to be studied by both sides of the trial bar and by appropriate committees of every bar association genuinely interested in the improvement of the administration of justice.

EDITORIAL 

27 

-  Shortcomings of the Plan 

The proponents of the impartial medical panel seek to avoid the "presentation of widely conflicting medical opinion evidence" (p. 3). They refer to discrepancies in medical claims as "not only disturbing to doctors and lawyers, but [they) also confuse and disgust laymen.” The expert medical witness, hired by contending litigants in current practice, is in effect an expert advocate instead of an expert analyst and expositor. Such hired expert medical testimony, it is argued, is notoriously unreliable. The injection of a court-appointed expert, we are assured, will largely do away with this unedifying battle of the experts. Champions of the plan also draw a bead on corrupted testimony given by a small coterie of debased doctors. “Both the medical and legal professions, composed predominantly of honorable and dedicated men and women, are undeservedly stigmatized by a small outlaw fringe of brazenly unscrupulous practitioners and by a somewhat larger group of lawyers who have not disciplined themselves to conform to the highest ideals of their profession." 

Upon careful reflection, we submit that a clear-eyed study of both the apparent and subsurface shortcoinings of the impartial medical plan as applied in New York City must lead to its rejection. 1 The Illusory Quest for Medical Certainly In many vital areas of traumatic medicine, uncertainty, if dismaying, is also inevitable. While we all affirm the "efficacy of effort," there are two things it does not do much good to get feverish about, the impossible and the inevitable. So long as medical diagnosis and therapy lie in the battleground of controversy, it is irrational and a disservice to the cause of justice to seek to conceal or suppress such controversy. Let it be remembered: our target in personal injury as in all litigation is truth - not certainty. As the great Holmes unerringly put it, "Certitude is not a proof of certainty. I have been cocksure about many things that were not so.' 

An expert has been relevantly clefined as "il man who voids small errors as lie sweeps on to the grand fall:cc:y." He has also been acutely described as a man "sellom in doubt, frequently in error." 

It was Jessel, Master of the Rolls, himself no mean expert in the field of equity, who nilmitted with it candor not vouchsafed by the

VOL. 20 

NACCA LAW JOURNAL 

authors of the Report, "I may be wrong; I frequently am, but I seldom doubt.” In the medical schools, medical libraries, hospitals, clinics, in the pages of the American Medical Association's Journal, vigorous controversy, widespread and clamorous disagreement among competent doctors, are exhibited and even cultivated. Indeed, such intramural disputes among medical specialists as to debatable issues are as old as the practice of the healing arts. Should we try to still such medical controversy in the courtroom by giving, in effect, ultimate adjudicative authority on the medical issues to an “impartial” board of doctors? Is not the time-tested, democratic, and received common-law method of pitting doctor against doctor, theory against theory, and idea against idea, hammering out the truth by the adversary system, superior to using court-sponsored medical experts, wearing "invisible judicial robes," to allay debate and stifle controversy?

Current Areas of Medical Uncertainty 

Not only may competent lawyers differ from competent doctors on such issues as the effect of trauma on cancer, multiple sclerosis, or arthritis, but it is well known that able doctors energetically disagree among themselves on such controversial questions. 

Put a doctor holding one view on the impartial medical panel and he is thereby inescapably cloaked with an actual if invisible aura of infallibility. As that unstinting, eminent and unimpeachable professional, Harry A. Gair, has summed it up, “Unless trial by jury is to be abolished, it is unfair to pin a badge of honor on one witness as having the full confidence of the court, and withoiding it from another, patently. labelling him as undeserving of confidence.”

It is a false equation to assert that impartiality equals truth. In 

1491 the world was flat. Christopher Columbus, a partisan, insisted it was round. There is the risk of a kind of donatured impartiality in which the impartial expert favors truth no more than he does error. So it goes: the heresy of one generation is the coinmonplace of the next. The zealot of one year is the devotee of truth in the next. The so-called impartial expert, while on the stand, wearing his badge of court-sponsored distinction, may in fact mount a steed for his own medical ism, and by parading it on the White Horse of his official status, so overpower the jurors and tip the probative scales that they accept his fiat for fact. And the expert's ism of today may perhaps become the wasm of tomorrow.

EDITORIAL_ 

29 The court's expert might be certain, alright, only he might also be certainly wrong, as neither expertise nor impartiality can guarantee final truth in diagnosis, etiology or prognosis. 

If it be asked why the errors of the "impartial" expert cannot be uncloaked by exacting cross-examination, permissible under the plan, surely Harry Gair, with customary bull's-eye delivery, has given the answer, “Buttressed as the witness is by the Court's sanctions, selected to give the best possible truth, which is to say, to resist the ignorance or errors of partisan doctors, of what avail, cross-examination?” And if a premise of the plan is the jury's inability to decide the existence or degree of physical injury or medical causation, how do they acquire prerogative under the plan to disbelieve or repudiate the court's anointed expert? Is not the plan in fact calculated to subvert and transform the role of the people's jury into a rubber stamp for the "impartial” expert's monistic findings? These, in an area of medical uncertainty, may be nothing more than polemic constructions and untenable hypotheses whose systematic, and now oracular, defense has become the expert's life mission.

The mutability of medical opinions and the constantly expanding area of disagreement on the part of medical experts may be easily illustrated by reference to such major areas of dispute as fractures (a line in an X-ray may mean a fracture to one physician, an artefact to a second, and a normal structure to a third), disc injuries of the spine without demonstrable X-ray evidence, head injuries, the effect of effort and stress on coronary thrombosis, post-concussion syndrome, dissipation or triggering of cancer by trauma, traumatic neurosis, lower-back injuries, and other areas where medical opinion is divided into dissentient schools. In all these fields, conflicting medical opinions divide competent doctors into two (or more) schools of thought. In the medical schools, journals and institutes, professional biases, one way or the other, are commonly expressed. 

The greatest experts take sides on these questions. If so, have they not become partisans, if not to plaintiff or defendant, then to a school of thought, a point of view? If a partisan to the one or other school of thought is on the so-called "impartial” panel, he brings his opinions with him: the wine must taste of its own grapes.

And what of the school of thought not represented on the "impartial” panel, thereby labeled as less worthy of credence, demoted into darkness while the panel member is elevated to the light, 1 ! 1

VOL, 20 NACCA LAW JOURNAL 

wrapped in the accolade of the court's confidence? Should we further entangle the Gordian knot by getting an "impartial" ex- Lert from each of the warring schools of thought? Will the court with double experts be engaged in duplicity? Or would we then be multiplying the confusion the plan was designed to attenuate -disordering the trial and possibly deranging the triers with a jumble which would "confuse a convocation of casuists"?

The answer must surely be, with Mr. Gair, that the jury would still have to choose between them, which is what they have been doing since "1066 and all that." The truth frequently lies at the bottom of the well, or is to be found in the sea of controversy, not in the impartial expert's dipper. The jury should have access, through the windows of its mind, to that uncharted sea of controversy, with its ebb and flow of contrary opinions, its undertows and cross eddies of minority views, and not be confined to gazing. upon the narrow estuary of "official truth” in which the "impartial” expert, at the helm of the court's flagship, flies his pennant of proficiency. As Holmes said, our ideal may be repose, but our destiny is effort.

Conclusion 

As long as there remain substantial differences of medical opinion in important areas of traumatic medicine, with respect to which competent doctors disagree, there is no viable alternative to entrusting such debatable issues to the common sense and enlightened conscience of the American jury. The jury should be provided with all the data that expert testimony, adduced by the parties under the tested adversary system, can give. It should have the benefit of careful instructions as to the things it is to consider. But it should not be deflected or disconcerted or disoriented by the injection into the proofs of the “official” truth of court-sponsored, state-anointed "impartial” experts. Truth remains, what it has always been under our received Anglo-American jury system, the power of opposing contentions to secure reasoned probative acceptance in the evidentiary competition of the courtroom. The jury's judgment should not be put in leash to the impartial expert's opinions nor fettered to the side favored by such aura of arrogated authority. Any cure which is worse than the ailment should be received with exacting scrutiny and embraced with the proper degree of detachment. The Impartial Medical Panel, with due

EDITORIAL 

31 

respect for its admirable motivation, is too much like throwing the baby out with the bathwater, and should be studied with archscepticism. 

THOMAS F. LAMBERT, JR., Edilor-in-Chief.

* By Elwoop S. LEVY 

of thc Philadelphia Bar ?

All persons having a part in judicial administration are today justifiably concerned with the modern massive problemi of congestion in the courts. Throughout the nation, particularly in metropolitan arcas, backlog is the rule rather than the exception. Approximately 75 to 80 per cent of all civil matters awaiting trial are personal injury cascs, many of which involve the taking of medical testimony and the resolution of differences in medical opinion. It is thus to the core of court operations that proposals for the use of "impartial nicdical testimony" are directed.' 

Advocates of the plan, first used in New York County beginning in 1952,9 warmed by the ardor of reformi and inspired by the purposes which motivate it, commend it 10 uis now as the panacca for many of our court-system ills. As the courts, laboring to digest huge volumes of litigation, show signs of distress, the proponents of the plan prescribe it as a kind of wonder-drug, a new and great catharsis, sweet-tasting, palatable meclicini', which can and will cleanse the system, eliminate all symptoms of distress and restore the body judicial at the trial level to wholesome, licalthy ſunction. To what extent il aids or harms the patient justilies critical study. 

Under the leadership of Justice David W. Puck, or New

York's Supreme Court, liirst Department, the plan was employeul experimentally from 195.2 10 1954 in the trial courts of New York and Brona (utics. In these two counties its lise has continues to date as it part of the regular court proccclure. 2 *A paper delivered March 21, 1952 itt Washington, D.C. ät

1. Report, Impartiid Merlical Testimony," Special Committee of the 

Associations of the Itar of the City of New York, Macmillan, 1956, p. 3. !!! 15. 3-18

LITLIRTLIL. NI:111:11. TESTIMONY 

349 Brielly, the plan provilles for tlic naming by medical societies of panels of impartial, well-qualifica, medical experts in various specialties, who have not been identified either with the plaintill's or defendant's side of personal injury litigation. Whiere a sharp sedical dispute emerges at pretrial conference the presiding judge may, without the consent of the parties, refer the case to onc or more impartial experts selected by rotation from the lists. After reviewing all medical records and conducting one or more examinations, the expert lodges his report with the medical report office, which in turn forwards copies to both counsel and to the judge. The impartial expert may thereafter be called to testify by either side or by the court. He is paid out of funds now included in the court's budget. 

In 1956 the New York program was endorsed by the 

Section of Judicial Administration of the American Bar 

Association. The House of Delegates of the A.B.A. then adopted with approval the report of the Section on Judicial Administration. In June of 1958 a similar program was established in the United States District Court for the 

Eastern District of Pennsylvania sitting in Philadelphia."

An analogous plan had previously been announced in 1955 in Baltimore under the aegis of Emory H. Niles, Chief Judge of the Supreme Bench of that city. 

But all is not sweetness and light. Many attorneys and indeed judges, just as genuinely concerned with improvement in the administration of justice as the advocates of the plan, are sharply opposed to it and are sincere in their conclusion that it imports into our present trial system elements of such danger to trial by jury as to outweigh any of its claimed advantages.

To date none of the adjoining counties or departments in New York have adopted the plan in use in New York and 

3. Peck, "Impartial Medical Testimony," 22 F.R.D. 21, 26 (1958). 

4. 1957 Annual Report of the American Bar Association, pp. 184-185. 

5. Local Rule of June 2, 1958, U.S.D.C., E.D.Pa.

Bronx Counties. Both the Maryland State Bar Association and the Baltimore City Bar Association in 1955 disapproved any extension of that state's then existing practice to include the so-called "impartial medical" and the "Baltimore Plan" has yet to win any wide use even in that city. On March 5, 1959, the Medico-Legal Committee of the Philadelphia Bar 

Association, following prolonged study, recommended that that Association disapprove any use of the plan in the state courts. I.

At the very outset, a critical defect in the plan for impartial medical testimony arises from the fact that it presupposes that medicine is an exact science. This is fundamentally erroneous. From the very nature of the subject, medical opinions cannot be formulated with absolute mathematical certainty. Despite the enormous advances made by medicine in recent years, there remain vast areas, including the sequelae of trauma, which are today undergoing constant study, experiment and thoughtful revision. The process of learning and of change is steady and progressive. What was medically right before may be wrong now, and what is correct now may be erroneous in years to come. Yet it is in this large field of medicine, with all of its unknowns, with all of its unfolding yet tentative experimental knowledge, that the impartial expert will have his trial impact.

No fair-minded man can deny that in a substantial number of individual cases equally competent and equally honest physicians can and do disagree. It does not at all follow that differing opinions are due to bias, partiality or corruption, yet this sinister and unfortunate suggestion is implicit in the plan.

In the course of a syniposium on “Thic Doctor in Court- Expert Medical Testimony' conducted by the Medical and 

Chirurgical Faculty of Maryland, Dr. George 0. Eaton, an 

6. Vol. 60, Maryland State Bar Association Proceedings, pp. 35-75, 1955. .

1.11PIRTlill, Altillltill, TESTIMONY 

351 eminent surgeon of the City of Baltimore, in discussing "The l'iewpoint of the Traumatic Surgeon," said:? "Criven osilctly the same facts, cycl conscientious experts can and will disagree with regard to their significance and potentialities, so that ille're disagreement lufwech experts cloes not imply dishonesty or incompetence on the part of one or both of them." ils stated by Dr. Hlubert Winston Smith :8 "Let it he said that all people competent to speak recognize that medicine is not illl exact science, in its totality, but a mixture of science and art. There is much room ſor honest dillcrence of opinion and for varying clinical judgments on open subjects. No one would hold all witnesses to subscribc to a single view. in cases where good men may differ."

The report of the Special Committee recognizes not only that there may be and frequently are areas in which physicians of unquestioned integrity and competency may reach differing conclusions on the same facts," but further agrees that there are many problems on which meilical knowledge does not provide a clear-cut solution.10 

It seems obvious that in this area to label a doctor as impartial does not for the nioment mean that he is right. 

Thus where honest and qualified experts disagree, either the plaintiff's or the defendant's doctor may be right and the "impartial” expert wrong.

This inherent difficulty is further compounded by the existence in significant fields of so-called "schools of thought.” This does not involve opinion which may vary from case to case, but rather involves steadily held conceptions or preconceptions, cach of which is usually supported by impressive authority. Which way the issue is resolved thus depends upon the chance selection of the next expert by 

7. 13 Maryland Law Revicw, 1. 304 (1953). 

8. 18 Annals of Internal Medicine, Vol. I, p. 457 (1943). 

10. Id., p. 7.

rotation on the list, with the outcome predetermined by the school of thought to which he belongs. 

For example, it is accepted that chemical, thermal or repeated mechanical trauma may produce cancer. Can a single mechanical trauma producc cancer in a susceptible individual? Can such trauma aggravate pre-existing cancer?

Can it precipitate metastasis and thus convert what may be an otherwise favorable situation into a hopeless one? On these points there are sharply divergent schools of thought, each of which claims support by eminent and respectable medical authority." If this be the issue in the case, then the result is foreoruained by the school of thought to which the selected impartial belongs. If he agrees with plaintiff's medical witness that causal relationship exists, it is likely plaintiff will prevail. If he disagrees, it is just as likely that defendant will prevail. But who is to say the "impartial” is correct? Does this bring certainty to the courtroom? It may bring certainty, at the risk of a grave miscarriage of justice, but it will not settle the medical conflict and the controversy will continue to rage.

Nor is it any answer to say that such a case involves but a small number of the total of personal injury claims in litigation. To the advocate consulted in such a matter, and to his client, such statistics are of small comfort.

But even in cases which may be statistically in the majority, medicine is itself classically divided. During a two-year period about 40 per cent of all cases recurred in impartial experts under the New York plan involved claims of post-concussion syndrome.'? Is there unanimity williin the medical profession as to the cause and nature oi this syndrome? Is it organic? Or is it l'umcrional? Is it a combin-

In matters as frequently seen as back injuries, cually benest and qualitied cloctors are similarly divided. Until quite recently diagnoses of ruptured interyertebral disc were seldom heard. low back complaints, even though crippling, were variously diagnosed as lumbago, sacro-iliac and lumbosacral strain. Tov often they were described derisively by defendants' counsel and by the physicians they retained as "railroad spine," or "courthouse spine" which according to them would miraculously cure spontaneously upon the therapy of a money payment. But Dr. Ben R. Wiltberger, writing on "The Medico-Legal Aspects of Low Back Pain" reports:16 “During the past ten years the majority of authorities have changed their concept of low back pain and its causes and have completely eliminated such diagnoses as lumbago, sacro-iliac and lumbosacral strain. It is now the consensus of opinion among such well-known authorities as Doctors MacNab, Harris, Key, Kimberley, Caldwell, Shutkin as well as many others, that, percentage-wise, the intervertebral disc is involved in the large majority of cases of idiopathic low back pain. These men fcel that the intervertebral disc is involved in approximately seventy-five per cent of the cases of all low back pain, and they divide the pathology into degenerated lumbar discs and rupturce intervertebral discs.” ها

Nor is there agreement on such questions as the effect of stress on coronary thrombosis, the effect of trauma on multiple sclerosis or on Parkinson's disease, the relationship between trauma and arthritis, or the question whether a real risk is involved in myelography. 

Justice Walter R. Hart, of the Supreme Court of New 

York, Second Department, referring critically to this aspect of New York's plan, has this to say:17 “I have heretofore stated that the doctors are assigned under a rotating system which introduces an element of chance into the situation. For instance, there is a difference of opinion with respect to injuries to the abdominal organs, one school holding that a normal appendix cannot be injured by means of a severe non-penetrating blow to the abdomen. Drs. Liniger and Melineus say yes; 

Drs. Jennings, Burger and Jacobi say no. In the case of a diseased appendix Drs. Bissell, Kelly and Goldbeck say yes; Drs. Fowler and Kessler say no. Myocardial infarction due to trauma, Dr. Boas proponent; Drs. Master, Dack and Jaffee opponents. There is likewise a wide conAict on the causation of Parkinson's disease and neoplastic diseases. It will be readily seen that a plaintiff's case is won or lost, depending upon which doctor is next on the rotating list of the particular panel. In the case of a myocardial infarction, if it drew Dr. Boas he is in, but the next plaintiff would draw Dr. 

Masters and he would be out.”

Thus, the validity of the litigant's complaints will be judged by these conceptions, or preconceptions, even though a substantial body of medical thought may ditier. 

The injured plaintiff will be judged by the fortuitous circumstance of the 'impartial cluctor's school of thought."'is 

16. Id., P. 441. 

17. Reported in "The Plaintili's Avocate;" Vul. 2, No. 7, p. 23, October, 1958. 

18. Book Review, "Tompartial Medical Testimony." 29 T.I... No. +, D. 473 (1956).

"The greatest experts take sides on tliesc questions. If so, have they not become partisans, if not to plaintiff or (lefendant, then to a school of thought, a point of view? If a partisan to the one or other school of thought is on the so-called 'impartial' panel, he brings his opinions with him: the wine must taste of its own grapes. 

And what of the school of thought not represented in the 'impartial panel, thereby labeled as less worthy of credence, demoted into darkness while the panel member is elevated to the light wrapped in tlie accolade of the court's confidence?"19

Here the point must be emphasized that these differences in opinion, the existence of and disputes between schools of thought, are not of the Bar's making. They simply echo the controversies between men of unquestioned competence and integrity within the field of medicine itself: "Before we of thie Bench and Bar submit the substantial rights of litigants to such heavy endorsenient of the opinions of one witness, is it too niuch to require that the men of medicine convince their own brethren before they would persuade us ?''20

However commendable may be the object of the project in preventing what is criticized as a “battle of experts” and in freeing the jury from choosing between conflicting points of view, it will succeed in presenting but a single point of view. And this even though there may be a substantial body of respectable medical authority to the contrary. 

III. 

This brings us to the most serious vice in these proposals: that by designating a medical witness as court appointed and "impartial" the court has in effect cloaked him with a robe of infallibility. He is thus elevated to a semi-judicial status which by its very designation, imports that his conclusions are far more worthy of acceptance than those of his medical brethren who have not been thus distinguished. It is inescapable then that in a vast majority of 

19. 20 NACCA Law Journal, p. 29 (1957). 

20. Emile Zola Berman, Esquire, of the New York Bar; Comments on “Impartial Medical Testimony," ,” 1956 Proceedings, Section of Insurance Law (A.B.A.) p. 298.

cases, the trier of fact, whether judge or jury, will do no more on the medical issue than represent the decision of the “impartial," surrounded as he is by the halo of truth. 

Apart from the fallacy that impartiality necessarily equals certainty and truth, this procedure is totally inconsistent with and does serious violence to our adversary process of trial by jury. Under that system, the validity of which has yet to be disproved, the credibility of witnesses, the credence to be given the bases for their conclusions, are all for the jury. But as soon as a witness becomes court-sponsored, and once the representation is made that this witness was selected not only for his great knowledge but also for his "impartiality,” that he is not a "paid partisan" but is above the implications of such term, that he is an aloof, objective scientist who occupies as to the medical phase of the trial the same, or nearly the same, high function performed as to the law by the judge, then, for all practical purposes, trial by a single witness is substituted for trial by jury. There is not the slightest doubt that in the overwhelming number of such cases the fact-finding process on the medical issues will be transferred from the jury box to the "impartial" expert, bearing as he does the imprimatur of the court's advance attestation that he alone is the fount of medical truth. Trial by jury then becomes no more than an empty illusion, a shibboleth to which lip-service is paid while its destruction is endorsed.

To many experienced trial lawyers and to a significant number of judges, this is not mere idle criticism. Even 

Justice Peck has observed, “There is some force in the tears these lawyers voice ..., pre! but he seems perfectly willing that the risk be run. At least live julges who participated in the New York experiment recognized the reality that the "impartial” testimony "exules an almost ineradicable odor

But even this is not the end of the danger in the plan. 

Ilhile it may be true that doctors of high repute are on such a panel, it is equally true that not all doctors of high repute are on such panels. Yet implicit in the entire concept of impartial medical is the erroneous assumption that variance in medical views between opposing physicians arises out of unseeming, improper or even dishonest partisanship. This is plainly unfounded. “Unless the trial by jury is to be abolished it is unfair to pin a badge of honor on one witness as having the full confidence of the court and withholding it from another, plainly labeling him as undeserving of confidence."1 The vast array of physicians of unquestioned integrity and competence, who have not been selected for appointment to such panels, have yet to be heard from, yet this grossly unjust implication remains.

disability and prognosis, the trial advocate should bc cntitled to rely on his own medical information and to produce that medical testimony which supports hiin. What is more, he is entitled to do this without his medical witness being stigmatized as partisan and simply because he is partisan, suspect. 

Only in this manner can the jury be given full access to all views without the extreme control by the views of a single court-sponsored medical spokesman. Human experience over the years has demonstrated that the element of chance is best averaged out against a representative crosssection of the community, which is completely free to accept that view which to it seems most reasonable. The impartial medical plan does not improve this process. It destroys it. · And in destroying it, it creates far greater evils than the ailment it is intended to correct. 

The Report of the Special Committee of the Bar Association of the City of New York purports to recognize that the overwhelming number of lawyers and physicians are both honorable and dedicated. 32 Yet they are stigmatized by implication by the activities of what is described as "a small outlaw fringe."33 Says the Report :84 "Both the medical and legal professions, composed predominately of honorable and dedicated men and women, are undeservedly stigmatized by a small outlaw fringe of brazenly unscrupulous practitioners and by a somewhat larger group of lawyers and doctors who have not disciplined theinselves to conform to the highest ideals of their professions." 

While the correction of this condition is imdoubtedly the joint responsibility of both professions, we will have lost completely our perspective iſ we propose to remedy this by such radical surgery as to eliminate the right to trial by judge and jury as we know it. 

Rather does the solution for this condition lie with militant surveillance both by bar associations and medical

IV. 

Finally, one must observe that it is difficult to compre- Jiend how the plan can relieve congestion at the trial level by adding more rather than fewer trial witnesses. The answer may perhaps lie not in the direction of trial, but rather in the direction of use of the plan for the purpose of promoting an increased number of pre-trial settlements. But here again it must be carefully controlled and the role of the impartial must be properly evaluated both by the court and by counsel, so that compulsion is not substituted for conciliation.30

In this connection a curious side effect of these proposals for impartial medical has been to create the erroneous impression that this plan alone represents salvation and that this is the solution to calendar congestion. The concept is pressed so strongly that all attention is diverted from other proposals far more likely to accomplish the desired relief, without any reference whatever to the impartial medical plan. One has only to observe the spectacular accomplishment of the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Manhattan) by the vigorous and effective use of pre-trial scttlement conference to see that here lies the most encouraging technique for the climination of current backlogs. 

35. Scc "Scientific Proof and Relations of Law and Medicine," Dr. 

Jubert Winston Smith, 18 Annals of Internal Medicine, Vol. 1, p. 457 ; sce also "The Minnesota Plan for Expert Medical Testimony," Chicago 

Dar Kecorri, Jamary 1957. 

36. Scc l'cck, “Impartial Mclical Testimony," 22 F.R.D. 21 at p. 24 (1958).

V. 

The entire system of the administration of justice in this class of litigation should not be pinned to the fallibility or infallibility of a single witness. As Dean Roscoe Pound has written :87 "Legal precepts presuppose as given the facts to which they are applicable. When the facts are established a rule of law fixes the legal result. But the facts in any concrete controversy are seldom so given to us as to permit a mere mechanical process of applying the appointed legal precepts. They must be ascertained, and in ascertaining them courts have always had to call upon the medical profession for assistance in interpreting the evidence. To take a memorable example, in the English witchcraft trials of 1644 an experiment conducted by some skeptical persons threw doubt upon the testimony for the prosecution. Thereupon the court called Sir 

Thomas Browne, a leading physician of the time, and as the report tells us, 'a person of great knowledge.' After hearing the evidence and seeing the children claiming to be bewitched, he gave it as his opinion that they were, told of some recent cases in Denmark where there were the same manifestations, and explained how the witches had operated. The court could do no more than accept the opinion of so great an authority. But the lesson of judicial fallibility resulting from fallibility of the means of ascertaining the facts is always with us.”

Neither judges nor lawyers can bring certainty to medicine. This must and should be left to the men of medical science. Until that certainty is achieved, proposals such as the impartial medical expert plan have no place within the framework of trial by jury.

THE JUDICIAL CONFERENCE OF MICHIGAN IMPARTIAL MEDICAL TESTIMONX IN CIVIL ACTIONS 

Prepared by George M. Mack of the Judicial Conference research staff under the guidance of Professors Charles W. Joiner and John W. Reed of the University of Michigan 

Law School.

---

IN CIVIL ACTIONS 

One of the areas vulnerable to criticism in the advocacy system is the treatment of expert witnesses, particularly medical experts, during trials. The guns leveled by the critics are double barreled: one barrel is aimed at the use and abuse of the hypothetical question; the other points to the tendency in experts toward partisanship which too often turns the civil action into the well-known "battle of the experts." 

The former problem concerns itself with the manner in which an expert's opinion testimony is presented to the fact-finding body, while the latter centers on the quality and objectivity of scientific testimony, i.e., the degree of credibility in the expert witnesses.

This study is devoted to the latter problem. Discussed are the specific objections to the present method of presenting expert testimony, the various approaches open to solve the problem, the status of and the difficulties involved in present Michigan law, and some specific schemes which have been suggested and/or adopted to cure present evils. 

The last portion of this study deals with a discussion of specific elements thought to be necessary for the successful operation of any impartial medical expert witness . program. 

Purpose of experts: To Inform. 

The early experts participating in trials were appointed by the court to advise both judge and jury on matters outside the knowledge of the ordinary layınan. During the last century and a half the manner of bringing experts into court has changed to where now they are almost exclusively introduced as witnesses for one side or the other. 

However, the purpose for such testimony has remained the same: to present specialized information for the benefit of the judge and jury. 1

- Doctors Object. Since the drift away from the use of non-partisan experts, lawyers, when preparing personal injury cases, have often found it difficult to obtain the services and cooperation of competent medical specialists. Many highly qualified physicians refuse to testify in court for reasons which have been both publicly and privately expressed for years, Compared to the quiet objective air of their offices and laboratories, the contentious atmosphere of courtrooms is unbearable. Some doctors complain that they have suffered great inconveniences in the services of incon- siderate attorneys. They have been made uncomfortable by the cutting inquiries and ill conceived abuses of cross examiners. They have been bulldozed into extreme positions in disputed areas where authorities differ. 

They have been pitted against colleagues in the public arena of the courtroom.

In preparing for and in participating in the presentation of a case, qualified doctors have used up many valuable hours which otherwise could have been devoted to their own professional practice. And likely as not when the experts appear in court they find the juries more ready to believe "the unqualified medical advocates on the other side. ,,2

Lawyers Object. 

On the other hand, the legal profession has had its own reasons for distrusting the doctor in court. 

A recent report of a special committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York stated in part: 

.. not all doctors who testify are equally competent . 

Nor are all doctors equally impartial. Some are above suspicion. A few are corrupt. In between are a number who become infected with bias when called as witnesses in the conventional way. Cast in the roles of partisans, subjected to hostile cross-examination, and paid by one side, they

tend to color their testimony. Their opinions may be expressed a little more strongly than the facts or the state of medical knowledge warrant; and needed reservations may be omitted when convenient. 

As experts, they receive not ordinary witness fees, but special compensation, sometimes very substantial in amount. Too often their testimony reflects the 3 partisan source from which their compensation comes. 

Methods of Approach. Keeping this in mind, a number of approaches to the problem have been made, all of which have aimed to improve the status of expert opinion evidence. Many of these proposals are concerned primarily with criminal actions, in particular dealing with the plea of insanity. But some have focused their attention on civil actions where one of the principal causes for long delay in bringing a' case to its completion in either a jury verdict or settlement between the litigants has been the extremely diverse claims between the parties over the causation of personal injuries 4, and the extent thereof. In these cases expert medical testimony is necessary to establish the claims, and it is here that the parties find convincing medical men with astonishing differences of opinions. It has long been felt that if a way could be found to sift the medical evidence, to allow the doctors to refrain from partisan advocacy and to approach the case as they would a medical problem, the opening wouid then appear to achieve more friendly settlements out of court and to expedite the testimony when a case does reach the trial stage. Education,

It has been suggested that the primary solution to the problem is to educate both the legal and medical professions in their mutual problems and responsibilities in respect to the administration of justice with- in the community. This is being done in many localities through the cooperation of the medical and bar associations. 

But to obtain understanding of

another's problem and do no more is merely to tolerate a difficult situation, not to solve it. 

Higher Standard.

Coupled with the demand for a greater understanding between the two great professions has been the suggestion that courts set a higher standard for competency in experts. 7. ? At present a general practitioner may be held competent to testify on subjects ordinarily set aside for specialists. 8 

It is felt that in many cases there would be much less medical controversy and more authoritative opinions if the parties were required to seek out specialists where the inedical treatment so requires. When the courts come to require a high degree of medical knowledge in those who purport to give expert testimony, then many, if not all, of the professional medical advocates will disappear from the scene. 

This will not eliminate the tendency to be a "team player" with the side which calls the expert, but it certainly will eradicate the practice of passing off the bottom of the barrel as the cream of the crop.

Greater Fee. In many jurisdictions an expert witness may receive no more compensation for his professional services than an ordinary witness. 

It has been suggested that this be changed to permit the expert to receive his customary professional fee. 

This may increase the willingness of those, who now are reluctant to sacrifice patient-timne, to place their knowledge at the disposal of the court and jury. 

Court-Appointed Expert.

Most significant in recent years has been the adoption of provisions which permit the courts to appoint their own experts who then will testify along with the witnesses for the opposing parties. 

It is felt that this impartial status is more suited for a man of science who is trained to seek the truth through objective studies and to follow it no matter

where it may lead.

This status seems to appeal more to the real specialist vho is devoted to a scientific approach to the world about him. 

Secure in the guarantee of an impartial expert, some jurisdictions have been able to 1 obtain whole panels of the best medical specialists in the community and 10 have found the physicians ready and willing to serve in this capacity. In participating, however, the doctors understand that they must be subject to cross examination.

Thus the legal and the medical professions have combined their respective tools for the search of truth into the accepted proceed - 11 ings for the adininistration of justice. 

Opposition to Court-Appointed Experts. Although the views in favor 12 of such a method appear to be in the overwhelming majority, they are by no means unanimous. Those opposing a change in procedure, though recognizing the problem, believe that it can be solved within our present system of advocacy. They recommend the above mentioned educational program to increase the understanding between the legal and medical professions, the raising of standards of both professions, and the placing of stricter requirements in the qualification for practicing as an attorney or an expert witness in the courtroom.

In addition they decry the failure by the bench and professional associations to use their powers of discipline. But stronger than their proposals to correct the abuses in the present system has been their persistent opposition to the court appointed expert. Primarily they fear that due to greater prestige with the jury a mistake by the court's witness will cause irreparable damage to a claim which cross examination cannot salvage. 

They fear that in many areas an expert will be appointed more out of local political considerations than because of his skill and experience in medicine.'

These arguments are valid as applied to some schemes, but more recent innovations (described below under "Specific Proposals") have been designed to answer these objections and still seek the aid which impartial testimony can give.

Michigan Law. No such procedure is available in Michigan at the present time. In 1905 the Michigan Legislature enacted a statute which provided :

In criminal cases for homicide where the issues in. volve expert knowledge or opinion the court shail appoint one or more suitable disinterested persons, not exceeding three, to investigate such issues and testify at the trial; and the compensation of such person or persons shall be fixed by the court and paid by the county where indictment was found, and the fact that such witness or witnesses have been so appointed shall be made linown to the jury. 

This provision shall not preclude either prosecution or defense from using expert witnesses at the trial. 14

Five years later in 1910 the Supreme Court of Michigan was called upon to determine the constitutionality of this provison and found it invalid 15 in People v. Dickerson. 

The court said that the provision was unconstitutional for three reasons.

One, inasmuch as the statute authorized the appointment and selection of witnesses by the judge, it improperly delegated administrative powers, because the duty to designate witnesses to testify 16 (for the state) in a criminal case is that of the prosecutor. 

Two, there was no notice required to be given to either the accused or the prosecutor; thus, the reasons impelling the selection of experts could not be revealed, the names of the experts could not be indorsed on the indictment as required by law, and the accused would not know in advance the persons who were to 17 testify against him.

And three, it provided for undue influence by the court upon the jury insofar as the jury might interpret the expert's testimony as the opinion of the court itself. 18

Separation of Powers. 

The separation of powerg argument ignored the whole history of the use of expert witnesses, particularly the practice of using an impartial witness to aid the judge and the jury in understanding difficult scientific problems. The court-appointed experts were not witnesses for the state, because it was just as possible for the impartial experi to support the contentions of the defendant. In addition, courts could require 19 the prosecution to call a witness and have been known to perform other 20 so-called administrative functions.

Notice. 

The question of notice seems to present the most telling argument against the 1905 provision. Although the court was in error in saying that the names of the expert witnesses could not later be indorsed 21 upon the indictment, and although there is no assurance that the court's expert would testify against the accused, there were good grounds for requiring notice.

Statutes and rules adopted subsequently in Michigan and in other states which provide for a court-appointed examiner or expert witness 22 have included a notice provision and usually provide for a hearing. 

Undue Influence Upon Jury. The principal objection to this method of obtaining impartial testimony at the present time is the same as the court voiced in the Dickerson case: when the judge announces to the jury that the witness was appointed by the court, the jury then places more faith in the impartial witness than in the experts for the parties. This is exactly what is anticipated and desired by the proponents of this measure. If, in fact, an impartial expert witness is desirable, then the existence of his impartiality must be made known to the jury, for otherwise the confusion is compounded by adding another noise to the din of battle. 

But several distinctions must be

AU

clarified in order to justify the revelation of court a pointment. The expert must be a highly qualified specialist in the field of medicine in which the information is sought, and this should be revealed during the questioning to establish his credibility. 

When the judge announces to the jury that the expert was appointed by the court, he is doing nothing inore than announcing that this witness is not a partisan representative. The judge is neither attesting to the truth or accuracy of the testimony itself nor giving his own opinion through a mouthpiece. The court expert should always be subject to cross examination, which is usually specifically provided for, and both sides should be free to present their own experts to the jury. If this is done, there 23 should be no legitimate objection of unfairness. 

A further distinction must be made between the scope of our discussion and the Dickerson case. 

This study concerns itself with the proposals for securing impartial witnesses in civil actions, not criminal cases. în criminal homicide actions, such as provided for in the 1905 Michigan statute, juries may receive the impression that all of the power of the state has been directed against an accused defendant, This impression is not present in a civil action between two private individuals when the court profers an impartial witness to inform the jury. Many of the devices designed to protect a person accused of a crime are neither necessary nor available to a party in a civil action. 

Thus, the ruling in the Dickerson case, howsoever it might be criticized, is not applicable to non-criminal cases. 

Other Michigan Statutes.

At the present time the Michigan statutes not only permit, but require a judge to appoint physicians to examine a subject in the following types of proceedings: blood test for determination of

ستننس- .'t "" ات

24 paternity in a bastardy action, 25 commitment of drug addicts, commit- 26 27 ment of criminal sexual psychopaths, determination of criminal insanity, 28 commitment of the mentally diseased or insane, hearing for release from 29 a state hospital claiming sound mind, examination of persons who have 30 been discharged from state hospitals, and sterilization of mertal defect- 31 ives.

In addition, it is provided that the Workman's Compensation Com- mission may appoint an examining physician when disputed claims are heard. 

Also the Michigan Supreme Court Rule 41, 82, provides that the court may 33 order a physical examination of a person in advance of trial.

So also do 34 

the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. 

Thus, it can be seen that Michigan practice does utilize impartial medical testimony in certain cases. 

But the area, aside from criminal cases, in which there is the greatest need for halting the battle of the experts by employing an impartial witness has so far been bypassed.

Specific Proposals. The proposals and legislation to achieve impartial expert, primarily medical, testimony have been many, too many to describe in detail herein. However, a few of the principal procedures are outlined below together with references to other provisions to be found elsewhere. 

At the end of this portion of the study is a general critique given in the form of recommendations of preferred methods. 35 

MODEL EXPERT TESTIMONY ACT 

ACT

This act was approved by the National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform State Laws in 1937 as a Uniform Act and was redesignated a Model Act in 1943. In addition to the procedures for securing impartial expert testimony is a provision dealing with the hypothetical question which will not be discussed herein,

다​.

Type of Cases Where Invoked. 

Civil or criminal cases whenever "'issues arise upon which the court deems expert evidence is desirable." 

Procedure. The court, on its own motion or on the motion of one of the parties involved, appoints one to three experts on each issue after reasonable notice to the parties informing them of the names of the experts proposed for appointment. However, if the parties agree upon certain experts then they will be appointed. After reasonable notice the experts shall exarnine and inspect such persons and subject matter as directed by the court within the scope so directed and each party shall be permitted to be present at such examination and inspection. The experts may be required by the court to prepare a written report under oath and file it with the clerk of court where it will be open for inspection by any of the parties. Testimony.

At the trial the expert may be called to testify by the court or by either of the parties. The fact that he has been appointed by the court will then be made known to the jury. He shall read his report subject to objections on admissibility. He is subject to cross examination by any party on his qualifications and on the subject of his testimony. 

The compensation of the expert is fixed at a reasonable amount by the court. In criminal actions it is paid by the county. In civil actions it is paid in equal parts by the opposing parties to the clerk of court and thereafter assessed as costs.

The receipt of any additional compensation by the expert or the offer or promise by any person to pay more compensation is made unlawful. 

Other Experts. Any party may call his own expert witness after giving reasonable notice to the other parties of the name and address of that expert.

一一​.一

They are permitted similar access to persons, places and things under inves. tigation as is the court appointed expert. 

The court may permit or require a pretrial conference among experts and any two or more of them may unite in a report which may be introduced into evidence by any party or by the court. 

Similar Provisions.

Similar recommendations have been made by the Judicial Council of New Jersey in 1931, the Medico-Legal Society of New York in 1907, the Board of Circuit Judges of Wisconsin in 191.9, the Committee of 

Jurisprudence and Law Reform of the American Bar Association in 1926, the Medico-Legal Society of Massachusetts in 1897, the Committee on Crime and 36 Insanity of the cimerican Institute of Criminal Law in 1914, the American 37 

Law Institute in 1942, and the Law Revision Commission of New York in 38 1936.

In October 1942, South Dakota by the Rule of the Supreme Court 39 adopted the entire Model Expert Testimony Act.

Similar provisions have 40 41 42 been adopted in California, Rhode Island, and Wisconsin.

43 

UNIFORM RULES OF EVIDENCE, RULES 59, 60, 61 

In 1953 the National Conference of Cominissioners on Uniform State Laws adopted the Uniform Rules of Evidence. 

Sections 59, 60, and 61 pertain to the appointment and compensation of an impartial expert by the court. 

Type of Cases Where Invoked. In any action where the judge "determines that the appointinent of expert witnesses 44 niay be desirable," . . Procedure.

The judge will order the parties to show why an expert should not be appointed, and after an opportunity for a hearing, will accept nominations and appoint an expert. If all parties agree on an expert, then he shall be appointed. The expert must consent to serve as an impartial witness. 

The judge determines the scope of the duties of the expert and so informs him

at a conference at which the parties have an opportunity to participate. After the examination the expert then informs the parties of his findings. 

Testimony. The expert may be called by any of the parties or by the judge. He is subject to direct and cross examination by each party. 

The facts concerning the appointment of the expert are to be revealed to the jury as relevant to the credibility of the expert. 

Compensation. The expert is entitled to reasonable compensation as determined by the judge. It shall be paid, in a criminal action by the county as costs, and in a civil action by the opposing parties equally and then taxed as costs.

Other Experts. 

The parties may call their own expert witnesses at their own expense, but the amount of compensation so received by these experts may be subject to inquiry as relevant to their credibility. 

Similar Provisions. In drafting these sections the Commissioners patterned them after Rule 28 of the Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure and oriented them to the Model Code of Evidence, Rules 403 and 407, the 

Model Expert Testimony Act, the Uniform Act on Blood Tests to Determine 

45 46 Paternity, and the Uniform Rules of Criminal Procedure, Rule 37.

NEW YORK CIVIL PRACTICE ACT $306 

Type of Cases Invoked. Actions for damages due to personal injury. 

Procedure. If the defendant shows the court that he is ignorant of the nature and extent of the plaintiff's injuries, the court will order the plaintiff to submit to an examination by one or more court appointed physicians or surgeon..

The manner and scope of the examination will be determined by the court. If the plaintiff is a female, she may be entitled to have present at

the examination her personal physician and a relative or other person as the court directs. Testimony.

The testimony of the examining expert may be taken by deposition. Compensation. 

Not mentioned. 

Other Experts. 

Not mentioned.

NEW YORK COUNTY IMPARTIAL MEDICAL EXPERT TESTIMONY PROJECT 

47 Due to the vast number of civil damage actions awaiting trial in New York County in 1952, the Appellate Division of the Supreme Court of that county took a number of steps to relieve the courts of that backlog. Among these measures was a new court rule permitting the judges of the trial courts to appoint medical experts to resolve differences between parties in especially trying cases.

The Rule was first adopted as an experiment, but it proved so beneficial in encouraging settlements, bringing about more equitable verdicts, improving the quality of medical examinations, and reducing the time 4. of trial that it has been permanently affixed in the rules of that court division. 

In October 1953 the project was extended to Bronx County with similar suc- 49 cess.

The experimental project was financed by grants froin the Alfred P. 

Sloan Foundation and the Ford Motor Company Fund until 1955 when the burden was assumed by the courts. 

Type of Cases Where Invoked. In any personal injury case in which before trial a judge believes that an examination of the injured person and a report thereof by an impartial medical expert would help materially in the just determination of the case.

Procedure. After consulting with counsel for the respective parties, usually during the pre-trial hearing, the judge may order the examination and report through the "Medical Report Office'' of the court. 

The examination is then made by a member of a rotating panel of physicians, picked for their particular qualifications by the New York Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society, and a report thereof is filed with the Medical Report Office. Copies of the report are made available by the Office to all parties. Another pre-trial conference usually follows the distribution of the medical report. Testimony.

Either party or the trial judge may call the court expert, who is subject to questioning by any party. Compensation,

When his bill is approved by the court, the impartial expert is paid his regular professional fee out of court funds. There is no charge against the parties, Other Experts. Not mentioned. 

Similar Provisions, See Baltimore, below. 50 

BALTIMOR E CITY IMPAR TIAL MEDICAL TESTIMONY PLAN 

Recently, the courts in Baltimore have adopted a plan similar to that of New York County. However, it was not incorporated into any procedural code or formal court rules. 

The plan is administered under the inherent power of the courts to appoint their own expert witnesses ir appropriate cases. 

Types of Cases Invoked. In "extraordinary cases, where there is great divergence between medical reports or in other special circumstances.

'' 

Procedure. The judge, at the request of one of the parties, usually during the pre-trial conference, presents a list of names of members of a

panel designated by the State Medical Society (Medical & Chirurgical Faculty of Maryland) to the counsel and asks if they object to anyone on the list, Il 80, then the disapproved physicians are striken off the list and the judge appoints one of the remaining experts. If possible, this selection is done by agreement. The judge then informs the doctor of his appointment as an expert. The time of the examination is arranged for by one of the lawyers. 

After the examination, the doctor sends his report in triplicate to the judge who then sends one copy to each side and keeps one for his records. TI Testimony.

The expert testifies in court, but the details of the procedure are not known. Compensation.

Counsel for the party requesting the appointment of the impartial expert either guarantees the payment of the fee or makes a deposit with the court to cover the fee. The bill of the examining expert is submitted with his report and is paid by the court. 

The fee is later taxed as costs and distributed among the parties at the discretion of the judge. 

Other Experts. Not mentioned. 

Similar Provisions, See New York County, above. 52 

MINNESOTA PLAN 

In 1940 the Minnesota State Bar Association and the Minnesota State Medical Association jointly adopted a plan to discipline errant courtroom practitioners of both professions. This plan operates under a different theory and is not strictly aimed at securing impartial medical testimony. It is designed to place a tighter control over the courtroom practices of attorneys and doctors,

We include it here, however, as a technique to be employed should it be thought that the primary solution lies in the area of education,

control and stricter requirements for qualification of experts. It requires no legislation, but instead the professional associations assume a more strict attitude in policing the activities of their members, Procedure.

The Minnesota State Medical Association has set up a Committee on Medical Testimony to review the testimony of doctors suspected of unethical conduct in acting as expert medical witnesses. 

It handles complaints against doctors appearing as expert witnesses received from doctors, judges, lawyers and other interested parties. The committee concerns itself with the evaluation of the sworn testimony given by the doctor complained against. It calls for assistance experts from relevant specialties who examine the testi. mony and make findings as to whether or not it was justifiable or at least honest. If the findings are that the witness was not acting in good faith or that his testimony was dishonest or if there was any other form of unethical conduct on his part, the committee then recommends to the State Board of Medical Examiners disciplinary action. 53 The plan operates sirnilarly with respect to attorneys and the appropriate committee of the Bar association encouraging a greater use of the bar association's power to discipline its members. 

Similar Provisions.

Chicago and the State of Illinois adopted the 54 

Plan in 1946.

INHERENT POWER OF COURT 

In at least iwo jurisdictions courts have appointed impartial experts without any legislative authority whatsoever. 

Their action has been approved as an exercise of the court's inherent power to inform itself of the facts of the case, 55 

In State v. Petty at the request of the prosecutor in a homicide case, the trial court appointed three physicians to examine the defendant who pleaded "sadistic insanity" in defense. In affirming the verdict of guility the

Supreme Court of Nevada denied that there was any element of self-incrimination present and stated that if the trial court in appointing the physicians did not express any opinion upon the facts, then the procedure was proper. 56 

In State v. Horne a hornicide case, the trial court called a physician against the wishes of both the prosecutor and defendant, qualified him as an expert, and examined him as to the mental condition of the defendant who pleaded insanity. The North Carolina Supreme Court held that the calling of an impartial expert witness "is a matter within the sound discretion of the trial judge. He has the right to call to the witness stand and examine any witness who may be able to shed light upon the controversy. He should exercise this right with care and should so conduct the examination as not to 57 prejudice either party."

CRITIQUE 

Comment on the above methods of providing an impartial medical expert may best be done by outlining what might be an ideal procedure for accomplishing the purpose intended, viz., obtaining impartial opinion testimony which is not biighted by unconscious partisan bias or outright dishonesty, while still providing the litigants with the protections inherent in the adversary system. In addition to increasing understanding between the two professions, raising the standards of competency of experts, encouraging greater control over the trial by the judge, and tightening the disciplinary control of professional societies, something more must be done to improve the fact-finding process in this area.

The following elements must be considered in setting up a procedure for obtaining impartial medical testimony:

1. Rules or Statute. In recent years more emphasis has been placed on the authority of courts to make rules of procedure. Recent judicial action in a number of jurisdictions reaffirm the inherent power of courts to appoint 58 expert witnesses.

In line with this trend, it is preferable to enact impartial expe testimony procedure as a court rule. 

2. Manner of Initiating Procedure.

In the Uniform Rules of Evidence and the New Yori. County Project the judge initiates the appointment procedure when he sees it desirable at the pre-trial conference. In Baltimore, New 

York State, and hode Island one of the parties must make the initial motion for the use of tie procedure. Most provisione allow for both methods but leave the final determination to the judge's discretion, and this seems to more fully utilize the procedure in the maximum number of cases. 

3. Notice and Hearing. Notice and a subsequent hearing are desirable not only for constitutional reasons, but for practical ones. 

The notice may be given at the pre-trial conference or at another time and should result in a conference between counsel and judge so that the judge may explain his reasons for invoking the procedure, 4. Agreement as to Expert and Scope of Examination. 

To avoid a later disruption of the procedure it is preferable to gain the assent of all of the parties as to the witness to be appointed and the nature and scope of his examination and report. There is a danger, though, that both sides oppose the use of an impartial expert and as a result agree upon a less effective witness and a more limited scope of inquiry than otherwise would be had. 

Therefore, the judge should have the ultimate authority here as elsewhere, The Baltimore Plan, providing that the parties may disapprove names on the

panel while the judge choose from the remaining experte, seems to supply a satisfactory answer to this problem, and if it is found that one party should insist on disapproving all names presented, then there could be instituted a limitation on the number of "strikeg" as is done with jury panels. 

5. Qualified Group of Experts. One of the objections to the provision in the New York Civil Practice Act, as well as to most of the other plans, is that there is no ready-made group of highly qualified experts from which a judge may choose. Critics of court appointed expert proposals point out that at its worst the failure to specify exactly who is to be appointed permite judges to reward friends while at best it forces a man trained in law to make a med. 59 ical decision as to who is a qualified specialist. 

New York County and Baltimore, in permitting the medical profession 60 to choose their own experts, seem to have provided an answer to the critics. 

Baltimore courts place only one restriction on the choice of the medical 80ciety, viz., that the physicians named to the panels be those "whom the doctors themselves would consult in cases involving members of their own fam. ilies; whose judgment and standing in the medical community was such as to ,,61 put them above suspicion of either favoritism or incompetence, The classification of the panels shouid be determined beforehand, but there should be leeway to add other panels when needed. 

New York County set up 16 panels (later added two more) while Baltimore designated 18 fields 62 of medicine to be covered. .

Baltimore panele contain three men each, but Chief Judge Niles of the Supreme Bench of Baltimore recommends the New York County system of varying the size of the panels in relation to the anticipated uspp.

Thus, the orthopedics, general surgery, neurosurgery, and 63

radiology panels should be considerably larger than those devoted to dermatology, obstetrics, gynecology or endocrinology, Needless to say, all physicians appointed to the panels should con sent to their assignment and the duties entailed thereby. 

6. Examination and Report. Some provisions attempt to spell out how or under what conditions the examination is to be held, who is to be present, with whom the court's expert shall consult, etc. 

Others say nothing except that an examination shall be held, Ideally, the mode of examination should be left up to the expert. Doctors vary in their methods of examinations and have different preferences concerning with whom they consult or what medical records they may wish to see. 

This also may vary with the case as well as with the doctor. The court, therefore, should not provide for the presence at the examination of anyone else or the submission to the impartial expert of any medical records or reports without first consulting with the appointee. 

In addition, some cases may require more than one expert, either because of different medical claims or the complexity and difficulty of a single claim. Joint or separate examinations should be permitted depending on the preferences of the doctors and, above all, consultation between the impartial experts should be encouraged,

Since the expert has been appointed by the court, the court should relieve the expert of as many of the procedural details as possible. Thus, the report should be filed by the doctor with the court and then distributed by the court to the parties.

Following the submission of the impartial expert's report the judge may feel it advisable for the "partisan" physicians and the impartial expert to get together and try to reconcile their differences. This may be done in

- the presence or absence of counsel, whichever is thought preferable. The impartial expert's report should be submitted first, however, in order to provide a solid base on which to talk over differences. Should the impartial expert later wish to amend his report, then he should be permitted to do so, hut this change should be noted and open to questioning during the trial. 

7. Reasonable Fee,

Since high-grade physicians are anticipated to serve on the expert panels, one cannot expect that they will donate their valuable hours to the courts over a long period of time for nominal witness fees. 

They should be paid a professional fee for their professional services, even at the risk of an occasional excessive fee. 64 In Baltimore seldom has the examination fee been more than $100 while in New York fees for examinations have ranged from $25 to $200, averaging $55 per examination and $78 per case (some cases requiring more than 65 one examination).

The practice in New York has been to stop using the services of doctors whose fees appear to be too high. Since the good will and cooperation of the medical profession is essential to the successful operation of such a scheme, the question of high fees is a delicate matter. 

Such cooper. ation might not be obtained if a fixed schedule of fees were instituted or if 66 courts were to disapprove some bills as unreasonable, 8. Payment of Fees.

Two principal methods have been used to pay the fees in civil cases: payment out of court funds and taxing as costs to the litigents.

When the court pays there is likely to be less resistance by the partie o involved, especialiy where one or both are financially insecure. 

In addition there is added assurance of the expert's impartiality. 

However, certain 67

litigants may choose to lean on court provided experts rather than to hear the additional expense themselves. 

This situation can be avoided if the judge makes the final determination on whether or not to use the procedure. The cost to the taxpayers should not be excessive, as was discovered in New 68 York,

The principal difficulty in providing for payment of the fees by the court is involved in obtaining funds for such payments. Where courts must rely on county funds, they may find it difficult to obtain authorization from hard-pressed county boards of supervisors for such an expenditure, Tazing the fee as costs will relieve the courts of any expense involved and will certainly encourage even more responsibility among parties in making 1 or disputing claims.

The spectre of such additional cost might have a great influence on spurious claimants or stubborn defendants. 

It is necessary, however, to use some plan to assure the payment of the doctor, for in too many cases a losing party who is charged with the costs proves to be uncollectable at the end of the trial.

Several systems have been devised to guarantee payment.

In Baltimore, where one of the parties initiates the impartial expert procedure, counsel for that party is required to either guarantee the fee or 1 place a deposit with the clerk of court, Another method provides that each 69 side contribute 50% of the fee. 

In imposing this additional cost upon the litigants something more than 70 a court rule may be necessary. Michigan statutes permit the taxation of expert witness fees as costs, but this provision may contemplate only parti- 71 san witnesses.

It seerns, however, that in Michigan the power of the courts to fix and tax costs has long been recognized and is presently exercised in 

Supreme Court Rule 5. 72

. 

9. Informing the Jury of the Status of the Impartial Witness. 

Since impartiality is of more value when its existence is known to the fact-finders, the jury should be informed that the impartial witness was appointed by the judge, Chief Judge Niles of Baltimore has said: . . It is expected ... that the fact of the appointment by the court shall be made known to the jury, and in this respect the court-appointed expert is on the same basis as the other experts, for the jury always knows which side has called a given witness. i. 

There is fear among some attorneys that the appointment of an expert witness by the court will ''invade the province of the jury" and may make such a witness a "dictator" who may deprive a deserving litigant of his rights. It may be that the jury will give more weight to the testimony of an impartial expert appointed by the court than to the testimony of a witness called by one of the partisans, but I cannot believe that such an effect would be harmful when it is of the essence of the ratter that the impartial expert is in the top ranks of his profession, and that he is chosen by the court and the redical profession itself solely on the basis of his ability to tell the jury the medical truth so far as it can be ascertained. 73

The court might explain to the jury that although the witness was chosen for his impartiality, it is up to the jury to determine the weight to be given to his testimony. 

10. Rights of Parties During Expert's Testimony. 

Most schemes permit the expert to be called by either party or the court and permit cross examination by one or both sides. In addition, the expert is usually permitted to read his report as part of his testimony, but any part of it may be subject to other objections under the rules of admissability of evidence. 

No party should be deprived of any of his rights to challenge the expert's qualifications, credibility, and testimony.

. 

Conclusion. If these basic elements are provided, then the objectives of a plan for impartial medical testimony should be achieved. The beneficial results should be quickly realized. There should be an impressive rise in the number of settlements, a shortening of the duration of trials, and a considerable improvement in the quality and accuracy of the presentation of medical 74 facts and opinions in civil actions both before and during the trials. These results will be justified in a greater acceptance of the judicial procedures by, and more cooperation from, the medical profession, less frustration in the jury during the trial, and greater satisfaction and trust in the ultimate outcome of civil cases by the bench, bar, and general public.
