Partial Text of a Panel Discussion 

By 

Harrison L. McLaughlin, M. D. 

New York Before 

The American Association for the Surgery of Trauma 

October 3, 1958

When first invited to become a member of the Impartial Panel which Judge Peck and Doctor Craig have described, I found myself in somewhat of a quandaty, for my immediate reactions were quite contradictory. 

I did not want to have anything to do with it, and yet I knew I should.

I wanted no part of anything that might 'entail an appearance in court as a medical witness. 

But, here was organized medicine and the law collaborating in an effort to clean up a mess for which neither was entirely blamless. 

And so, with more than a few misgiving's, but because this eminently worthy project was sponsored by such stable and responsible bodies, my acceptance was inevitable. As 

I recall my misgivings, which at the time seemed very well founded, it now occurs to me that they were really nothing more than the reflection of two 'emotions anxiety that I might not acquit myself creditably amidst

Page 2 the complexities of legal and court procedures about which I knew nothing, and unpleasant memories of real or fancied personal affronts and inconveniences which had attended my previous unavoidable entanglements in court. 

Prolonged association with a Trauma Service had subjected me to an to an equally long and liberal education in the ways, wiles and importunities of lawyers who wished to buy my services as a witness, and in the blandishments of litigating patients who desired an additional advocate. 

A11 paid lip service to the truth. 

None ever asked outright for testimony that was anything but the truth, but few seemed interested in the whole truth only that portion of the truth which would be self-serving. 

I recalled the time and trouble entailed in building around my usual daily haunts an impenetrable defense against these onslaughts. 

I had visions of a disintegration of the personal ivory tower in which I had enjoyed such a splendid isolationism from matters legal. 

I had memories of the inconveniences, and of the actual or potential neglect of medical responsibilities, necessitated by my few previous appearances as a medical witness, and of the times I had been enjoined to appear as a witness and waited around interminably in some court room, only to be dismissed finally after a wasted day, with the offhand advice that the case had been settled out of court.

There were bitter memories of the distaste associated with certain

Page . techniques of cross-examination techniques which by innuendo, questioned my competence, cast suspicion upon my motives and, in general, promoted an acute (if temporary) collapse of my ego.

Above all, there was an abhorrence for participation in any situation which might permit even the strangers in a court room to catalog me in the same category as some of the professional medical witnesses I had had the opportunity 1 ! to see and hear in action. for, who is there in any court to tell the judge and jury which medical witness Is, and which is not, exploiting his ignorance or prostituting his profession? 1 And so, with these misgivings in the background, 1 I became i member of the Impartial Panel of Medical Bxaminers, and awaited my first assignment with both curiosity and trepidation.

It was not long in coming, and the pattern of its implementation (which I shall outline) has remained 1 constant throughout the many subsequent examinations which have been delegated to me.

I received a call from a gentleman who introduced . . 1 himself as a representative of the Medical Records Office of the Supreme Court.

He was not importunate. 

He called sufficiently in advance so that a date for the examination he wished me to make would be convenient for all parties concerned.

He was (and is to this day) much more considerate and understanding than the average doctor who refers a

Page patient for consultation.

Informally, when necessary, he may appraise me of the particular discrepancies in the medical evidence available which necessitate an impartial examination, or of some specific medical point upon which an impartial opinion is desired.

He makes certain that prior to, or coincidently with the examination, both parties to the action provide the examiner with all medical data in their possession (including x-rays, photostatie copies of hospital charts, doctors' reports, etc.). : Almost invariably the plaintiff is escorted by counsel to the impartial examination. But, contrary to what experience might lead one to expect under such circumstances, none of these counsellors has ever been anything but helpful, considerate and respectful, and it has been a source of some gratification to realize how 1 many plaintiffs' lawyers seem as genuinely interested in their clients' physical and emotional welfare as in the potentialities of their legal position. Consequently, these examinations have been carried out in an atmosphere that has been wholesome, pleasant and devoid of inconvenience or suspicion.

A report is submitted, similar to that which one would make to a referring physician, but also incorporating an adjudication or explanation of real or apparent discrepancies in the medical data reviewed. 

Whenever possible, 1 a firm answer to any specific medical question that has

!

Page 5. been raised is included. 

After reviewing this report, the presiding judge may (upon occasion) call the examiner and informally question him further in order to clarify specific points which bear upon the decision he must make, and which his layman's mind has been unable to resolve. 

Having accepted this assignment as a duty of good medical citizenship, it was a pleasant surprise when the same gentleman who arranged the appointments, also requested that a bill corresponding to regular consultation fees be submitted with each report. 

In a committee report upon the activities of this project (published in book form þy 

MacMillan and entitled "Impartial Medical Testimony") 1 it is stated: "Fees charged by impartial experts for making examinations and reports (not testifying) have ranged from 1 a low $25 to a high of $200. 

The average has been about 1 1 $ 55 per examination."

It is clear from this that the 1 efforts of the impartial examiners have not been entirely 

1 abors of love. 

Finally, there came the day when the gentleman from the Medical Records office called and apologetically informed me that my presence was required in court to substantiate a report which I had made. 

Although this has happened only once in my six years of association with the project, it was it was the day I had feared. 

But, what a difference there was between this and previous court experiences. There was no subpoena. 

I was advised of the dates and probable duration of the trial, and invited to designate the day

Page and the hour at which it would be least inconvenient for me to attend.

Upon my arrival in court at the appointed time the proceedings were interrupted at the first opportunity and my testimony taken without delay. 

The examination and cross-examination were sharp and stimulating, but clean and friendly.

I was, indeed, treated as a friend of the court, and as such, the impact of my testimony was apparent, even to me. This raises the only point about which I have any personal reservation's concerning the propriety of impartial medical testimony.

Medical opinion is, of necessity, predicated upon variable proportions of scientific fact, accepted theory, common sense, and what our legal colleagues disdain as speculation, There are, and will remain, , great voids in medical knowledge, concerning which the most authoritative of opinions must be based upon presently accepted theory and speculation, and about which one opinion, whether partisan or impartial, may be fundamentally as valid as another.

This places a great, and possibly an unwarranted, responsibility upon the court court to "separate the wheat from the chaff," and as great a responsibility upon the impartial examiners to refrain from any exploitation of their status as "friends of the court.

Judge Peck has described the effectiveness of this program in facilitating and accelerating the settlement of claims, and the healthy influence it has had toward

Page 7. reduction in the back-log of personal injury suits pending trial.

The dilentante physician might retort: "These things are benefits which accrue to the legal profession and have no bearing upon medical problems or treatment." 

But, this is not true... Was it not Plato who said (2,000 years ago): "This is the greatest error in the treatment of human sickness that there are physicians for the body, and physicians for the soul, and yet the two are one and indivisible?" 

Only the surgeon "with his head buried in the sand" will fail to recognize that litigation is a most common and patent source of chronic emotional stress which delays and, indeed not infrequently, precludes complete recovery from an injury, and may produce a chronic physical and emotional invalid.

It is not necessary to dwell upon harmful and mischievous influences against recovery recovery so very often exerted by lawyers and litigation. 

It is enough to say that they can be prevented or palliated by any surgeon who is interested in his patient as person as well as problem, if he will only take the time and trouble to do so. 

Neither is it necessary to emphasize the therapeutic benefits inherent in a speedy termination of these intangible adverse influences. 

Therefore, I can do no less than congratulate

Judge Peck and Doctor Craig and their colleagues upon the development of a program which, as I have observed it,

Page 8. 

benefits not only Medicine and Law, but the effects of which also constitute good sound medical treatment. 

I submit that it would not be amiss for national associations of medicine and the law to collaborate toward a geographical extension of this or some similar plan,

I commend this thought to The American Association for the Surgery of Trauma, along with the information that 80 per cent of all the cases in trial courts of the United Stateş are suits for the recovery of damages consequent to personal injuries. 1 1

11

1 

FOR MEDICAL TRUTH IN COURT 

(From the November 7, 1960 issue of the 

Miami Herald, Miami, Florida)

Over the judges' dais in Dade County Circuit courtrooms a sign reads: "We who labor here seek only truth."

The proposal by the Dade County Medical Association to insure impartial medical testimony in personal injury suits is in line with this aim. 

The doctors suggest that a panel of physicians be set up to testify as to the nature and extent of injuries sustained by plaintiffs. They would have no interest in litigants on either side. Their fees would come from public funds and have no relation to the outcome of the case. The only criterion would be making the facts known.

This is as it should be but, unfortunately, is not always the case. As most persons who have served as jurors know, trials of damage suits are often marked by conflicting medical testimony. Expert witnesses flatly contradict each other and the result is to obscure the truth.

A series of articles in The Herald some months ago reported instances of padded medical bills, of doctors whose fees were based on the size of the verdict, of collusion between some doctors and some lawyers. 

To be sure, only a fraction of either profession was involved. But the frauds had widespread consequences, not the least of which was sky-rocketing rates on liability insurance.

Both professions moved to clean house. The DCMA proposal is the direct result of this. It has been under. consideration for two years. It is similar to systems in several metropolitan areas where the medical expert in damage suits is not an interested party but a disinterested agent of justice.

The DCMA has taken the lead to end a system open to abuses. The next step is up to the legal profession and the courts which must approve the plan before it can be implemented. . 1

SIX MONTHS REPORT ON USE OF RULE 20 AND RULE 21 IN THE UNITEV STATES DISTRICT COURT NORTHERN DISTRICT OF ILLINOIS BY THE HONORABLE JULIUS H. MINER TUESDAY, MAY 24, 1960 SHERMAN HOTEL LOUIS XVI ROOM 12 NOON ILLINOIS STATE MEDICAL SOCIETY COMMITTEE ON MEDICAL SERVICES AND PUBLIC RELATIONS

Good afternoon ladies and gentlemen.

The courts have long been concerned with the growing backlog of civil cases brought about largely through the growth of personal injury litigation. Our 

United States District Court adopted Rule 20 on November 3, 1959 which is concerned with Impartial Medical Testimony, and Rule 21 which permits the court to separate the issues in appropriate cases. When Rule 21 18 invoked and there is a finding of "No liability", there 18 no need for medical testimony. 

The District Court has simply adopted a rule formalizing procedure under which the Court may exercise its unquestionable right to call its own witness in the type of case where most thoughtful observers have long recognized the desirability of such a procedure. (1) As Professor Wigmore said "The appointment of disinterested expert witnesses by the Court is one of the expedients employed for reforming the defects of the partisan system of providing such testimony. 

The wide recognition of the clear propriety of such a procedure 18 implicit in the multitude of statutes and rules, in both England and the United States, providing for court-appointed experts (see Wigmore, Evidence 563 N. 7 (3rd ed. 

1940) for a collection of such provisions) and in the use of impartial medical testimony in other federal district courts without any specific local rule of court.

Viscount Kilmuir, the lord high chancellor of Great Britain, said here on 

April 30 at the dedication of the new University of Chicago Law School "That the laws and legal system of a nation must be changed periodically to meet the changing needs of society and it is equally true that the machinery of the law needs overhauling from time to time. New parts have to be substituted for old, new techniques applied and old assumptions reconsidered." 

HISTORY This joint venture on Impartial Medical Testimony had its inception in March of 1958 at which time the Illinois State Medical Society, the Chicago Medical Society and the United States District Court held preliminary talks on ways and means of bringing closer together widely divergent medical reports and professional conclusions on the same plaintiff.

Two years ago this month the Honorable David W. Peck of New York honored the thirty-five judges of the Seventh Circuit, United States District Court by giving them a detailed resume of the need, purpose and results of the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in the Supreme Court of the State of New York. 

At that same meeting the Honorable Otto Wiesley, Chairman of the Industrial Commission of the State of Utah outlined the need, experience and results of the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in Workmen's Compensation and Occupational 

Diseases in Utah. 

(1) See Impartial Medical Testimony (A report by a Special Committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York) Macmillan 1956: Sink, The 

Unused Power of a Federal Judge to Call His Own Expert Witness, 29 So. Calif. L.

Rev. 195 (1956): Peck, Impartial Medical Testimony, A Way to Better and Quicker 

Justice, 22 F. R. D. 21 (1958).

Otto Wiesley said, "We do not claim that the panel 16 always right. We do know that they come as close to the MEDICAL TRUTH as it 18 humanly possible. All elements of bias, prejudice and suspicion are gone. 

In October of 1958, Dr. Howard Reid Craig, Dr. Harrison McLaughlin and the 

Honorable David W. Peck, all of New York, spoke to The American Association for the Surgery of Trauma here in Chicago at the Drake Hotel telling of their experience with the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in the Supreme Court of New York. 

In January of 1959, a meeting was held at which representatives of all the principals involved in the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in Chicago were present. The Honorable Michael Igoe was an observer at this meeting. 

On April 2, 1959, a luncheon and afternoon symposium on Impartial Medical Testimony sponsored by the Chicago Association of Commerce and Industry was held at the Morrison Hotel. Present were three United States District Court Judges from 

Chicago and one from Philadelphia, six judges from the Superior and Circuit Courts of Illinois, representatives from the Illinois State Medical Society, the Chicago Medical Society, the Illinois State Bar Association, the Chicago Bar Association, the American Medical Association, and representatives from many other organizations. 

PROF ES S I O N A L.

E X PERT TES T I F I ERS 

Let me clear up something for you. We keep talking about experts. In the past it was the family doctor who treated the patient, who made the examinations, wrote his reports and testified in court if the case came to trial. 

Nowadays, however, it seems mandatory for both the plaintiff and defendant lawyers to send the plaintiff for treatment, examination and reports to their OWN professional experts. These experts have developed into such PROFESSIONAL TESTIFIERS that they spend practically all of their time making examinations, writing reports and appearing as EXPERT WITNESSES at trial, even though the plaintiff was seen for the first time a few hours or days before the trial (the plaintiff having been under competent care for months or years previously).

Those of us who have had the opportunity of trying a volume of personal injury cases have been somewhat concerned with the caliber of medical testimony which we heard in our courts. We also were aware of the frequency with which a few doctors appeared to testify as experts, and often wondered what factor was responsible for such frequency of appearance. I am aware of the concern which the medical profession has expressed in this regard and have noted its attempts to educate its members in their responsibilities in connection with their testimony in court.

Some of these professional experts claim to be authorities in every branch of the Medical Sciences. In one day these so-called professional experts will testify as an experienced authority in neurosurgery, and within a few hours in another court as leaders in the specialty of orthopedic surgery or gynecology.

These doctors invariably make the same extravagant claims of disability, and practically always delve into the probabilities and possibilities and the dire consequences and sequelae that may subsequently develop. The plaintiff's and the defendant's professional expert witnesses are ordinarily poles apart, and yet these same examinations were made within a reasonable period of one another. 

These are NOT reports made by impartial doctors interested in MEDICAL TRUTH but rather by strictly PARTISAN doctors.

In fact, this method of EXPERT TESTIFIERS has gone so far that if expert Dr. X appears for the plaintiff, it is almost certain that expert Dr. Y will appear for the defendant to counter the claims of Dr. X. This procedure does not establish the medical truth. 

When one of these regular PROFESSIONAL plaintiff or defendant EXPERTS testir'ies, it is not his bedside manner, but his JURY SIDE manner that may count heavily with the jury. The jury in the particular case does not know that Dr. X or Dr. Y 

is a PROFESSIONAL EXPERT TESTIFIER specializing in Medical Testimony and not in medical practice. On the witness stand he presents an ingratiating "Church 

Deacon" attitude, that of the do-gooder, a social minded, ethically sterile, too honest, smooth and sympathetic type who is engaged in a FIGHT for high verdicts, high fees, long drawn out jury trial, and who may have more than a completely honest interest or at least does have a personal interest in the outcome . 

IMPARTIAL 

PANEL MEMBER

On the other hand I believe the Impartial Panel Member is a leader in the medical profession. He has been chosen by the doctors for his known competence, training, established qualifications and specialization and personal disinterest in the case assigned to him.

He has the ability (1) to explore widely divergent opinions of partisan doctors; (2) to make his own examination; and (3) to make a factual, objective and honest report to the Court and the contending lawyers. In other words, IMPARTIAL MEDICAL 

TESTIMONY is a method of exploring divergent contending claims, making a competent examination and a report and arriving at TRUTH and JUSTICE in a calm, unhurried impersonal fashion.

PANEL MEMBERS

One hundred forty-six panel members on twenty-one panels were selected from 4,200 "Votes of Confidence" sent in for these outstanding doctors by the members of the Medical Societies themselves in Cook County. This was the result of wide area nomination, numerous screenings, proposal and final selection, and then contact with the proposed panel member to obtain his willingness to serve in such an impersonal capacity.

Members of the Illinois State and Chicago Medical Societies Committee met with the Judges of the United States District Court for a whole day of discussion in 

October of 1959.

PRO C E D URE As you know, in order to make this examination and his report, the Panel Member Is furnished with copies of all reports and examinations of the plaintiff's and defendant's doctors, as well as all x-rays, laboratory reports and hospital records subpoenaed to the panel member several days before the date set for the examination of the plaintiff. These reports and hospital records are very helpful or enlightening to the panel member who has at his disposal at one time ALL the evidence from both sides. These hospital records are usually very valuable because the history, physical findings and laboratory findings are here recorded before any idea a LITIGATION enters the picture and by disinterested parties. 

The panel member then proceeds to take his own history directly from the plaintiff and to carry out a complete physical examination and arrange for indicated necessary laboratory tests. Therefore, with all the histories, physical examinations and laboratory reports at his immediate disposition, he writes & REPORT based upon ALL the facts as well as his conclusions as to the present status of the case and with special attention to any questions which may have been specifically raised by the court. 

In many

Three copies of the examination and report are sent to the Clerk of Court. These are distributed to the court and one each to plaintiff and defendant lawyers several days before the date set for the RESUMED PRETRIAL hearing. instances the dispute as to the nature and extent of the injuries has been resolved at once, and the case settled. In other cases, the wide divergence of opinion has been brought closer together and the cases settled before or during trial. If the case goes to trial, the PANEL MEMBER may be called as a witness by (a) either party, or (b) the court and cross-examined just the same as any other witness. 

The PANEL MEMBER is qualified by the court when he takes the stand and his position in the case explained to the jury. 

There are many more things I could say with respect to this plan, but summing up its real value, I would say that it provides the courts with expert testimony in appropriate cases, which testimony is given with a desire to produce the medical truth with no corresponding interest in slanting the testimony toward either the plaintiff or defendant. This does not mean that another doctor, equally wellqualified and equally impartial, will not disagree with his findings to some degree, but I repeat, it does provide the court with the type of testimony which it needs for the purpose of providing substantial justice for all litigants. The battle of the experts, each of whom may consciously or unconsciously slant his testimony to favor either the plaintiff or defendant does nothing to contribute toward the achievement of justice. If not restrained by some device such as the use of 

Impartial Medical Testimony, it could continue to grow and result in even greater congestion in our courts with the accompanying injustices which partisan testimony of this type is capable of producing. 

The FEES for examinations and reports and for appearing as a witness are paid on order of the court by either plaintiff and/or defendant. The fees for services have been very reasonable and in accordance with a fee which the panel member

would ordinarily charge in his private practice to a private patient in moderate circumstances.

OBS ER V A I I ONS 

Impartial Medical Testimony does NOT substitute a neutral party for JUDGE or JURY or the capable LAWYER or the qualified PHYSICIAN. It does not take away but adds a rather simple technique of aid in the just adjudication of the difficult case or in cases where an impasse has been reached, or in those where opinions are at extremely wide variance.

Impartial Medical Testimony should speed up pretrial hearings, speed up the actual progress of the disposition of cases and elevate the position and level of dependability of Medical Testimony.

The Plan should help to determine FACTS, search out the TRUTH and reduce the glamorized concepts of disability, damages, and the extent of injury or disease to a sensible level. Justice should be speeded up, settlements arrived at earlier and more equitably. 

SUMMARY 

Bitter, long drawn out trials comprizing wide conflict of medical conclusions concerning opposing litigants has done much to tear down the high regard of the professions and the judges. 

Impartial Medical Testimony removes "shenanigans and horseplay" from litigation and assists in the purposeful honest administration of justice. 44 cases have been settled by Impartial Examinations and report only.

2 cases have been settled by Impartial Examination and Report short of trial or verdict by jury. 14 20 cases are still in the process of PROCEDURE.

cases

I should like to make it clear to you that in adopting Rule 20 there was no disagreement among the judges of our court. We all felt that it was a necessary device to accomplish substantial justice in our courts. We further felt that it was the responsibility of the court itself to provide appropriate means whereby testimony of the highest caliber would be available for the guidance of the court and jury We further felt that this obligation could not be delegated and we, therefore, after a full investigation, decided to adopt the plan. 

The fact that the Court Administrator has seen fit to recommend the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in his first report looking toward reducing the backlog in the state courts, particularly in Cook County, provides added weight toward the part this plan can play in the achievement of substantial justice.

THE RESULTS 

The results of Impartial Medical Examination and Reports to date have been highly SALUTARY. The widespread differences of opinion as to the nature and extent of the injuries have been materially narrowed and the cases settled without going through long, bitter jury trials with PARTISAN doctors.

How did the plaintiff and the defendant fare as a result of these Impartial Medical Examinations and Reports? It was NOT a one way street. The contention of the plaintiff and the defendant have been supported in specific cases by conclusions of the examinations and reports. 

I want to emphasize that it is the medical truth we are after and let the "chips fall where they will." We are not in business for one side or the other. 

Incidentally, I just received a letter from the Honrable Francis L. Van Dusen of the U. S. District Court in Philadelphia where they have been using Impartial 

Medical Testimony for 23 months. In short, their experience has been 61 cases where Impartial Medical Testimony has been invoked. 24 cases are settled by examination and report short of trial.

10 cases (16%) are settled after the doctor testified at the trial.

27 cases are in the process of procedure. 61 cases total.

THE FUTURE 

The judges of the United States District Court, Northern District of Illinois heartily APPROVE OF the use of Impartial Medical Testimony in selected cases. 

We are going to very carefully scan the conclusions of partisan doctors of the litigants, and in every case where the findings are too far apart, Rule 20 will be invoked and the plaintiff sent to an Impartial Panel Member. 

We, the judges of the United States District Court take this opportunity to thank the doctors of the Illinois State Medical Society and the Chicago Medical Society for their help and cooperation in searching out the Medical Truth. Without your wholehearted help at all times, this material aid in cutting down on court congestion and bringing about equitable settlements would NOT and can NOT have been possible 

We thank you.

Section of Judicial Administration 

American Bar Association

 By Honorable Emory H. Niles 

Chief Judge, Supreme Bench of Baltimore

Delivered Before the Section of Judicial Administration of the American Bar Association in Dallas, Texas, on August 28, 1956

I am glad to have the opportunity to appear on this program with Judge Peck in order to address you for a few minutes on the subject of Impartial Medical Testimony. 

But I cannot appear, as he does, as the presiding officer over an established system which has been put into effect and proved its worth. Judge Peck is a modest man; his casual references to his book "Impartial Medical Testimony", published by the Macmillan Company this year, do not do justice to the great achievement which has effected under his leadership by the courts, bar associations, and medical association of New York City. In that book the entire scheme is outlined, and many of the results are given in detail, case by case, showing exactly how it works. I commend that book to all of you. It is short, and when you have finished reading it you will, I think, agree that the New York Plan is a valuable contribution to the administration of justice. 1 1 I cannot, in addressing you here, pretend that in Baltimore we have an established and comprehensive Plan for Impartial Medical Testimony in full operation. The subject has been under discussion for three or four years, and our Plan has been tried out in a number of cases. There has been, however, some opposition to it, and while I believe that the Plan will grow, and be accepted within a reasonable time, I must confess that as of the present the situation could be best described as an emergence from the experimental stage and an entry into the stage of education and acceptance by the Bar. II

As a background for my remarks, I begin with a re-take of that hallowed courtroom drama with which we are all familiar--a drama in two acts: 

Act I. Q. Doctor A, as expert for the plaintiff, to what degree, in your opinion, is the plaintiff disabled ?

A. He is totally and permanently disabled. 

Act 2. Q. Doctor B, as expert for the defendant, to what degree, in your opinion, is the plaintiff disabled ?

A. He is not disabled at all, He could work if he wanted to. 

He could work if he wanted to. He is a malingerer and a loafer.

Expert opinions do not always diverge to the extent of 100%. But opinions expressing differences as great as between 30% and 70% 15% and 85%; or "substantial" and "minimal" are heard every day.

---

What is the reason? There are several reasons. Everyone present knows them as well as I do. (1) Medicine is not an exact science, and honest differences of opinion are normal. (2) Every doctor, like every lawyer, has a constitutional bias which predisposes him in favor of one side or another. (3) Many doctors by. their professional activities in testifying for plaintiffs or for defendants have acquired views modified by their professional experience. But the great and controlling reason is that, in our adversary procedure, there is a tremendous and apparently irresistible urge in a witness to testify for the side that calls him, to resolve doubts in favor of that side, to exaggerate, it may be, in favor of that side, and to absolve one's conscience by "leaving it to the Jury." 

On a lower plane, crass financial interest colors the testimony of a doctor who knows that his bill will not be paid unless the plaintiff recovers. 

On the lowest plane, the expert has a financial stake in the verdict, and is in fact on a contingent fee. 

I hasten to disclaim any intention of attacking the medical profession. Its standards of conduct and discipline are higher, if anything, than those of the lawyer. But just as we lawyers have unworthy representatives in our ranks, so have the doctors, And it is our system of inquiry into truth, rather than the abuses of which some doctors and lawyers are guilty, which I wish to examine, and if possible improve. 

Nor do I attack the adversary system as such. It is a good system, and in many ways the best system yet devised for determination of disputes. But like many "isms" it is not a hard, logical and unbending product of dialectial necessity. It is a practical affair, and it can be improved. As instances of its improvement in recent years, I cite two great developments which have grown up during the professional lives of most of us here--Discovery and Pre-trial. Both have demonstrated that they have great value; and both, in theory, are diametrically opposed to the sharp and unmodified adversary theory, Added to this underlying difficulty, is the distaste which many of our best physicians feel for court proceedings. I live in a great medical center, and I know that while it is easy to get medical expert witnesses to testify for one side or the other, I know also that it is hard to get the best doctors to do so. Doctors are professional men of responsibility and integrity; many of them simply will not subject themselves to the prodding of their own side to exaggerate, and to the imputation of the other side that they are coloring their testimony to promote the financial interests of their patients or themselves. By our system of partisan expert witnesses we have alienated and deprived ourselves of the services of the best, and accepted at the same time criticized, and been shocked by, the performances of the worst. III 1 So much, then for the general picture. I have said nothing new. You are all as familiar with the prevailing conditions as I am. The question is what we can, or are going to, do about it. Judge Peck has told you what he and his colleagues have done in New York. His answer is to devise a system of Neutral or Impartial Medical 

Experts. My answer is the same. The only difference between Judge Peck's system in New York and ours in Baltimore is in method. There is a more basic difference between both these Plans on the one hand and the Minnesota Plan on the other, And I

presume upon your time today because I believe that it is far easier, and may in the end be far better, to do what we have done in Baltimore, than what they have done in either New York or Minnesota. I should therefore like to address myself to two matters in the limited time which I have. The first is the difference between the 

Baltimore Plan and other Plans. The second is a simple outline of how the Baltimore 

Plan actually works. 1 :

There are several ways of approach to the whole problem. Passing over for the moment the question of the power of the court to do anything, the first question is whether we are thinking of a system (a) of adducing reliable evidence, or (b) of disciplining improper conduct in either the legal or the medical profession. Minnesota proceeds, I believe, on the disciplinary theory; New York and Baltimore proceed on the impartial evidence theory. In this respect I believe that New York and Baltimore have the better side, for the Minnesota Plan presupposes that there has already been evil done, and merely tries to prevent more evil in the future. 

It allows one horse to be stolen from the stable before the door is locked. The second and greatest difference between New York and Baltimore is that the Baltimore Plan does not require outside money. Although, as Judge Peck could have told you, the amount required was not great, nevertheless, some outside persons had to be educated and interested enough to put up an initial fund to try the experiment. Now, when it has proved successful, the cost is borne by the taxpayers generally. Under, our Plan the cost is borne by the litigants, like all the others costs of litigation.

The third difference between the New York and Baltimore Plans is that in New York the impartial expert is appointed by a system of rotation, operated impersonnaly, anonymously and without choice by judge or counsel. Under our system, the judge and counsel for both sides have an opportunity to discuss the available experts whose names are on a list chosen by the medical society, and to agree on one satisfactory to both sides. This, in a period of transition and education seems to me to be beneficial. . 11 The four difference, if it is in fact a difference, is that no legislative or executive action was needed. We have no new statutes, no new offices, no new jobs. 

We do not even need new rules of court. All we need is the cooperation of the medical profession, which we have, and the support of the legal profession, both on the bench and at the bar.

IV 

I am of course not familiar with the details of the law of the various States, but I do not believe that such differences are important. Maryland is a common law state, where Law and Equity have not been consolidated; where Code Pleading is anathema; but where, since 1634, there has been a steady development of law which sometimes has been behind and sometimes has been ahead of the progess made in other States and in the Federal Courts. At the present time I should say, on the basis of Mr. Chief Justice Vanderbilt's maps with their black and dotted areas, that we are considerably ahead of the average. But for the power of the Court to appoint 

---

. experts of its own, we have amplc authority both from the period of the early 1900's, sometimes regarded as benighted, down to the present period of modern rules, which makes clear not only that the court has the inherent right to appoint expert witnesses in appropriate cases, but further, that those experts may be of its own choosing (See United Rys. & Elec. Co. vs. Cloman (1908) 107 Md, 681; Purdum vs. Lilly (1944) 182 Md. 612).

I have not examined the cases from other States, but I would suggest that it is highly probable that similar decisions can be found in most of them. 

That the problem of impartial expert testimony has been widely considered is evidenced by the action of the Commissioners for Uniform State Laws, who in about 1936 prepared the "Uniform Expert Testimony Act" which provided that the Court should have power to appoint experts of its own choosing. This Act, the name of which was later changed to the "Model" Act, was approved by the American Bar Association in 1938. Thereafter the American Law Institute in its Model Code of 

Evidence adopted a similar provision. Its later, and present form of "Model Rules of Evidence", the same provision has been retained (rule's 401-410). 

I have not had an opportunity to examine the literature of this subject in detail, but I have found no pronouncement by an eminent legal writer doubting the power of the court to appoint an impartial expert or opposing the idea of doing so in proper cases. 

The important point for us at the moment, however, is that, without any text writer or Model Act, or Uniform Act, or Code or any other authorization, a court has the power, upon traditional and accepted principles to select and appoint an expert to advise the Court or the jury upon matters relevant to the issue in a case before it. 1 The Baltimore Plan was devised by a joint committee of the Maryland and Baltimore City Bar Associations, and the State Medical Society, which bears the name of the Medical & Chirurgical Faculty of Maryland. Our Plan is simplicity itself, although the meetings and explanations as between the two professions required a number of conferences, and proceeded over several months. 

At the request of the legal associations the Medical Society appointed eighteen panels of doctors who were, in the eyes of the doctors themselves, eminent practitioners in the various specialties into which medicine is divided. The specification laid down by the lawyers was a wholly informal one. The lawyers simply said they wanted physicians named to the panels whom the doctors themselves would consult in cases involving members of their own families; whose judgment and standing in the medical community was such as to put them above suspicion of either favoritism or incompetence. As a result the following panels were appointed, consisting of three doctors each: Allergy

Anesthetics 

Blood Cancer

Cardiology

. 

Chest Diseases 

Dermatology 

Genito-urinory Diseases 

Gynecology

Internal Medicine Neural Surgery 

Neuropsychiatry

Obstetrics 

Ophthalmology 

Orthopedic Surgery

Otolaryngology Pathology

Pediatrics Psychiatry Radiology

Surgery The names of the doctors composing the panels, while not aecret, were not published.

When a proper case arises for the appointment of an impartial witness, the judge, usually during a pre-trial conference, telephones to the office of the secretary of the Medical Society and asks the names of the doctors on the panel of, for example, radiology. 

The secretary gives the names to the judge, and the judge makes these names known to the lawyers. At the same time he asks the lawyers if they have objections to any of the three who have been named. If there is objection, the judge, although not compelled to do so, eliminates the doctor so objected to, and proceeds to appoint one of the others. 

The selection can usually be made by agreement, and agreement is important for obvious practical reasons.

HI The judge thereupon communicates with the office of the doctor (who has already, by appointment to the panel, expressed his willingness to act) and informs the doctor or doctors secretary that the court has appointed the doctor as an impartial expert, and that a named patient will appear at the doctor's office at a time to be arranged by one of the lawyers. The judge, of course, in the pre-trial conference has charged one of the lawyers with the duty of making appropriate arrangements as to time and place. 

The judge also requests the doctor to send his report in triplicate to the judge, and upon its arrival he immediately sends one copy to each side, and retains one for his records.

With such great simplicity does the system work, but two important points have not been covered. The first is how the matter starts, and the second is who pays the bill. Experts are not appointed in every case, but only when requested by one side or the other. It is not contemplated that the appointment of such an expert shall be a routine matter, but that it shall be done only in extraordinary cases, where there is great divergence between medical reports or in other special circumstances.

Although we have no formal system of pre-trial in Baltimore, it is a simple matter, when a wide difference of opinion in medical testimony has been disclosed by Discovery procedures, for one lawyer to inform his opponent that he intends to ask for an impartial witness, and that he will do so, in the judge's chambers, at a certain time. For the record, the lawyer desiring the appointment prepares a simple petition requesting it, and if the appointment is made, the judge signs an equally simple order in the following form: .

ORDER 

It is ORDERED by the Superior Court of Baltimore City that be examined by Dr. at such time and place as agreed in upon by counsel. A fee for said examination is to be deposited by the amount of $. with the Clerk of the Court, Counsel are to furnish Dr. ...... with the following data: 1 . 1 If in a given case the attorneys desire formal notice of the application, it can be given in the usual method adopted for other pleadings relating to interlocutory applications. 1 1 The whole matter is discretionary with each judge, Some judges believe that the system is excellent, and favor the appointment of neutral experts; some judges are not so convinced, and believe that the old-fashioned system of allowing the parties to call their own experts is better. The bar is also divided on the subject. i 1 1 The second problem is the question of who is going to pay the bill. A short but somewhat inaccurate answer to this question is that the person who wants the expert examination pays the bills. This in practice is easily arranged. Before signing the order of appointment the judge simply asks the attorney requesting the appointment if he will guarantee the bill, or deposit a sufficient amount with the Clerk to pay the bill. Since the attorney asking for the examination knows that it will involve some expense, he is prepared to say "Yes"'; and the Court, depending upon circumstances, either accepts a letter of guaranty filed by counsel, or requires a deposit of an appropriate sum with the Clerk. No difficulty with forms is encountered here, for the attorney making the deposit simply signs a short order to the Clerk, as follows: -.... -..-- "Mr. Clerk: Please deposit the sum of $. .... delivered to you herewith in the within case, subject to the order of the Court." 

There is, however, no universal rule as to who in the end shall bear the cost. 

The matter is usually left by agreement to the discretion of the Court. 

One of the essentials in the whole system is that the doctor selected knows that he is not being retained by one side or the other, but is being requested to advise the Court itself. In order therefore to be certain that the doctor does not even know who is paying the bill, which usually accompanies the doctor's report, the judge simply signs an order, as follows: 

---

7- "Mr. Clerk: Please pay Dr. .... whose address is ., the sum of $... from the sum heretofore deposited in this case, Judge."

The obvious question, of course, arises as to how much the doctor's bill will be. While this might cause great difficulties, it has not done so so far. If the matter arises, the doctor is asked in the initial communication with him about how much he thinks the prospective examination will cost, and when he has named a figure, the amount to be deposited with the Clerk is fixed so as to cover that amount. Usually, however, both the lawyers and the judge know about what the examination will cost, and it is seldom that the deposit required is more than $100. Conversations with Judges in New York. indicate that they too have not had difficulty in the amount of the doctors' fees, although, as you know, their method of paying them is entirely different. The specialists most frequently involved or orthopedic surgeons, neuro-surgeons and general surgeons, and their charges are not difficult to anticipate. With respect to psychiatrists there is more difficulty, for the problems with which they deal are more obscure, and it is more difficult to foretell the amount of time which they will be required to expend in arriving at conclusions.

The important point that I wish to make here is that it is not necessary to devise a complicated system of administration, legal, clerical, or financial, to put the system into effect or to make it work. VI What have been the results so far? While, as I have said, the Plan has not been used by all the judges, nor is it favored by some lawyers, it has been tried in perhaps a moderate number of cases and, as far as I am able to tell (although I am a biased witness) the Plan has worked well. Although to certain plaintiffs' lawyers the Plan, seems to be a defendant's Plan, it has not turned out to be so, In at least one case, a report by the impartial physician was far more favorable to the plaintiff than to the defendant who had asked for it, with a resultant settlement far greater than the defendant had been previously willing to offer. In another case in which the neutral report did not bring about a settlement, the doctor was required to testify; and although his appointment had been requested by the defendant, his testimony in court was far more favorable to the plaintiff than to the defendant. In some cases a claim made by a plaintiff of a great injury has been regarded by the neutral as excessive. The important thing is that both for the lawyers and for the courts, including the jury, a means has been opened of obtaining evidence as to injuries which is truly impartial. 

No one would contend that the neutral or impartial witness is infallible, but neither, I think, would an open-minded observer deny that the introduction of such impartial testimony into the trial is an improvement upon the situation that prevails without it. 

Some opposition to the Plan has arisen from a misunderstanding as to the status of a court-appointed expert when actually testifying before the jury. Let me emphasize that it is not intended, and is not in fact a case, that the appointment of an expert witness by the court prevents or precludes either party from calling as many other experts as he desires, nor is any party prevented from cross-examination of the expert appointed

by the court in the same manner as any other expert may be cross-examined. It is expected, however, that the fact of the appointment by the court shall be made known to the jury, and in this respect the court-appointed expert is one the same basis as the other experts, for the jury always knows which side has called a given witness. 

There is fear among some attorneys that the appointment of an expert witness by the court will "invade the province of the jury" and may make such a witness a "dictator" who may deprive a deserving litigant of his rights. It may be that the jury will give more weight to the testimony of an impartial expert appointed by the court than to the testimony of a witness called by one of the partisans, but I cannot believe that such an effect would be harmful when it is of the essence of the matter that the impartial expert is in the top ranks of his profession, and that he is chosen by the court and the medical profession itself solely on the basis of his ability to tell the jury the medical truth so far as it can be ascertained. VII case.

As I have said, our Plan has not yet reached the stage at which we can look back and suggest desirable changes on the basis of broad experience. A number of points come to mind in which we may be able to improve the system. One of these is the enlargement of our panels of physicians. At the present time only three names appear in each specialty. This number seems inadequate for some specialties, such as orthopedic surger, and perhaps more than adequate for other specialties, such as dermatology. Another possibility is the appointment of more than one expert in a given "Two heads are better than one" in many cases, even though in other cases "too many cooks spoil the broth." Another possibility is the joint examination made by a neutral in the presence of experts for the opposing sides; in other cases in my experience this has proved a useful device. One point on which the doctors do not seem to be uniform in their practice has to do with the amount of data which they require before making their examination. Some doctors wish to see all the reports that have been made to date; some doctors prefer to see none, and to make their own evaluations without reference to what other doctors have said. So far we have left this matter to the discretion of the individual physician appointed by the court. rrr..T..!

The main problem of the future seems to me to be one of education of both bench and bar. It has taken a long time to educate the bench and bar on the two subjects that 

I mentioned previously, namedly, Discovery and Pre-trial. But just as in those cases their actual use has proved of great benefit after the lawyers and the judges really learned what could be done with them, so in the case of Impartial Medical Testimony I believe that time and increased experience with it will result in great benefits, and will contribute in substantial measure to the solution of one of the most important problems which we have today in our courts.

By HONORABLE DAVID W. PECK 

Member of the New York Bar

Former Presiding Justice Supreme Court of New York Appellate Division First Department

A Way to Better and Quicker Justice By Hon. DAVID W. PECK 

In the cities throughout the nation there is an insistent pressure on the courts to speed up, to shorten and quicken the process of case consideration, and expedite dispositions. So singular is the emphasis on expedition, the handling of a larger volume of cases in less time, that qualitative features of court work are seldom mentioned.

Congestion and delay are the stark facts on the litigating scene. 

They must be faced squarely and dealt with directly. But, at the same time, those concerned with the administration of justice should give thoughtful consideration to the means of improving the quality of the judicial product—increasing the likelihood of reaching the right result at the end of the judicial process. 

Lawyers and judges are natural traditionalists, but surely they must recognize the requirement for new and improved techniques, akin to the advances made in the other walks of life, to meet modern needs for expertise, economy and expedition in the resolution 1. The author is a well-known member of the New York Bar. He was appointed Justice of the Supreme

Court of New York, First Judicial Department, First Judicial District, in 1913 and was subsequently elected for a term ending December 31, 1957. Justice Peck served as Presiding Justice of the Appellate Di vision, Supreme Court, First Ju dicial Department, since 1947. He was not a candidate for re-election upon the expiration of his term and re-entered the practice of law 21 in New York City. He has been actively interested for many years in the improvement of the administration of justice and is a popular speaker before Bar Association groups and frequent participant in legal seminars and panel discus sions. The Publisher expresses its appreciation to Justice Peck for this contribution to Federal Rules 

Decisions and is pleased to make this discussion of impartial medical testiinony available to its readers.

22 FEDERAL RULES DECISIONS 

of controversies. If the flight of commercial litigation from the courts has not sufficiently demonstrated the demand for constructive change, the prospect of personal injury litigation following a like course out of the courts should prompt creative thinking and affirmative action.

The most promising area of litigation in which to concentrate is the personal injury field. Personal injury cases constitute 75% of the litigation in the courts of the country and present one of the most vexing court problems-expert testimony——which peculiarly lends itself to improved handling.

The problem is two-dimensional-how to save on the large part of case and court time consumed in the medical inquiry, and how to secure more reliable guidance for a jury in the medical aspects of a case, which are beyond jury competence and understanding except through the receipt of trustworthy expert advice. 

Under present procedure, where the medical testimony comes from no objective or necessarily qualified source, and only through the hirelings of the parties, partisan experts, medical mouthpieces, the jury is more apt to be confused than enlightened by what it hears. It hears black from one expert, white from the other, a maximizing or minimizing of injuries in accordance with the interest of the source of payment for the testimony. 

An enormous amount of time is spent on the battle of partisan experts, and in the end the jury is unable to detect the medical truth or to pass upon underlying questions of competency and honesty between the medical contenders. Indeed, the witness with the cutivted courtroom manner, rather than with the superior knowledge and greater integrity, may make the best appearance and carry the jury. The premium thus placed on personality and patter is so great that lawyers become more interested in retaining a good testifier than in retaining a good doctor. 

That a jury does not receive altogether trustworthy information and reliable guidance under this purely adversary method of presenting medical evidence through partisan experts is abundantly clear. A sample study of cases in the Supreme Court of New York 

County revealed that in a quarter of the cases there were gross errors in the reading of X-rays and that in over a quarter of the cas es essential tests to warrant a diagnosis were not made. 

It may be that the ultimate decision even of medical questions, in cases which must be tried to completion, will have to be made by a jury. It is certainly in order for the parties to have their own medical advisors and be entitled to call them to the witness stand. 

Still it would seem that the court should not be limited or con-

다​.

Cite as 22 F.R.D. 21 

fined by this partisan process of producing evidence, and that the court should be able to find and produce medical evidence of the highest authority and unquestioned integrity. Objective truth should be sought from a source which carries the best guarantee of trustworthiness. 

It was with these thoughts in mind, five years ago, that the Justices of the Supreme Court of New York County sought a source of reliable expert opinion in personal injury cases. Consultations were had with the leaders of the medical societies and heads of the Bar associations in the community, and there was a joint undertaking of the judges and of the two professions to establish a panel of independent experts, of the highest standing, who would be available to respond to a call from the court for truly expert and objective medical findings on conflicting medical claims. The stated requirement for panel membership was that the doctors should be top authorities in their branches of medicine and men who were not previously identified particularly with the plaintiff's or defendant's side of personal injury litigation. The responsibility of selecting the members of the panel was delegated to a joint committee of the New York Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society.

It was for the court to devise the best means of employing the panel. This was not difficult to do. The problem is essentially one of detecting or selecting the cases in which the panel can be usefully employed and of calling upon the panel at a stage early enough in the case to make its services effective in establishing the medical facts and contributing to a settlement of the case before trial. It is not the desire of the court to use the panel in all or even most cases. Indeed the purpose is to confine its use to those cases where there is a substantial divergence of medical opinion between the parties.

As all cases in the Supreme Court of New York County are put through a pre-trial conference shortly after they are brought, the initial pre-trial conference provides the best means of ascertaining the cases in which the medical differences are of a degree which warrants a call upon the panel. In those cases a reference to a member of the panel in the required specialty is made, and his report after examination of the plaintiff is submitted to the court and supplied to all counsel. An adjourned pre-trial conference is then held in which the case is discussed in the light of the report of the independent expert. As might be imagined, the medical controversy is usually settled on the basis of the report of the independent expert, and if the dispute as to injury is the stumbling block to a settlement, the case is usually settled forthwith.

22 FEDERAL RULES DECISIONS 

The court rule under which the panel was established provides that the independent expert may be called as a witness at the trial by any party or by the court,* His testimony is thus made available to court and jury in those cases which cannot be settled and must be tried.

The most tangible result of the use of impartial medical testimony, which can be statistically expressed, is the settlement experience. Approximately 575 cases have gone through the New 

York referral procedure. Settlements at a pre-trial stage, attributable to the panel procedure, were made in 330 cases. Settlements during trial were made in another 62 cases, while 68 cases were tried to completion. There remain 115 cases awaiting trial or other disposition. It may fairly be assumed that at least half of those cases will still be settled, bringing the total number settled to 75% of the number of cases referred. 

This settlement ratio is not impressive in itself, as it is below the settlement average in the run of cases, but it is very impressive when one remembers that none of these cases could be settled at a regular pre-trial conference, and that they constituted a hard core of the most difficult cases, which by their nature would have taken longer than ordinary cases to try. Thus the settlements effected contributed markedly to the relief of congestion and to the promptness of dispositions. 

Equally or more important, the judges and lawyers concerned with those cases are satisfied that the dispositions made were more 

* The court rule reads as follows: 

"1. There is established in the Supreme Court for the County of 

New York an office to be known as the Medical Report Omce, which shall be in charge of a deputy clerk of the Supreme Court. "2. In any personal injury case in which, prior to the trial thereof, a justice shall be of the opinion that an examination of the injured person and a report thereon by an impartial medical expert would be of inaterial aid to the just deterinination of the case he may, after consultation with counsel for the respective parties, order such examination and report, without cost to the parties, through the 

Medical Report Ollice of the Su preme Court, New York County.

The examination will be made by a member of a panel of examining physicians designated for their particular qualifications by the

New York Academy of Medicine and New York County Medical So ciety. Copies of the report of the examining physician will be made available by the clerk of the Medical Report Olice to all parties. "3. If the case proceeds to trial after such examination and report, either party may call the examining physician as a witness or the trial justice may, if he deems it desirable to do so, call the exam. ining physician as a witness for the court, subject to questioning by any party, but without cost to any party."

Cite as 22 P.R.D. 21 in accord with right and justice than would have been likely if the cases had gone to trial with only partisan medical testimony produced. And in the cases which went to trial, the judges certainly, and it is believed the lawyers generally, found that the impartial experts contributed significantly and soundly to the adjudication,

The impartial expert's role at the trial is perhaps worthy of special comment. All lawyers who have had any experience with the 

New York panel concede the value of the panel in the pre-trial consideration of a case, but a few lawyers seem to feel that the jury should not be exposed to an independent or court-designated expert.

The court has not been persuaded that there is any vice or danger in allowing him to appear. It has felt on the other hand that there is no warrant to shut the jury off from this source of enlightenment. No halo is placed around the independent expert. He is open to full examination and cross-exarnination as any ordinary witness. The court charges the jury that his testimony is not to be accepted or given any particular weight merely by reason of his identification. His professional standing and independence are hardly open to question, but that is not because of his designation, but rather because he is in fact and beyond any doubt impartial and of recognized professional standing. He may be questioned, however, as to the certainty of his conclusions, as to doubts and differences of medical opinion in the area of the injury involved, and his opinion may be otherwise tested, qualified or discredited by an informed cross-examiner.

There is no reason to think that a jury which has the endowed responsibility of weighing evidence and determining the facts, and which has the presumed capacity of performing this function, cannot properly weigh and evaluate the evidence of the independent expert. The chance of jurors being misled by the impartial expert is hardly as great as the chance of error in confining their consideration of medical evidence to the conflicting and confounding views of paid partisans. The concern expressed on this point invites the reminder that a trial is a search for truth, not a game of chance. Whatever legitimate aid a jury may be given in reaching the right result should be forthcoming and not cut off. 

It is interesting to note that in the cases in which the impartial expert was called as a witness at the trial he was called almost equally by the plaintiff and defendant. In thirty-five cases he was called by the plaintiff and in thirty-nine cases by the defendant. In only one case was he called by the court.

---

22 FEDERAL RULES DECISIONS 

A panel of impartial experts, similarly recruited and employed as in New York, has been in use in Baltimore, Maryland, for the past several years. The results have been similarly appraised. . 

The principal difference between the New York and Baltimore plans is that the doctors in New York are paid entirely from court funds, while in Baltimore the doctors are paid by the parties either by agreement, assessment of costs or by direction of the court. 

The budget director and appropriating authorities in New York City are satisfied that the expenditures involved in operation of the plan are a positive economy in saving court time and expense. 

The Section of Judicial Administration of the American Bar As sociation, after studying the operation of the New York and Baltimore plans, reported that "We are satisfied from our observations of the New 

York and Baltimore experience that the introduction of impartial medical experts of the highest standing into those cases where the parties are in dispute as to the medical facts has the following wholesome and beneficial results: (1) It improves the process of finding the medical facts, vastly increasing the likelihood of reaching the right result. (2) It serves to relieve court congestion by bringing about the settlement of many cases which would otherwise have to be tried and which by their nature would entail lengthy trials. (3) It has a prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of medical testimony in court. (4) The modest cost involved in the payment of independent experts is a positive economy in effecting a large saving in court operations." 

The plan and procedure for establishing and employing an independent medical panel may readily be adopted in any community where there is a volume of personal injury litigation in the courts, and where there is a sufficient number of qualified doctors available to constitute a panel.

The first and principal essential of a successful panel program is the selection of experts of the highest qualifications. Such doctors would eschew the role of a partisan and would ordinarily refuse to appear in litigation. They are almost invariably willing, however, to respond to a call from the court as a public and professional duty. It is not difficult, therefore, to recruit a panel, but it is essential that the selection be made by professional bodies on the basis of professional qualifications. 

The panel may readily be employed at either the pre-trial stage or trial stage of a case, but its most effective use is at a pre-trial

-- 
---

Cite as 22 F.R.D. 21 stage. It is therefore recommended that the selection of cases for a panel reference be made at a pre-trial conference at which the medical claims and differences can be detected, and that the findings of the independent medical expert also be taken up at a pre-trial conference with a view to effecting some agreement upon the medical facts and a disposition of the case. 

The manner of payment for the services of the impartial expert is optional. It can be made, as in New York, from court funds, or, as in Baltimore, by some arrangement or division between the parties.

In concluding its report on Impartial Medical Testimony, the 

Section on Judicial Administration of the American Bar Associa tion said: "The establishment of impartial medical panels in the courts in centers of population would be a fruitful undertaking by the bench, bar, and medical profession. Indeed, we can think of no single step that would be as pointed and effective a contribution to the administration of justice as enlisting this aid in the consideration and disposition of personal injury cases."

The House of Delegates of the American Bar Association, at its annual meeting last year, adopted the report of the Judicial Section, recommending the creation of panels of impartial experts under court aegis in communities in which such panels might be effectively employed. It is to be hoped that the recommendation will be picked up and pursued by Bench and Bar in many cities. The successful and satisfactory experience of New York and Baltimore is almost certain to follow.

RESUME OF THE DINNER MEETING OF THE JUDGES OF THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT FOR THE EASTERN DISTRICT OF PENNSYLVANIA AND THE PHYSICIANS OF THE MEDICAL SOCIETY OF THE STATE OF PENNSYLVANIA SERVING ON THE COURT'S  PANEL, AT THE RACQUET CLUB OF PHILADELPHIA ON SEPTEMBER 23, 1959 

2 

Dr. Crane welcomed all present (135) at the meeting and thanked the doctors for being willing to serve on the panel. He discussed the reasons for having the meeting and emphasized the reasons why efforts should be made to improve the quality of medical testimony such as is given in most courts today. 

He then introduced Chief Judge Ganey.

Chief Judge Ganey addressed the meeting and stated that he and his colleagues were most happy and appreciative of the large attendance of doctors. "We had noticed a steady deterioration of medical testimony we had been getting. Judge Peck decided to do something about the medical testimony given in the courts. We now have this Impartial Medical Examination Rule. 

Judge Van Dusen and I were appointed by my predecessor, Judge Kirkpatrick, to make a study of the situation. We had several meetings and formulated our present rule for impartial medical examinations and it has been put into effect in our court. We now feel at liberty, under the rule, to call upon any doctor on the panel to come in to testify or render a report in connection with an alleged injury."

Chief Judge Ganey then explained parts of the Impartial Medical Rule, including methods of payment by the parties of the panel doctor's bill. He then stated, "We are now getting a much better quality of testimony than we ever did before. We feel this is due to the presence of the impartial doctor in the picture. My colleagues and myself have a deep appreciation of the way the plan is working with your help, cooperation and interest and the fact that you have taken time out of a busy life to come here tonight." Chief Judge 

Ganey emphasized that the rule was designed to assist the judicial process in reaching the truth and it had been of material assistance in reaching this aim, both through decreasing the amount of exaggeration by some "professional" court doctors and in encouraging the more able doctors to testify in court. 

Chief Judge Ganey then introduced Judge Van Dusen. 1 Judge Van Dusen explained that objections had been raised as to the legality of the plan in one of the first cases in which it had been used. The objecting attorney sought a writ of mandamus and prohibition in an effort to prevent the use of the plan, but the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit denied the writ and the Supreme Court of the United States refused to review the decision of the Court of Appeals. 2

did not act until about mid-March 1959, many lawyers were unwilling to use the kan until the last three months of the past trial year (March 15--June 15) due to fear that their cases might have to be reprocessed if the plan was declared invalid. For this reason, motions have been made requesting use of the plan in only 38 cases up to September 1, 1959, but it is expected that more use will be made of the plan in the coming year. In 5 of these cases (more than 10%), settlement has already been achieved on the basis of the doctors' reports. In

Judge Van Dusen emphasized that the plan was only used in the cases with difficult medical problems, which are hard to settle before trial, so the fact that the 9 cases mentioned above had been settled was a major accomplishment. 

Judge Van Dusen explained that the following two factors were the major reasons given for objection to the plan: A. K.

The contention that the use of the court-appointed doctor is a denial of the right of trial by jury as guaranteed by the Sixth Amendment of the United States Constitution since 1791, since the jury is apt to be dominated by the court-appointed doctor. 

Experience in New York shows that the juries do not always agree with the panel doctor. Also, the cases decided about 1791 show that the courts at that time were still following the original practice3 of selecting their 4 own expert witnesses, so that use of this procedure does not alter the method of presenting expert testimony used when this amendment was adopted.

The plan is not used where the subject is one on which there is no generally accepted agreement among medical experts. In at least three cases, the motion for appointment of a medical expert from the panel has been denied because the subject matter was not appropriate for appointment of a doctor from the panel.

Also, the plan has been most helpful in its prophylactic effect on the testimony given by medical witnesses in the court in the many cases where it is not used, since every such witness knows that if he "goes overboard," the judge can summon a doctor from the panel to give his view of the matter. For this reason, every doctor is rendering a service, even if he is never called on to make a report. It was pointed out that the greatest demand was on the fields of orthopedics and neurology.

If a nonpanel consultant is used and the case gets to trial, an appellate court would very likely require the entire case to be retried because the trial court had not insisted that all persons making examinations on its behalf be taken from the panel set up by its own rule. Then, about a month later, these attorneys come in for an informal conference with the judge to discuss settlement, Sometimes the partial doctors ask the lawyers to raise a question regarding the report. In New York, in many cases, such questions have been straightened out by having the partial doctors meet with the impartial doctors without the judge being present.

I called in counsel and told them I was worried about it as it was a terrific responsibility and I was going to appoint an impartial doctor-a brain surgeon. 

As a result of this conference, the case was settled on a very reasonable basis, Both attorneys were happy and satisfied."
