CONFERENCE ON  PENN-SHERATON HOTEL PITTSBURGH FRIDAY, MARCH 24, 1961 

The meeting was called to order at 1:30 P. M. by the Honorable 

William H. McNaugher, President Judge, Common Pleas Court of Allegheny County.

Judges McNaugher, Soffel, Smart, Weiss, O'Brien, Duff, Lewis, Brown, Cercone, McKenna, Van der Voort, Weir and Brosky; also

Judge Samuel J. Roberts of the Common Pleas Court of Erie County; 

Doctors J. Everett McClenahan, William F. Donaldson, and 

Glenn O. Smith, of the Medico-Legal Committee, Allegheny 

County Medical Society. and:

Dr. McClenahan introduced Justice Aron Steuer of the Appellate 

Division, First Department, Supreme Court of New York, who is Chairman of the American Bar Association's Committee for Expert Testimony.

Judge Steuer said that there are two medical societies in New York, and the heads of both set up a panel of doctors whom they deemed to be the leaders in their fields. They were to be men with no connections with litigants, who had not been identified with either plaintiffs or defendants. 

The doctors served as a public service, since the fees were far below that which they would normally receive. There were fifteen fields of specialization, with different numbers of doctors in each field. For instance, in 

Orthopedics, where there would be a large number of calls, there was a larger number, while in Gynecology, where the calls would be fewer, there were only two or three doctors.. 1 Judge Steuer mentioned these factors, which are present in the New York Plan, as being essential to the success of any Impartial Medical 

Testimony plan: 

1. The community must have a sufficient number of practicing physicians to supply a group or panel of men who are respected in their profession and who "have no axe to grind." 

2. Where it appears that there will be a contest in regard to injuries at the pre-trial stage, or later, the judge can, on his own motion, request an examination by an impartial doctor. The judge does not know who the doctor will be. A clerk holds the lists and takes the top name from the list in the field designated by the judge. When all the doctors on the list have served, then the clerk starts again at the top, so that no one doctor will be required to devote too much of his time. Both sides send all their medical material to the doctor designated and he examines it together with the patient and makes a report in triplicate, one copy to the judge and one to each of the parties. This report is used at further pre-trial or in the trial.

CONFERENCE ON  PENN-SHERATON HOTEL, PITTSBURGH FRIDAY, MARCH 24, 1961 

The meeting was called to order at 1:30 P.M. by the Honorable 

William H. McNaugher, President Judge, Common Pleas Court of Allegheny County. Present: 

Judges McNaugher, Soffel, Smart, Weiss, O'Brien, Duff, Lewis, Brown, Cercone, McKenna, Van der Voort, Weir and Brosky; also 

Judge Samuel J. Roberts of the Common Pleas Court of Erie County; 

Doctors J. Everett McClenahan, William F. Donaldson, and 

Glenn O. Smith, of the Medico-Legal Committee, Allegheny 

County Medical Society. and:

Dr. McClenahan introduced Justice Aron Steuer of the Appellate 

Division, First Department, Supreme Court of New York, who is Chairman of the American Bar Association's Committee for Expert Testimony.

Judge Steuer said that there are two medical societies in New York, and the heads of both set up a panel of doctors whom they deemed to be the leaders in their fields. They were to be men with no connections with litigants, who had not been identified with either plaintiffs or defendants. 

The doctors served as a public service, since the fees were far below that which they would normally receive. There were fifteen fields of specialization, with different numbers of doctors in each field. For instance, in 

Orthopedics, where there would be a large number of calls, there was a larger number, while in Gynecology, where the calls would be fewer, there were only two or three doctors,

Judge Steuer mentioned these factors, which are present in the New York Plan, as being essential to the success of any Impartial Medical 

Testimony plan: 

1. The community must have a sufficient number of practicing physicians to supply a group or panel of men who are respected in their profession and who "have no axe to grind. 

2. Where it appears that there will be a contest in regard to injuries at the pre-trial stage, or later, the judge can, on his own motion, request an examination by an impartial doctor. The judge does not know who the doctor will be. A clerk holds the lists and takes the top name from the list in the field designated by the judge. When all the doctors on the list have served, then the clerk starts again at the top, so that no one doctor will be required to devote too much of his time. Both sides send all their medical material to the doctor designated and he examines it together with the patient and makes a report in triplicate, one. copy to the judge and one to each of the parties. This report is used at further pre-trial or in the trial.

3. Either side or the court may call the doctor as a witness at the trial. The fact that he is a court-designated doctor is made clear to the jury if he testifies. He can be cross-examined by either side. 

As time goes on, the plan is decreasingly used. That is a good thing, because it prevents exorbitant medical claims being made, as it becomes known that any such claim is to be met by competent testimony. 

It usually goes in cycles, because the prophylactic effect wears off, and you have to start over again after an interval. The great benefit of the system is that it prevents exaggerated claims being made. 

Judge Steuer said a number of problems are encountered: 

1. There is the usual cross-examination as to the uncertainty of medical diagnosis and differences of opinion that can be legitimately entertained by doctors. That gives rise to the "two schools of thought" proposition. Actually, there is very little in the field of medicine on which there are two schools of thought, but in any particular case there may be room for differences of opinion.

Where there is a bona fide difference of opinion in the profession which can be truly described as two schools of thought, no question is presented in regard to impartial testimony. In every case the plaintiff must prove his claims which includes proving the claim as to which there are two schools of thought.

As the claim is necessarily one of theory - as for instance that a certain physical condition or disability can be of traumatic origin plaintiff must show that the belief is held with reasonable certainty. If there are two recognized schools of thought no honest doctor can be reasonably certain that the school he favors is correct in its view. All he can say is that in his opinion that view is preferable; so, there being no reasonable certainty, the point cannot be established.

If the question of which school of thought is correct is allowed to go to the jury you are in effect saying that a question which the medical profession has been unable to solve over the centuries is to be answered by twelve men whose sole qualification is their entire ignorance of the subject. 

2. When a court appoints a doctor it is very difficult to contradict his testimony. That is so, and it should be so the very stature of the doctor has that effect, even though he is not called by the court.

12 1 (Judge Steuer commented at this point that his colleagues at New York had inquired whether he intended to speak at this meeting on any of the techniques they had evolved for the control of calendars. He said, "Although I was not asked to talk on that subject, I am prepared if you would like such information".)

The meeting continued with questions by various officials present, and answers by Judge Steuer:

Judge Weiss: Was there much objection from the Bar when they started the plan?

A. NACCA objected as they do in every jurisdiction, but in 

New York they are not well accepted in the profession and their objections didn't carry much weight.

Judge Van der Voort: Is there much trouble getting doctors' reports from both sides?

A. None whatever, because even if there is no impartial medical witness we have a system that a few days after the joinder of issue on request each side must supply the other with all their medical reports. They have to do it or their case is not put on the calendar. 

Judge Brosky: We have a proposed draft of the rule which refers to the fact that he may be called for one side or the other, Lawyers I have spoken to have objection to that phase of it - what if he switches over from plaintiff to defendant?

A. He never switches from one side to the other. If one side calls him they inform the court they would like a doctor to testify. When he is sworn the jury is told of his connection with the case and he is examined by either the side that requests him to be called, or by the side the court designates, then either side may cross-examine. 

Judge Brown: Did you do this in New York without legislation?

A. Yes. 

Q. How do you charge the jury? We charge that the plaintiff has the burden and the witnesses of the plaintiff would be those he calls, not what the court calls.

A. Yes, that is so, you have to explain that the doctor was called for the assistance of the court that does not mean his testimony must be accepted, but it is to be considered in the light of the facts and of his relationship in the case.

Q. 

On appeal whose testimony is it? A. 

Neither sides' testimony. Q. 

How does it become evidence ? A.

It is testimony given at the request of the court. 

Judge Soffel: It is stated that it is the court's testimony? A. 

Yes, that's right. 

Judge Weir: Who pays for it in New York ?

The pi A. It is paid for by the State in all cases. On the question of fees, the doctors are told before they are put on the panel that they can charge whatever they deem proper, but what we deem proper is somewhat less than those doctors would command either for examination or for their time at court. pose of that is this we don't want it to become a source of income for any doctor. We want it to be a public service, but not too onerous. When the doctor submits his report he submits his bill, which must be approved. Only one doctor ever sent in a bill that we thought was excessive in the light of what he was supposed to charge. In that instance we paid the bill and took the doctor off the list. 

Judge Cercone: Since there might be three doctors instead of only two, does this system expedite the trial of cases?

A. It does not expedite the trial of the particular case in which the panel doctor testifies. But it does expedite the calendar as a whole by doing away with testimony where there is no genuine controversy, and also by disposing of a great number of cases through settlement by resolving the issue of damages.

Judge Weiss: If it is a question of getting funds from the State approximately what would a doctor want - what is reasonable in New York? 

A. The usual fee for examination is between $35 and $50. If the doctor testifies - we generally arrange it so he doesn't have to wait - he gets about the same amount more. Originally the Sloan Foundation and the 

Ford Fund financed the first two years of the operation and after that the State took it over.

The same as paying the jury?

A. 

Yes, that's right. 

Judge Brosky: Why an "impartial expert" ? Is he impartial? Why should a court doctor be permitted to testify for one side or another? 

A. He is not testifying for either side. He may be called at the request of either side, but he is not their witness, Judge Weiss: Dr. McClenahan, would your panel work for about the same price as the New York doctors? No doctor who has asked to be put on it has been named. 1 Judge Roberts: If a member of the panel has testified in any other litigation is there nothing that restricts him? 

A. (By Judge Steuer): The panel is made up of men who have not so testified.

Judge Roberts: Assuming that he was a member of a court panel, and that was made known to the jury? 1 A. Your question refers to a doctor who has been appointed to the panel and subsequently testifies in cases in which he was not designated by the court, and his qualifications include reference to the panel. I have never seen such an instance. Of course, if a panel doctor was the actual treating doctor he would probably have to testify. While our rule does not in terms prevent a panel doctor from acting as an expert in cases in which he was not designated by the court, I know that our panel doctors make it a point to refuse such retainers and if an instance arose I am quite sure that a doctor who did so testify would be removed from the panel. 

Judge Brown: Suppose the court wants this expert and neither plaintiff nor defendant are interested and neither will examine him: Who examines him?

A. 

That problem hasn't arisen.

TE

Q. I think it would arise here. We have a strong rule about a judge interfering with the trial. 

A. As the question has not come up, I can not answer by relating the way the situation has been handled. It is hard to see how the question would arise because the report would naturally sustain the position of one side rather than the other, as these contentions are opposed. Or at least, it would support a part of one side's claims, and that side would examine the doctor. It is conceivable that a report would contain matter that neither side would want, and neither would choose to examine. The court could then ask the doctor to read his report, and then let both sides crossexamine.

Dr. McClenahan: In any rule you consider adopting, be sure that the rule states very definitely that the impartial witness must be identified in court as the court's witness. The lack of that will destroy the effectiveness of the whole thing. If nobody knows he is the court-appointed witness, to the jury he is just one more doctor, which would only confuse the issue. 

Judge Steuer: You will find that the ratio of times in which an impartial doctor testifies to the number of times he makes an examination is very small. Out of every 100 examinations you won't find more than 4 times he will be asked to testify. The other cases will be disposed of without trial, or the conflict in medical opinion will be abandoned. 

Judge Weiss: They have this rule in Philadelphia, don't they?

A. 

Yes, in Federal Court.

Judge Weir: One of the objections I have heard stated is that 

Pittsburgh is a heavy industrial district and that many of the doctors tend to be employed by corporations. I don't know whether that is correct or not. 

Dr. Donaldson: This is not true not a man on the panel has any connection with any company here in town or in the area. 

Judge Weiss: How much money is appropriated by the State of 

New York for this?

A. The original appropriation was $25,000 and now it is a part of the court budget. It has continued to be $25,000, but we have never spent anything near that in any one year. In fact an appropriation of $15,000 would be ample.

Judge Weiss; I reached my conclusion at the last meeting - the only question is who pays for it. I am pleased to have heard Judge Steuer express himself that it ought to be paid for by the government. I don't think you should put it on either side.

Judge McKenna: It would save so much in jury fees. 

Dr. McClenahan: I want to take this occasion to thank the judges for being so kind as to attend this meeting and to thank Judge Steuer for coming. It was wonderful to have you, Judge, and we are very grateful to you. 

Judge McNaugher expressed for the court sincere thanks for the presentation of the subject. He said we have a heavy backlog of cases and he was sure that it would be cut back very definitely with this system. 

He also thanked the doctors for taking charge of this luncheon and having such a splendid speaker come before us to present the matter, The meeting adjourned at 2:45 P.M. Secretary

THE NEUTRAL MEDICAL EXPERT 

Ву 

WILLIAM F. SMITH 

This paper was delivered at a joint meeting of the Middlesex County Medical Society and the Middlesex Bar Association on January 18, 1961. The writer was the Chief Judge of the United States District 

Court for the District of New Jersey, and a member of the Court for twenty years. He has been elevated recently to the United States 

Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit. 

1 1

.)

The subject assigned for discussion is "The * * Appointment of the Neutral Medical Expert" in personal injury litigation.

There are two basic plans under which such appointments are made by the trial judge in the APPROPRIATE cases,

There are embodied in each essential provisions common to both, and the plans differ only in the procedural aspects.

These plans have provoked considerable controversy, particularly among the members of the bar and bench; they have received the enthusiastic support of their proponents and the forthright criticism of their opponents, These medical plans are, nevertheless, worthy of thoughful consideration by the members of the legal and medical professions and the members of the bench.

We have a common interest in a serious problem which confronts the courts and a joint obligation to arrive at a feasible solution in the public interest. 

It is generally recognized by the members of the professions that the present method under which expert medical testimony is presented in the trial of an action has led to many abuses and to justifiable criticisms.

Since 1937 there has been an everincreasing demand for some reform.

The members of the professions must recognize this demand and willingly undertake the task.

We must also recognize that, unless we do, the * The term "Neutral Medical Expert" is preferred to the term "Impartial Medical Expert." 

The medical expert can be rightfully expected to be neutral as between the litigants; however, he may be the partisan of an opinion fairly and honestly entertained.

"meddlers", lacking the experience and necessary qualifications, will undertake it for us with troublesome results, It is with these considerations in mind that I present to you for frank and open discussion: the essential features of the plans, the claims made for them by their advocates, the criticisms urged by their adversaries, and a few observations of my own. 

RECOMMENDED PLANS 

We shall first direct our attention to the essentials of what I shall identify as the "model plan." They are embodied in Rules 403, 407 and 409 of the Model Code of (1) 

Evidence, patterned after Sections 1,2,4 and 9 of the Uniform (2) Expert Testimony Act. It should be noted that these essentials are also included in the proposed revision of the Law of (3) Evidence submitted to the Supreme Court of New Jersey.

Appointment of Neutral Expert 

Where it appears that an independent examination of the injured person and a report thereon may be of material aid to the just determination of the medical issues, the trial judge may, after notice to and consultation with the counsel 1 for the respective parties, appoint a neutral medical expert to make the necessary examination and report thereon to the court.

The report is open to inspection by either party. 

Where the parties agree on the selection of a medical expert the nominee selected must be accepted by the trial judge. 

If the parties cannot agree the trial judge may appoint the medical expert.

The Rule clearly reserves to counsel for the respective parties a right to submit nominations. 

The appointment of the neutral medical expert may be made either on the motion of a party or the motion of the -2-

court. these motions will not be necessary in those cases in which there is an adequate pretrial conference; in fact, this has been the experience in those courts in which medical plans are in operation. 

The need for the neutral medical expert may emerge at the pretrial conference upon proper exploration of the medical issues, A careful examination of the medical reports then available will frequently disclose the need for such appointment, A consul- tation with counsel for the respective parties will frequently lead to the solution of the problem. 

Neutral Expert as a Witness 

A neutral medical expert may be called as a witness by either party to the action or the court. 

He may be examined and cross-examined in the same manner and to the same extent as any other medical witness. 

The Rule requires, however, that the role of the neutral medical expert as an APPOINTEE 

OF THE COURT be made known to the jury. 

This provision has been criticized by members of the bench and bar and their (4) criticisms are not without merit. We shall discuss these criticisms at a later point in this paper. 

Hypothetical Question

The Rule included in the "model plan" does not prohibit resort to the hypothetical question as an effective means of eliciting the opinion of the medical expert. 

It does, however, eliminate the necessity for the hypothetical question, and vests in the court the discretion to prescribe the manner in which such a question shall be used, The medical expert may "state his opinion and reasons there for without first specifying the data on which it is based." 

The medical opinion -3-

thus stated is subject to inquiry on cross-examination and the witness may be required to specify the data upon which it is based.

It was observed by Professor McCormick that this expedient "does not lessen the partisanship of the question or the answer, but it does greatly simplify the examination, and removes the occasion for imperilling the judgment by mistakes in the form of the hypothetical questidk). We are inclined to agree with his observation.

Protection of the Parties 

The essentials of the plan provide at least a measure of protection for each party: "(a) 

The court may not appoint an expert until after notice and, if the parties agree, must appoint the person selected by them; (b) each party may call other experts but only after reasonable notice to the adversary and to the court; (c) each party may be represented at any * examination by an expert * ; (d) where the *, the report is open to court requires a written report (6) inspection by (either) party.' 11 

Selection of the Expert

The plan thus far outlined is in operation in the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, the United States District Court for the 

Northern District of Illinois, and the Baltimore city courts.

These courts maintain a panel of qualified specialists established by the recognized medical societies and, as is the case under the Baltimore plan, a panel established by the Medical and Chirurgical Faculty of the State of Maryland. 

The neutral medical expert is selected from these panels which are open to inspection by .counsel for the respective parties. -4-

Selection of the Expert Under the New York Plan 

The essentials of the "model plan" are embodied in the New York plan with one exception. 

The neutral expert is selected from a panel jointly established by the New York 

Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society. 

However, the parties to the action have no voice in the selection of the neutral medical expert.

He is selected by a "process of strict rotation" under the supervision of the (7) Medical Report Office. It is our opinion that this method of selection is objectionable for reasons hereinafter discussed. 

ADVANTAGES OF MEDICAL PLANS 

The proposals embodied in the medical plans were under consideration by the House of Delegates of the American Bar Association for at least two years prior to its annual meeting of 1957.

It was at this meeting that they approved a "national program, * * *, of fostering the creation and employment of panels of impartial medical experts" in personal (8) injury cases.

This resolution was based upon an earlier report of the Committee on Impartial Medical Testimony in which the "following wholesome and beneficial results" were claimed: "1.

It improves the process of finding the medical facts, vastly increasing the likelihood of reaching the right result. 2.

It serves to relieve court congestion by bringing about the settlement of many cases which would otherwise have to be tried and which by their nature would entail lengthy trials. -5-

3. 

It has a prophylactic effect upon the formulation and presentation of medical testimony in court. 4. 

The modest cost involved in the payment of independent experts is a positive economy in effecting a large saving in court operations."

These are generally the advantages claimed by the proponents (9) of the medical plan. It is further claimed that the medical plan is designed to correct the "abuses which have developed (10) since experts have come to be witnesses for litigants.'

NEED FOR REFORM We have heretofore stated that it "is generally recognized by the members of the professions that the present method under which expert medical testimony is presented in the trial of an action has led to many abuses and to justifiable criticisms.

We must admit that there is a need for some reform.

We are confronted, however, with a narrow question: 

Is the medical plan, heretofore discussed, the solution to the problem? It is the opinion of the proponents of the plan (11) that it is.

They appear to have the support of respectable (12) authorities who have given careful consideration to the problem. 

It must be admitted that the proposals embodied in the medical plan have many advantages; in fact, this is admitted by several (13) of the severest critics. There are admittedly some weaknesses inherent in the proposals, but are they such as to warrant their total discard? 

We think not. 1 -6- 1

Neutral Medical Expert Not Required 

There are many cases in which the services of the neutral medical expert are not required, and these are admittedly in the majority.

This is conceded by the proponents of the plans,

There are many cases in which the nature and extent of the plaintiff's injuries are not seriously disputed, and his right to recover rests upon the ultimate determination of the issue of liability.

There are other cases in which the nature of the plaintiff's injuries is conceded, but in which there is a justifiable difference of medical opinion as to their extent and consequences.

There may be in these cases issues of liability as well as medical issues. 

These cases present no problem. They offer no serious barrier to settlement or to the fair presentation of medical evidence at the trial. 

These cases contribute to the problem of congestion but only because of their number.

The problem of congestion may be substantially alleviated in these cases by the proper employment of the pretrial conference as soon after issue joined as the calendar of the court will permit. 

There are other cases in which the services of the neutral medical expert may not be required. 

These are cases in which the medical proof rests upon the testimony of a general practitioner selected by the patient and a specialist selected to aid in treatment,

If there is any measure of partisanship in their testimony, it can usually be detected upon careful cross-examination.

The defendant, of course, must rely upon the testimony of a medical expert, usually selected by the lawyer.

The weaknesses in his testimony may likewise be detected upon careful cross-examination. -7-

It is my opinion that any abuse which might arise can be readily detected at the pretrial conference and upon the examination of the medical reports. 

In fact, the exchange of medical reports in advance of the pretrial conference is in and of itself a corrective procedure.

The members of the medical profession can insure the efficacy of this corrective procedure by the careful preparation of fair and honest reports. 

These cases present no trial problem for either the 

Court or jury.

A qualified juror is sufficiently competent to determine not only the issues of liability but also the medical issues.

His competence is usually sufficient to enable him to evaluate the justifiable differences of medical opinion and to arrive at a fair and just result. 

The result may represent a compromise but this may be expected where the differences in medical opinion are justifiable.

Neutral Medical Expert Required 

There is, however, a third class of personal injury litigation in which appointment of a neutral medical expert may be advisable.

These are the cases in which there is a wide divergence of medical opinion and an equally wide divergence of opinion as to the value of the case. 

The medical experts may agree as to the nature of the injury but sharply disagree as to the extent of its consequences.

They may agree as to the nature and extent of the injury but entertain widely divergent medical opinions as to causation. 

There are, of course, other situations.

These cases offer insurmountable barriers to fair settlements.

The plaintiff, conditioned to the expectation of a large award by the "medico-legal" opinions, finds it difficult to consider any offer of settlement which fails to meet his demand. 

The defendant, usually represented by an insurance carrier, assured as to the merits of the defense, and likewise by the -8-

"medico-legal" opinions, frequently finds it difficult to see "any merit" in the plaintiff's claim. 

These cases are relatively few in number, but they represent the "hard core" of the congested civil calendar. 

They inevitably must be tried.

The justifiable criticisms which have been directed to the traditional method under which medical testimony is presented, is ascribable solely to these cases. 

They raise an almost insurmountable barrier to the fair and just determination of medical issues.

We must admit that the medical experts selected by counsel for the respective parties are usually competent specialists. 

Their selection, however, is frequently dictated not by their competence but by their effective courtroom manners.

This is true whether the medical expert is selected by counsel for the plaintiff or counsel for (14) the defendant, 기 ​The wide divergence of medical opinion frequently leads to what is commonly termed "the battle of experts." Each of the medical witnesses, after stating in detail an impressive list of his qualifications, presents his testimony with confidence and assurance.

The efforts of trial counsel to discredit his opinion ultimately leads to an acrimonious exchange between the witness and the examiner. 

As the cross-examination increases in severity the acrimonious exchange becomes more acute and, when this occurs, the medical witness becomes a medical advocate.

The contentious atmosphere is conducive to exaggeration.

The result is a rather "unwholesome spectacle" which reflects adversely not only on the members of the profession but on the integrity of the adversary system of trial. 

We might add that because of this "unwholesome spectacle" many competent physicians are reluctant to appear in court. -9-

What of the dilemma of the jurors? They are per- 7 fectly competent to evaluate the factual testimony and determine the issue of liability.

They must, however, determine also the medical issues. 

There are presented to them two diametrically opposed medical opinions, but they nevertheless are expected to arrive at a fair and just determination of these issues. 

They are confused and look to the trial judge for guidance, but, after the "battle of wits" has been concluded, even the experienced trial judge finds himself in no better position then they are.

If the trial does not reult in a disagreement, the verdict may be the result of mere guesswork. 

There is also a serious aftermath; the jurors may leave with an understandable distrust for the administration of justice. 

General Criticism of Medical Plan 

There are some critics who charge. generally that the medical plan will impair the traditional adversary system of trial.

The available evidence does not support this charge. 

It must be remembered that the adversary system is a sober and logical search for truth; it is neither a test of "medicolegal wits" or a game of chance. 

It is my personal opinion that a feasible medical plan, judiciously administered, will eliminate some of the abuses that have arisen and will thereby improve the adversary system of trial,

SPECIFIC OBJECTIONS 

The opponents of any medical plans have urged several reasons against their adoption. 

There are many who have voiced their opposition, but the grounds upon which the opposition is based are clearly set forth by Mr. Emile 2. (15) (16)

Berman and Mr. Harry Gair in a panel discussion conducted by -10-

the Insurance Section of the American Bar Association, by 

(17) Mr. Justice Walter R. Hart of the Supreme Court of New York (18) and by Mr. Elwood S. Levy. A fair appraisal of the proposed medical plans requires consideration of the criticisms urged by the opposition.

There appears to be general agreement that the employment of the neutral medical expert is conducive to settlement even in the difficult case. 

It is argued by some lawyers, however, that these settlements are ascribable to the apprehension by one party or the other that the neutral medical expert will be called as a witness. 

There are some proponents of the medical plan who subscribe to this view. 

I cannot agree that the argument is entirely true. 

There is clearly another factor which seems to be overlooked. 

Where the plan permits the representatives of the litigants a voice in the selection of the neutral medical expert, it is reasonable to assume that the lawyers, guided by the professional advice of their medical experts, will agree on a competent physician whose professional opinion will be respected.

It is my thought that respect for the judgment of the neutral medical expert will tend to influence the settlement negotiations,

This is particularly true if the medical experts will employ the technique of consultation, the technique in common use by physicians in their private practices. There appears to be no reason why the same technique cannot be employed successfully in the field of personal injury litigation. 

Neutral Medical Expert as a Witness 

The medical plan is criticized generally as a curtailment of the traditional adversary system of trial. 

It is argued that the neutral medical expert called as a witness, -ll-

either by a party or the court, encroaches upon the exclusive function of the jury.

It is further argued that the jurors, confronted by conflicting medical opinions, would be inclined to accept as valid only the medical opinion of the neutral expert, and this because of his appointment by the court. We admit that there is some merit to these arguments, but we cannot admit that they warrant a total condemnation of the medical plan.

It is seriously contended by the critics that the most serious vice inherent in the proposed medical plans is the right of either party, or the court, to call the neutral medical expert as a witness. 

It is argued that: "Once the impartial expert's status is disclosed to the jury and it is made clear that he was appointed by the court and selected for his outstanding abilities, * * * even an experienced trial counsel" could not effectively cross-examine "the court's . designated arbiter * * * and this even though the impartial's (19) opinion may be wrong. This argument likewise has some merit, but it does not warrant total condemnation of the medical plan. 

Answer of the Proponents

The proponents of the medical plan readily admit the weakness upon which these criticisms are based. 

They argue, however, that the adverse effect which may stem from the presence of the neutral medical expert as a witness may be eradicated by proper instructions to the jury. 

This remedy may be only partially effective.

We might examine the brief essentials of a jury instruction directed to the problem. -12-

"The plaintiff and defendant have called as witnesses 

Dr. Cramp and Dr. Milano. 

You will recall that the former has testified on behalf of the plaintiff and the latter has testified on behalf of the defendant. 

You, as the sole judges of the issues of fact, are the sole judges of the credence and weight to be accorded their testimony. 

You may consider in the determination of these issues: the circumstances under which each witness has testified, his relation to either the plaintiff or defendant, his demeanor on the stand, the extent to which his testimony is corroborated or contradicted, and such other facts, as in your judgment, may have influenced his testimony. "The Court has called as a witness one Dr. Bishop who has been selected from a panel recommended by the Middlesex County Medical Society. 

You are the sole judges of the credence a nd weight to be given his testimony. 

You may consider the circumstances under which he has testified and the fact that he had been appointed by the Court as a neutral medical expert."

You will note that in these instructions there are presented to the jurors two factors which may prove persuasive, to wit; first, the selection of the neutral medical expert from a panel established by a recognized Medical Society, and second, his appointment by the Court to serve in the particular case. 

I entertain some doubt that these factors can be completely eradicated by proper instructions particularly where there is a wide divergence of medical opinion. 

The criticism, although apparently warranted, does not require complete abandonment of the medical plan. There are possible solutions.

It appears from the available statistics that the neutral medical expert is called as witness infrequently, -13-

and when called, is called as frequently by the plaintiff as by the defendant. 

Where the neutral medical expert is called as a witness by either party to the action, he should enjoy no better status than the medical experts engaged by the litigants. 

The emphasis of his status as a "neutral medical expert appointed by the Court" should then be eliminated from the jury instructions.

There are other possible solutions, but the necessary limits of this paper will not permit our exploring them.

Divergence of Opinion School of Thought 

It is urged by some critics of the medical plan that medicine is not an "exact science" and, therefore, 'does not lend itself to certainty in the diagnosis and prognosis of personal injury.

This criticism is only partially valid. 

We must admit that in some fields there is a wide divergence of medical opinion as to the causal relationship between trauma and injury. There are many physicians who agree that prolonged (20) exposure to chemical or physical trauma may produce cancer. 

They may, however, disagree on the causal relationship between a single exposure to trauma and the aggravation of a pre- (20) existing cancer. There are other fields of medicine in which wide divergence of opinion is generally recognized, but these fortunately are in the minority.

The criticism overlooks the fact that in many areas the medical knowledge is sufficient to permit reasonable certainty in diagnosis and prognosis. 

The criticism does, however, point up a weakness in the New York Plan. 

You will remember that under this plan the neutral medical expert is selected under a strict method of rotation.

This method of selection introduces an element -14-

of chance if the neutral medical expert is called as a witness at the trial of the action.

The outcome of the trial may very well depend upon the adherence of the neutral medical expert to a particular "school of thought." 

This obvious defect, inherent only in the New York Plan, is overcome by the rules included in the "model plan." 

These rules provide that a neutral medical expert shall be appointed only after consultation with counsel for the respective parties.

It is my thought that in this situation *R***** ETTET en."" the conscientious lawyer would look for guidance to the medical expert selected by him.

There is an additional safeguard, to wit, the right of the trial judge to appoint more than one neutral expert.

It is my conclusion that after some experience with the medical plan we can probably overcome its few weaknesses. 

The members of the professions, law and medicine, should not admit defeat and abandon the plan. -15-

Forensic Medicine Pierre

The Judge Looks at the Impartial Doctor 

ARON STEUER 

New York

DOCTORS uy witnesses are exactly the same us doctors regarded as people. They have the same attributes in the courtroom as they have outside of it. The vast majority are honest and know what they are talking about. A some. what smaller majority are convincing in their presentation. This is the credit side. On the debit side is that few of them are disinterested or objective. The physician finds himself in court due to one of two relationships; He has cither treated the plaintiff professionally or has examined him for the purpose of testify. ing. In the first situation there is a personal relationship of varying intensity. But in al. most every case there is some degree of in. terest in the patient's welfare. In addition, the more serious the injury, the greater the skill the physician demonstrates in the result; or where, despite the treatment, the result is unfortunalc, the more easily is it explained by a grievous condition following the acci. dent. Doctors who are uninfluenced by these considerations may sometimes be found, but it is rare.

The medical man who testifies but does not treal is likewise subject to influences which affect his objectivity. Sometimes these are financial. An extreme example once came to my attention. An eminent specialist in the winter of his career accepted a very substan. tial retainer from a labor union. It enabled him to give up the bulk of a large practice which his years were making somewhat of a burden. This ideal situation soon proved il. lusory. The doctor believed he was being re. tained to advise on conditions in the industry as affecting the health of the workers. He soon found out that his chief occupation was to substantiate claims for workmen's compensation and to lend the prestige of his well. earned reputation to their support. And he discovered that he could be as impartial or objective as he wished, just so long as he found that the union member's condition was a result of an industrial accident. Although he found it out soon, it was nevertheless lou late, and the twilight of his career was filled with extraordinary conclusions based on very ingenious theories.

The nel result is that too often in the trial of a case involving medical questions, the

Instead, it is a correct praluation on the evi. dence presented. While often parallel, these concepts are distinct and different. Ours is in instance of the procedure shaping the concept. We have the adversary system in which each contestant presents his claim and the arbiter decides not on what he knows or can learn for himself, but on what is presented to him. Incidentally, this system is basically the concept of the free man --a distrust of all officialdom. To the first proposition, the answer is that when there are such variations of opinion. cach woll backed by solid protagonists, the impartial expert would be the first to admit it.

THE MEDICAL REPORT OFFICE SUPREME COURT, 1st DISTRICT 

Since December 1, 1952, Justices of the Supreme Court in New York County have been provided with a new and valuable auxiliary service to assist them and their juries in the determination of disputes as to the extent of damage in the many personal injury cases that clog their dockets. This facility, which centers in what is known as “The Medical Report Office”, was first established on an experimental basis under the aegis of a special committee of the Association of the Bar of the City of New York, financed by a grant from the Alfred P. Sloan Foundation and the Ford Motor Company 

Fund and created with the assistance of a joint committee of the 

New York Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical 

Society. Thus, it provides an almost unique example of the cooperation of private philanthropy, the medical and legal professions and the judiciary in the solution of a pressing inter-professional problem with wide public repercussions.

The large and constantly growing proportion of personal injury cases in the volume of civil litigation has emphasized more and more the necessity for the development of new techniques that will not only hasten the disposition of these cases, but also improve the quality of the ultimate result. It is clear that in litigation of this kind there are only two basic questions: (1) Liability, which rests primarily upon objective and relatively non-technical facts under well established rules of law; and (2) the extent of damage, usually involving highly technical conjectural and controversial medical testimony.

Both of these questions are decided finally, if no agreement about them can be reached before then, by juries instructed as to the law by judges or by the judges themselves in the relatively few cases where the parties are willing to forego their right to a jury. On the second question, the extent of damage, the jury must sift for itself the generally highly technical medical testimony and reach its conclusion on pretty much its own responsibility. And although relatively few of the many personal injury actions that are begun actually persist to the final verdict of a jury, since most are settled somewhere short of that, yet the amounts awarded by juries in the cases they decide are the yardstick that really determines the amounts for which litigants and their attorneys will settle in the many actions disposed of short of trial. Thus, to a large extent personal injury litigation had come to rest upon the inexpert judgment of a lay jury as to the highly technical and conflicting opinions of the particular medical witnesses called to support the opposing claims of the contending litigants who hired them. 

The sponsors of the medical expert testimony project considered that this imposed an intolerable burden upon the jury as a fact

finding institution and that even the judges in jury waived cases were not technically qualified to deal with it; that it was a basic cause of court congestion because the conjectural element involved is conducive to postponement of settlement, that the temptation which it provided for both doctors and lawyers to exaggerate, and otherwise resort to unethical conduct was a serious hazard for both professions; and that even if some public cost should be involved, there was a pressing need to correct the procedure and to improvise a prophylatic and impartial aid for the formulation and presentation in court of medical testimony. 

The impartial medical expert project, devised and proposed as a solution to this problem in New York City was established by rule of Court and without implementing legislation. The Special 

Rule adopted by the Supreme Court, Appellate Division, First Department, effective December 1, 1952, was at first limited in its application to New York County, but on October 1, 1953, it was extended by Court order to the Supreme Court in Bronx County. 

The Rule in its present form reads as follows: 

"1. There is established in the Supreme Court for the County of New York an office to be known as the Medical Report Office. “2. In any personal injury case in which, prior to the trial thereof, a justice shall be of the opinion that an examination of the injured person and a report thereon by an impartial medical expert would be of material aid to the determination of the case, he may, after consultation with counsel for the respective parties, order such examination and report, without cost to the parties, through the Medical Report Office of the Supreme Court, New York County. The examination will be made by a member of a panel of examining physicians designated for their particular qualifications by the New York Academy of Medicine and New York County Medical Society. Copies of the report of the examining physician will be made available by the clerk of the Medi, cal Report Office to all parties. "3. If the case proceeds to trial after such examination and report, either party may call the examining physician as a witness, or the trial justice may, if he deems it desirable to do so, call the examining physician as a witness for the court, subject to questioning by any party, but without cost to any party.' 

The successful inauguration and operation of the rule depends primarily upon three factors. (1) The selection of the doctors to constitute the panel; (2) the selection of cases for reference to the panel; and (3) the provision of funds to finance the procedure. 

The judges, lawyers and doctors who devised the project were well aware of the difficulties inherent in the problem of choosing impartial and reputable men to perform the medical examinations required by the rule. Wisely, they entrusted this to the medical profession itself. A special joint committee of the New York Academy of Medicine and the New York County Medical Society was

formed to choose the members of the panel. The announced requirement for membership was that the doctors chosen must be acknowlcdged leading authorities in the various branches of medicine involved and not prominently identified in their previous practice with service as experts for either plaintiffs or defendants in personal injury litigation. The specialties needed and the number required for each were indicated to the selection committee by the Justices of the Supreme Court. The committee then compiled the lists of names and persuaded those chosen to accept the assignment. Despite the announced specification that only experts of the highest qualifications—men who would ordinarily avoid the role of partisans and refuse to appear for either side in ordinary litigation—would be selected, it was not difficult to recruit the panel. Those selected were almost always willing, as a public and professional duty, to place their services at the court's disposal. 

Since the project was first established only for the court in New York County, the first list consisted entirely of doctors practising in Manhattan. Many of the doctors on the panel came from the staffs of the great hospitals and the faculties of the medical schools . in the City of New York. Later, when the procedure was extended to Bronx County, on October 1, 1953, a special panel of doctors from that County was selected by the Bronx Medical Society. 

The following lists show the specialties represented in each County, the number of doctors in each specialty and the number of cascs referred to each category, as of December 30, 1957.

This problem does not end with the initial formulation of the panels. Changes were made in panel personnel of both counties after a period of trial, and even now the panels are subjected to constant supervision and reviewing by the medical societies. New specialties are added as need for them develops, others are dropped for lack of use, and the membership changes sometimes rather radically, as doctors and their ability to serve change. A special effort is made to prevent service on the panel from becoming a means of entree for the less responsible members of the medical profession into what some of them consider to be the easy and lucrative business of partisan medical jurisprudence, and from this point of view, the vigilance of the medical societies has been especially important and valuable.

The second major aspect of the plan involves the selection of cases for reference. This is the province of the Justices of the 

Supreme Court, and their attitude plays a leading role in the success of the project. It has been the view of the Justices that the selection should be made at a sufficiently early time in the history of the litigation to permit accurate appraisal of the medical facts and at the same timë facilitate settlement before 'trial. When the project was established there was already in existence in the court a device that lends itself readily to this purpose. All cases of personal injury are processed through an informal pre-trial examination before a Justice regularly assigned to this work, as soon as possible after they are placed upon the court calendar. The primary purpose of this is to determine whether or not the case must go to trial, and if possible, to promote amicable settlement. Since the time of pretrial is usually some months following the accident, often the attorneys have indicated by filing a certificate of readiness that

all their pre-trial motions are finished, and the dispute has by then reached a condition of relative maturity, so that the true extent of physical injury and its probable permanence can be accurately determined. Yet the event is not so long passed, nor the positions of the parties so firmly fixed that impartial examination will not have a settling effect as to the extent of damage. 

So the Justices decided that one of the items that should be considered when a casc reaches them on the regular pre-trial calendar should be the question of reference of the case to the medical panel for independent cxamination. It was further established that the policy of the Justices would be to assign cases to the panel only on a selective basis. Not all cases require or would benefit by the process. Indeed, the principal usefulness of the device is generally in cases where there is a great disparity between the views of the doctors for the adversary parties and almost irreconcilable opinions as to the nature or extent of the plaintiff's injury. 

Such cases had been extremely difficult to settle without trial. So a reference to an impartial specialist would be likely to bring substantial results in the way of saving the court's time, if in even a few of them a trial cuuld be avoided. Thus, the pre-trial conference calendar is the main source of reference to the medical reports office. The judges conducting pre-trial, with the lawyers for both sides before them, the issues clearly defined by the pleadings and the medical reports of the doctor's for both parties in their hands, will decide whether the case is one of such sharp divergence of medical views as to justify the reference. If so, they will refer the case to the panel for examination. Consent of the lawyers is not required, but only rarely is there protest by them and some lawyers even request that the case be so referred. 

The pre-trial Justice then enters an order in memorandum form, referring the case to the Medical Report Office. In his order, he specifics the nature of the dispute, and the type of medical specialty involved and he fixes a date for a resumption of the pre-trial conference, at which time the case will be re-examined in the light of the impartial medical expert's report. He also notes the amounts of money offered by the defendant and asked by the plaintiff for settlement. The attorneys are then instructed to deliver the memorandum to the Medical Report Office in the court building and there to complete arrangements for the conduct of the examination. 

The Medical Report Office is now a part of the trial term calendar office. The Clerk in charge kecps records of the cases referred, has custody of the lists of names, addresses and telephone numbers of the doctors assigned to the panc), arranges for examinations, etc. 

Since the order of reference does not name the doctor by whom the examination is to be made, but only specifies the special field of medicine involved, the most important of the duties of the Medical Report Office is to select from the panel the name of an available doctor and arrange with him for the examination. The doctors are chosen as nearly as possible in regular rotation, and names coming up in this way are passed only if the doctor involved will find it

TA

greatly inconvenient or impossible to act. The names on the panel and the order of their service is kept in strict confidence by the clerk in charge in order to avoid pressures for assignment to a particular doctor.

When the attorney appears in the clerk's office with his order of reference, he and his opponent are consulted as to possible convenient dates for the examination. Then the clerk telephones the office of the doctor to whom the case should be assigned, arranges with him for the examination at a particular time and place, and notifies the lawyers accordingly. The clerk also reminds the lawyers of the necessity of furnishing to the doctor in advance, copies of examinations and medical records previously made in the case, and he assists in securing by subpoena duces tecum, the pertinent hospital records for the use of the court's expert. He sends a confirming letter to the doctor and gives his office's instructions as to where the report of examination is to be sent. Hospital record subpocnas arranged in the medical reports office are made returnable to the doctors office, and all reports and records of previous examinations are furnished to the doctor sufficiently in advance of the examination date to permit him to familiarize himself with the case before the examination appointment.

The doctor's examination follows the usual pattern of physical examination by privately retained physicians. The plaintiff's attorney is encouraged to go with him to the doctor's office. The examination elicits the medical history and symptoms. The examination proceeds with the use of such laboratory and other tests as the doctor considers necessary. The prescription of treatment, of course, is not involved, but the doctor's findings are embodied in a report to the Court and both parties, and may be the basis for the doctor's oral testimony at the trial, if there is one. 

The doctor's report is made in triplicate and he sends it to the Court's Medical Report Office. The bill for his professional services which the doctor submits to the office with his report is predicated on the notification previously given him that his fee is expected to be reasonable, and comparable to one he would charge to private patients of moderate financial circumstances. The medical records and reports used in the examination are either returned directly, or forwarded to the court office for return to the proper place. 

When the three copies of the report of the panelist reach the court clerk he retains one copy for the use of the judge at the resumed pre-trial. This, together with the doctor's bill goes to the courtroom on the day when the case appears on the pre-trial calendar. The Court clerk sends the other copies to the lawyers for the opposing parties.

The matter then awaits the time of the resumed pre-trial, which may be the date originally fixed by the original pre-trial Justice, or possibly, on an adjourned date made necessary for completion and consideration of the medical report. At the resumed pre-trial, the case is again examined in the light of the impartial report. The

expert does not attend the hearing, and his written report is the only evidence of his work. Settlement possibilities are again explored and if the case can not be amicably terminated, it is otherwise disposed of. Sometimes the report shows that the amount of damages could not exceed $6,000 (the top jurisdictional limit of the City Court) and if that is so, the case might be transferred by consent to the City Court. Also, the parties may be convinced that the controversy can be more quickly disposed of by waiving jury trial or, frequently, the entire matter may be remanded to the trial calendar, in a preferred status, for disposition in the regular manner. 

If settlement was not accomplished, but agreement is close, the Judge will note that fact on his memorandum and so the calendar and trial Justices will be able again to lend their good offices for settlement when the case reaches them. The pre-trial Justice also fixes the amount to be paid the medical panelist who performed the examination and instructs the clerk to arrange for its payment in the usual way from funds appropriated by the City of New York for that purpose.

One very important feature of the plan is the provision of the rule which permits, but does not require, the trial Justice or either party to call upon the medical expert to testify at the trial. This serves three primary ends. (1) It provides for the Judge and jury, an impartial, highly expert and informed factual basis for the formulation of final judgment on the extent of damages, thus aiding them to some extent in the difficult, technical job of reconciling conflicting expert opinions given by the hired doctors for the opposing parties; and (2) the very existence of the right, even though it is not exercised, may serve to restrain certain partisan experts from expressing their reports, or at the trial, extreme, misleading, or even false findings, which they know or think can be publicly exposed on the witness stand by a highly respected member of the medical profession; and (3) the procedure is an intangible force for pre-trial settlement of the case. The plaintiff with an inflated claim or the defendant who denies all or any substantial damage when it clearly exists to some degree, will be more truthful and reasonable at pre-trial if he has in his hands a neutral report on the injuries by a highly respected practitioner chosen by the court on recommendation of responsible leaders of the medical profession, who, he knows, will testify on the trial. 

If this were not the understanding at pre-trial, the value of the impartial expert would be greatly reduced.

If the presence of the expert at the trial is desired under this aspect of the rule, arrangement for his appearance is made by the clerk in the Medical Reports Office. No subpoena is used. Indeed, any direct communication between the attorneys for the litigants and the medical expert is discouraged. The office makes especial efforts to avoid waste of the expert's time and to meet his convenience in arranging for his appearance. Telephone communication between the doctor and the clerk's office as the time for testimony approaches is regularly employed to this end.

1 When the neutral expert is called to the stand he is treated the same as other witnesses. His status as an impartial court appo nted expert is made imown to the jury. He may be called by either party and examined and cross-examined like any other expert witness under the ordinary rules of evidence, except that generally his findings may be questioned even by the party calling him. But, as might be expected, the records show that there are few challenges to the weight of his testimony on grounds of incompetence or bias. 

If the expert was called by one of the parties, the judge may ask him additional questions. Sometimes his written report is received in evidence.

In only 76 of the 661 cases referred to the panel since its inception, have the panel members who performed the examinations been called as witnesses at the trial. 51 of these were New York County references and 25 were in the Bronx. In 35 of the cases where the panelist testified, he was called by the plaintiff; in 37 he was called by the defendant, in 3 cases he was called by both parties; and in one he was called by the court. The fees for trial testimony have ranged from $25 in one case to $400 in another. In all, 80 fees were charged (more than the number of cases, since sometimes two or more panel specialists testified in one case). The fees in only 4 were less than $100. Thirty-nine charged from $100 to $150, 20 ranged between $150 and $200 and 16 were $200 or over. In only one case is it recorded that the court reduced the fee for testimony l'equested by the panelist.

The pre-trial conference is not the only source from which cases are referred to the medical panel. Frequently the calendar judges, when they discuss settlement of cases at the regular calendar call, or when they consider a motion for a hardship calendar preference, or a motion to reconsider preferences previously granted or denied because the case is within or not within the jurisdiction of the lower courts, or a motion to remove the case from the City Court to the Supreme Court, or vice versa, or on a motion to restore the case to the calendar, may refer cases to the Medical Report Office. And often the trial Judges, after cases have been assigned to them cither just before or during jury trial or, in non-jury cases, even after trial for an evaluation of damages, may refer the case to the panel. 

Occasionally, special references are made, as, in one case, when the physical condition of a juror was in question, and in others when the need or desirability of a commitment to the City hospital at Bellevue arises on a motion.

A primary reason for the successful operation of the system is the method of financing it. For two years the plan was operated on an experimental basis through grants from the Ford and Sloan Foundations. The funds thus provided were used to pay the fees of the members of the medical panel, when approved by the court. 

Thus, the completely independent operation of the panel was assured, and the parties were not saddled as they are in some similar procedures with the cost of its use. Ifter the two year period ended, the sponsors of the project concluded that this feature of the

plan had been a substantial reason for its success. Accordingly, the Justices of the Supreme Court persuaded the City authorities to include in the Court budget an annual appropriation of $15,000, to permit the project to continue on a permanent basis, with its cost as a part of the regular court budget. Doctors' fees are now paid from the appropriated fund, without direct cost to the parties. So, the objection to an increase in the costs of litigation is largely eliminated. The Justices and the appropriating authorities in the New York City Government believe that the small additional public cost of the procedure is well repaid by the substantial savings in the cost of disposition of cases that without the services of the Office would involve all of the expense of prolonged and hotly contested jury trials. In the fiscal year ending June 30, 1957 and each year since then, the court has used the entire $15,000 annual appropriation through the assignment of cases to the panel. 

Fces for individual examinations (without trial testimony) have ranged between less than $20 for simple X-rays on one extreme to as much as $200 for a few of the more elaborate and difficult assignments, depending, of course, on the specialty involved and the complexity of the case. Most of the fees were between $50 and $150. 

As of June 30, 1958, the Justices of New York and Bronx County had referred to the Medical Reports Office from both Counties, a total of 661 cases, 437 of them from New York County and 224 from Bronx County. Three hundred forty-five of these (or more than 

50%) (284 from New York and 61 from the Bronx) came to the panel from the pre-trial Justices, in an effort to reconcile disparate medical claims. Two hundred eleven (179 in New York and 32 in 

Bronx Counties) or 61% of the total number referred at pre-trial were settled as a result of the reference. Thirty-six (29 in New York and 7 in Bronx) were settled during or after trial. Twenty-five (23 in 

New York and 2 in Bronx), were tried to verdict. Forty-four (29 in New York and 15 in the Bronx), were remanded to the trial calendars, most of them with preference but a few unpreferred. 

Twelve (11 in New York and 1 in the Bronx), were transferred to the City Court because the parties agreed that less than $6,000 was involved. Ten (8 in New York and 2 in the Bronx) were dismissed, discontinued or otherwise withdrawn and seven (5 in New York and 2 in the Bronx) were still awaiting resumption of the pre-trial conference at the time when these figures were compiled. 

The calendar Judges in both jury and non-jury calendar parts referred to the Medical Office a total of 133 cases, or 20% of the total referred, (31 from New York and 102 from the Bronx). Of these, 79 (60%) were settled before trial (20 from New York and 59 from the Bronx); 21 were settled during or after trial (5 from 

New York and 16 from the Bronx); 17 were tried to verdict or judgment (6 from New York and 11 from the Bronx). Only 3 were remanded to the trial calendar without result. One was dismissed and in twelve (all from the Bronx) the examinations had not been completed as of June 30, 1958.

As might be expected, the extent of use of the medical panel by the various pre-trial judges varies. Experience shows that generally 

49% of all cases that go to pre-trial are settled there without further effort. Thus, only 51% of the pre-trial calendar could be benefitted by reference to the medical panel. 

In the two years from December 1955 to November 1957, approxiinately 3,462 cases were pre-tried, by twelve different justices. Only 

94 of these, or 3% were considered suitable for reference to the medical office. But one judge sent to the office 5.6% of the 502 cases he pre-tried, whereas three judges sent none of the 601 cases they pretried to the office, and the others ranged from 4.4% to .5% of their total pre-trials. In addition, during this period 43 cases were referred to the panel by calendar judges and justices sitting in the trial parts. Thus, a total of 81,2% of the cases surviving pre-trial were referred to the panel.

133 66 37 80 76 38 Its work in the first year was greater than at any other time. It was least used in 1955, when the office was in transition from its experimental privately financed status to a permanent court office maintained at public expense. As the table also shows, the use of the office by the pre-trial judges has somewhat declined, while its relative importance to the trial parts and for miscellaneous purposes, chiefly in aid of the consideration of preferences, has grown, The amount of the appropriated funds is, of course, a factor limiting the use of the panel, and clearly the individual views of the justices as to the efficiency of the procedure and its usefulness in particular cases, are determining factors. Presiding Justice Bernard

Botein, of the Appellate Division, First Department, who was influcntial in the establishment of the original project, and who strongly supports it, has indicated an interest in enlarging its use. In a published article regarding the project, he says: “Despite careful screening by judges, cases will sift through and go to trial that fairly shriek for the enlightening report of an impartial medical expert. Occasionally we hear argument on such a case in the Appellate Division and the judges ask counsel why someone did not request the designation of a panel doctor."

He concludes by urging attorneys to make full use of the procedure.

Although 74% of the references to the panel came from either the pre-trial or calendar judges, there is a substantial use of its facilities by the justices of the trial parts, who are responsible for cases after they have been assigned for trial. References from this source may be made either immediately before or during trial, or, in non-jury cases, after trial for aid in the judge's determination of damages. The justices find that best results are achieved by allowing about two weeks for completion of each examination. But in emergencies, the members of the panel have cooperated to meet shorter time limits. 

Altogether, between the time of the establishment of the office until June 30, 1958, there had been referred to the office by the justices sitting in the trial parts 76 cases after they had been assigned for trial by the calendar judges. 54 of these were New York 

County cases and 22 of them were from Bronx County. This amounts to almost 12% of all cases referred to the office. Of these, 44 or 59% (29 in New York County and 15 in the Bronx) were settled before verdict or judgment. 20 (14 from New York County and 6 from the 

Bronx) were non-jury cases referred to the panel after judgment, in order to provide the judge with a guide for determination of damages. Of all those referred by the trial justices, 33 (23 from 

New York County and 10 from the Bronx) were referred before the trial began and 20 (15 from New York County and 5 from the 

Bronx) were referred during trial.

As previously indicated, the project has been responsible for many intangible and statistically immeasurable benefits even in cases where it is not used. It has made for a more wholesome and ethical atmosphere for the presentation of medical claims. It has improved the quality of medical testimony. It has constricted the area of dispute over medical facts and so promoted settlement. And in the cases where it has been used, the statistics prove that it has promoted the disposition of cases without trial. 554 of the 661 cases referred to the panel since 1952 came from pre-trial, calendar, or trial justices. The remainder were referred for special reasonssome on preference applications, some on motions to transfer to the City Court, or from the City Court to the Supreme Court, a few on special mental health issues, etc. But the 554 cases from the pretrial, calendar, and trial parts comprise the general run, and the effect of the panel on their dispositions is the immediate test of the usefulness of the procedure. Over 50% of them (290) were settled before trial assignment after the reference. 101 of them were settled after trial assignment. Thus, 391 cases, or 72% of the total were settled short of final judgment or verdict. Only 62, or 11% survived through verdict or judgment. The remainder were either remanded, transferred, withdrawn, dismissed, or are still awaiting completion of examination. It is true that the percentage of the cases referred * Botein, "Impartial Medical Testimony', N.Y.L.J., June 31, 1956, p. 4, col. 1.

to the medical office and ultimately tried to verdict is higher than is the percentage of all cases filed that go to verdict (usually between 7 and 10%), but the comparison does not detract from the validity of the settlement figures in cases that are referred, when it is remembered that these are the "hard core”, the most resistant to settlement, and the most persistent of all the cases on the calendar.

[Reprinted from Temple Law Quarterly, Philadelphia, Pa., U. S. A., Volume 34, No. 4, Summer 1961) THE IMPARTIAL MEDICAL EXPERT SYSTEM: 

THE JUDICIAL POINT OF VIEW HON. FRANCIS L. VAN DUSEN | 

The so-called impartial medical testimony plan involves the appointment by judges of a nisi prius court, in appropriate cases where the record discloses a substantial divergence of medical opinion, of a doctor from a panel of experienced physicians' recommended by the appropriate Medical Society (county or state) to examine the injured person (or the plaintiff's decedent in death actions) and to report to the court on his condition and its relation to the cause alleged by plaintiff. If the case goes to trial, and most of such cases are settled before trial on the basis of the report of the panel doctor, normally the doctor testifies as to his examination and opinion and is subject to cross-examination by the parties.

The single purpose. of expert testimony has, since the beginning of its use, been to provide guidance to the trier of fact on matters beyond the ken of ordinary laymen, so that the trier can intelligently decide questions which may depend on specialized knowledge or experience. However, the strains produced by the partisan selection and use of such testimony have seriously impaired its utility as an aid to the jury.

The impetus for this plan comes not only from the experience of the trial judges but also from long-standing professional dissatisfaction, both medical and legal, with sole reliance on partisan expert testimony. Evidence of this dissatisfaction is reported in England 

+ LL.B. Harvard Law School; U.S. District Judge, Eastern District of Pennsylvania; Vice-Chairman, Expert Testimony Committee, Judicial Administration, Section of A.B.A.; Chairman, Committee on Federal Procedural Rules of the Judicial 

Conference of the Third Circuit. 

1. The history of the use of experts as an aid to both court and jury is related in Rosenthal, The Development of the Use of_Expert Testimony, 2 Law & CONTEMP. 

PROB. 403 (1935). See, also, 7 WIGMORE, EVIDENCE, § 1917. Rosenthal traces the use of experts back to the 14th Century. Then, in cases requiring special knowledge, experts served as advisors to the court, whereupon the court would instruct the jury on the matters involved. Or the court would impanel a special jury of experts to decide the questions presented. With the development in the 16th and 17th centuries of proof of facts by witnesses and the concurrent development of juries in which the office of juror was distinct from that of witness, expert advice took the form of testimony by expert witnesses addressed to the jury. Rosenthal, supra, pp. 406-411. 

For a modern instance of the use of an expert as an advisor to the court and also of the court's use of its power to call witnesses on its own motion, see the opinion of Judge Wyzanski in United States v. United Shoe Machinery Corp., 110 F. Supp. 

295, 305-6 (D. Mass. 1953). (Civil antitrust case.)

as long ago as 1876. There, an English judge, in commenting on the natural inclination of a party to find, not the best scientist, but the "best witness”, stated : * * The mode in which expert evidence is obtained is such as not to give the fair result of scientific opinion to the court. A man niay go, and does some times, to half-a-dozen experts. ***

He takes their honest opinions, he finds three in his favor, and three against him; he says to the three in his favor, "will you be kind enough to give evidence?" and he pays the three against him their fees and leaves them alone; the other side does the

And the United States Supreme Court observed, in 1858: * * * Experience has shown that opposite opinions of persons professing to be experts may be obtained to any amount; and it often occurs that not only many days, but even weeks are consumed in cross-examinations, to test the skill or knowledge of such witnesses and the correctness of their opinions, wasting the time and wearying the patience of both court and jury, and perplexing, instead of elucidating, the questions involved in the issue. Winans v. New York and Erie R. Co., 62 U.S. 88, 101 (1858).

This century, with its rapid advances in the sciences, has witnessed a proliferation of the use of expert testimony, particularly medical testimony in actions seeking recovery for physical injuries. The increased use of such partisan testimony has intensified its study and criticism,

The nub of the criticism is that the credibility of such testimony, and, hence, its value as an aid to the fact finder,—the only basis for permitting its use,-has been materially diminished by the consequences of partisan selection and use.
